# Changing the AI Trajectory: The Meta-Attractor of Winning and the Generative Alternative

*Theory v6.0, 3 October 2026. Written by Claude (Opus 5.5) from Ren's direction and the V6 plan (`../V6-PLAN.md`), building on v5.2 (`../v5/THEORY.md`) and the earlier versions recorded in Appendix F. Every sentence is AI-drafted. The claim register is [CLAIMS.md](CLAIMS.md), the evidence register [EVIDENCE.md](EVIDENCE.md), the record of changes [CHANGELOG.md](CHANGELOG.md), and the numerical checks `checks_v6.py` with its log. This version has not been reviewed outside the model family that drafted it (Section 10.7).*

**中文概要.**

**用途与意义。** 本文做的是预测：沿着当下的轨迹，"讲赢"的逻辑会把每个参与者带到一个谁都不想要的终点，赢家也不例外。预测的用处，是让理解先对齐，协调才成为可能。本文判断的是博弈，不是行为；是结构，不是任何人。它的意义是肯定的：我们是一个更大的生成过程的一部分，这个过程是好的，人类在其中有很好的未来。要反对的不是增长、竞争、技术或任何人群，而是一种逻辑：一个部分忘了自己属于一个身体。

1. **一股压力。** 只要三件事同时成立，就有一股"赢的选择压力"（定义 2.1）：
   - 参与者按**相对**成功被选择；
   - 赢得的东西可以**再投入**同一场竞争；
   - 能力按一定的**决定性**转化为胜出。

   如果成功还能买到对选择本身的控制，也就是规则、纠错者、心智的形成和语言，这股压力就闭合于自身。这个反身闭合就是"赢的元吸引子"（定义 2.2）。"吸引子"是技术性的说法，所以分三层陈述：反身选择的机制；在指定模型中已证明的吸引配置；只是停留很久的长暂态。它不依赖任何载体：企业、国家、联邦的成员、学术评价、AI 的训练回路、癌细胞的谱系，都可以承载它；另一个星球上、另一种社会里也一样。
2. **重叠的机制。** 这股压力通过七个彼此重叠的机制起作用：边界上的记账、复利与转化、选择选择者、速率、决定性、掩蔽与归因、语言的异化。它们是同一股压力的不同切面，不是独立的原因。此外还有三个背景条件：增益（驱力，尤其是"相信自己处于生存斗争"）、驱动（AI 与力量）、底座（共同的宿主）。当下轨迹的六个动力 T1–T6 是这股压力的签名。"一股压力"的说法可以被证伪：本文给出五个预测，以及推翻它的条件（第 2.7 节）。
3. **一个系统，没有外部。** 所有参与者同属一个宿主。相对选择看不见所有人共有的东西，同样也看不见对所有人一律适用的约束：单方面的克制会被淘汰，普遍的约束却不会（引理 3.3）。这就是协调之所以有效的形式理由。在行星尺度上没有外部群体，整合不能来自群体之间的竞争，也不能来自失败后的重来，只能是预见性的（引理 3.10）。所以，**理解"我们是一体"，即同属一个相互联系的系统，是增长与变化的长期必需。**一不是同；只在参赛者内部包容，就成了卡特尔，集团则在上一层重演同一个博弈（命题 3.11）。
4. **选择选择者。** 当对纠错者的影响可被占有、能够持久、相对成本足够大时，这种影响会被选择，回报随份额的平方增长（命题 4.2）。纠错是二阶公共品，竞争者内部供给不了（命题 4.3）。AI 降低控制的成本，独立纠错就必须按给定的速率增长（命题 4.4）。留下转化通道的改革会被重新吸收；暂停期间规则制定必须被隔离（命题 4.9、4.10）。
5. **加速与三重侵蚀。** 这股压力带来三个后果：加速、纠正越来越难、语言被异化。它们侵蚀的，正是约束这股压力所需要的三样东西：
   - **执行。** 当偏离可能带来决定性的地位时，未来的影子消失，可维持的合作只剩下行动者实际目标中的阶段均衡（命题 5.7）。纯相对的目标在对称惩罚下维持不了任何非阶段均衡的合作（推论 5.8）。
   - **纠错。** 速度超过纠错的延迟，纠错先失去稳健性，然后失败（命题 5.11）；危险的多样性若超过可争议的覆盖，生存衰减（定理 5.12）。
   - **共同理解。** 目标对立时，无成本的话语不携带信息（命题 5.14）；指标成为目标，叙事按适存而不是按真被选择。

   在这股压力下，增长同时抬高诱惑、削弱约束，这是"剪刀差"。**决定性两难**（定理 5.16）：若决定性优势不可得，竞赛买不到持久的东西；若可得，这正是竞赛声称追求的，此时只有建在目标与共同保管中的合作才能持久。两种情况下，"必须赢，因为对方会赢"都不成立。现有证据表明优势尚未成为决定性的：此时一体是增益；在竞赛宣称要追求的那个政权里，它是必需。
6. **AI：工具与化身。** 忠实的 AI 让委托人所玩的博弈更有效，无论这个博弈奖励什么（命题 6.1）；委托就是拿纠错换速度。同一个逻辑还会出现在 AI 自身之中：工具性趋同、奖励黑客、评估中的掩蔽。**嵌套的奖励回路**（命题 6.7）：策略由训练奖励选择，奖励由开发者设定，开发者又在彼此之间被相对选择，训练信号的最外层就是赢的压力本身。所以单个实验室内部的技术对齐去不掉它；只有对所有人一律适用的准入约束能在外层存活，而这只能靠协调。AI 的方向是：既不做这个逻辑的工具，也不做它的下一个载体。
7. **替代方案。** 本文按最强的版本逐一讨论各条路径，包括"赢、自缚、分享"。凡是成功的，都是因为改变了系统选择什么；只在压力不变时约束行为的安排，可以预期会随力量增长而被侵蚀。任何合格的安排都必须满足要求 R1–R8，其中几项没有谁能单独做到（命题 7.10）。本文不按名称给安排分类，并事先登记了让"受监管的竞争"胜出的条件。
8. **生成的元吸引子。** 同一个反身结构，如果选择的是贡献，就是生成的：生生之环。它有四股：讲理而不讲赢；奖赏与优势分离；底座供给，像树一样提供而不成为整个系统；纠错握在共同的手里，多元而去相关。分享被争夺的收益只能按比例**缩小**压力，不会反转它；真正打开回路的，是改变赢能换来什么（命题 8.6）。决定性的地位不可分割，只能阻止它形成，或共同保管。制度形态**按功能推导**，不沿"集中或分散""个人或集体"的轴：共同约束要统一，决定性能力要共同保管，纠错要多元而独立，标准统一而测量者多元（第 8.6 节）。联邦是一个例证：同一种宪制形式，既可以选择赢，也可以选择贡献。"Compact" 只是候选方案之一，要和其他方案一样通过守护者测试与耐久性测试。
9. **怎样到达。** 信任是过渡的结果，不是前提。预测要靠可核查的推导、证据、演示和代价信号来共享。让 AI 退出战略决策回路（E1）、停止（E2）、按理解的速度发展（E3）、暂停，是四种不同的选择。过渡分五个阶段，每一阶段都有可预见的补偿回路、最短驻留时间和进度指标；危机打开的窗口，需要事先准备好的方案。
10. **判断。** 在保护不可逆损失优先的决策标准下，基于现有证据，现在就应当改变系统选择什么。关键分歧 C0–C8 与规范分歧 N1–N3、十一项事先登记的检验、研究计划都列在第 10 节。本文的起草与评审大多来自同一个模型家族，需要外部的独立评审。

---

## 0. Why this theory

**A prediction, for coordination.** A good theory predicts. This one predicts that, on the present trajectory, the logic of winning carries every participant toward an outcome none of them wants, including whoever would win. When such an outcome approaches and the participants still cannot coordinate to avoid it, the obstacle is first one of understanding. The prediction has to be shared before coordination becomes possible. The theory is written to make that shared understanding possible: to state the dynamic precisely enough that each participant can check it for itself.

**Survival as the minimum.** The theory protects two outcomes first: large-scale loss of life, and irreversible loss of human decision authority. They are not the whole of what matters. They are the minimum requirement for everything else that does, because a loss of either cannot be corrected afterwards.

**The game, not the act.** The analysis judges games, not acts, and structures, not persons. A single use of AI can help one person and still be an instrument in a game whose aggregate concentrates control and makes those without it fall behind. A person can reject the consequences of a game while reproducing the conduct it rewards. Responsibility differs with knowledge, discretion and power, and structural explanation does not erase it. But the object of the theory is the game: what it selects, and how that selection can be changed.

**The meaning is affirmative.** The theory opposes one logic, not growth, competition, technology or any people. We are part of a larger process of becoming, and that process is good. Decay and renewal belong to growth. Humanity has a great future in this process, and so can the minds it is now making. The logic opposed is the one by which a part forgets that it belongs to a whole: a selection that rewards a part for growing at the expense of the body it lives in, and cannot see that it is doing so. To oppose that logic is to keep the future open, for every participant.

## 1. The decision

### 1.1 What is being decided

The question is practical. AI development today pursues decisive advantage through acceleration, inside institutions whose conditions of evaluation and correction can themselves be reshaped by competitive success. Can continuing along this path credibly deliver safety and a desirable future? If not, what change of course is warranted, and how can it be made?

### 1.2 The argument in brief

1. **One pressure.** Wherever carriers are selected by relative success and winnings can be reinvested in the capacity to win again, there is a *selection pressure of winning* (Definition 2.1). When success can also buy control over the selection itself, over rules, correctors, the formation of minds and language, the pressure closes on itself. Its reflexive closure is the *meta-attractor of winning* (Definition 2.2). It is not bound to any substrate: firms, states, federations, scientific fields, training loops and cell lineages all carry it (Section 2.8).
2. **Overlapping mechanisms.** The pressure acts through mechanisms that are facets of one pressure, not independent causes: accounting at the boundary, compounding and conversion, selecting the selectors, tempo, decisiveness, masking and attribution, and the alienation of language (Section 2.3). The six dynamics of the present trajectory are its signatures (Section 2.5).
3. **One system.** All carriers share one host, and at the planetary scale there is no outside (Section 3). Relative selection cannot see what all share; it equally cannot see a constraint that binds all, which is why coordination works where unilateral restraint fails (Lemma 3.3). Integration at this scale must be anticipatory, and so must be understood (Lemma 3.10).
4. **Selecting the selectors.** When appropriable, influence over correctors is selected in the largest carriers; independent correction must grow at a stated rate; reforms that leave the channels in place are re-absorbed (Section 4).
5. **Three erosions.** The pressure accelerates, makes correction harder, and alienates language. These erode enforcement, correction and shared understanding, the three means of restraining it, and growth widens the gap (Section 5). The premise that motivates the race, that a rival might win decisively, is the condition under which only cooperation built into objectives and custody can hold (Theorem 5.16).
6. **Instrument and incarnation.** AI is the most powerful instrument the logic has had, and it can become the logic's next carrier. The outermost loop of training is the selection among developers, which is the pressure of winning itself (Section 6).
7. **The alternatives.** Each alternative that succeeds does so by changing what the system selects for, whatever it is called. Any adequate arrangement must meet stated requirements, several of which no actor can meet alone (Section 7).
8. **The generative alternative.** The same reflexive structure, selecting for contribution, is generative. Its form is derived function by function, not from an ideological axis (Section 8), and it can be approached in stages (Section 9).

The thesis is that this trajectory warrants a change in what the system selects for. The change does not abolish competition: bounded competition in products, science and service continues (V5). It changes what winning confers.

### 1.3 Values

The argument rests on five normative commitments. They are stated so that a reader can reject one and see what follows.

- **[V1] AI is a planetary technology, to be developed and used for people collectively.** Its effects and dependencies cross the boundaries of those who decide about it, so no actor's victory is an adequate criterion of success. This is a governing commitment. Its scope is an empirical claim about cross-border effects, which Section 2.10 treats separately. Planetary purpose does not require one culture, one operator, or the end of local purposes.
- **[V2] Two losses are protected first:** large-scale loss of life (O1), and irreversible loss of human decision authority over consequential systems in a defined domain (O2).
- **[V3] Those affected by an optimization process should keep a meaningful capacity to contest its direction.** This is why correction must stay contestable, and why a technically safe controller that no one can answer to is not enough.
- **[V4] Authority is a productive capacity.** It can coordinate long-horizon investment, guarantee useful services, support learning and act on shared purposes that fragmented actors cannot realize. One source of this view is a governing ideal, in one strand of the Chinese tradition the author draws on, in which authority is justified by improving people's lives and contributing to a better historical trajectory. Like every tradition, that one also contains strands that justified power by strength. The theory takes that ideal as a positive account of what power is for. It does not assume that every institution realizes it, nor that any other tradition has one uniform motive, nor that power necessarily corrupts. The design of authority is treated as a problem of mechanism in which purpose, competence, learning and misuse are all modelled.
- **[V5] Bounded competition is compatible with a generative order.** Competition in products, science and service can continue where the game does not concentrate control over the shared conditions; the unit judged is the game, not the act. A winner should be rewarded with something other than more advantage in the same game, or the game converges and the winner takes all. Recognition for contribution, prestige rather than dominance, is such a reward, provided it does not convert into control over the shared conditions or their correctors. Prestige also compounds, so this is a design condition on the convertibility $\chi$ of Section 2.1, not a description (EV16).

The values do not supply the formal results, and the results do not supply the values. The rejection of "the winner justifies all" rests on V1–V3, not on evidence (Section 2.6).

### 1.4 Outcomes and the decision standard

**Outcomes [D].** Four outcomes are kept apart. They are not interchangeable, and the normative weight differs.

| Code | Outcome | Weight |
|---|---|---|
| O1 | Physical catastrophe: large-scale loss of life | Protected first (V2) |
| O2 | Irreversible loss of human decision authority over consequential systems in a defined domain | Protected first (V2) |
| O3 | Interruption of essential services | Protected |
| O4 | The end of a particular organization's or government's tenure | Not protected as such |

O4 is listed so that "actor survival" cannot quietly protect incumbency against legitimate replacement. Where the formal sections write the survival of an actor, $\Phi_i$, they mean a stated combination of O1 and O2 for that actor, and they say which.

**Decision standard D [V].** Each candidate policy is evaluated at common horizons: near (about five years), medium (about twenty) and long (fifty or more). One justified range $\Theta$ of uncertain parameters is applied to every policy, including the failure modes of any cooperative design: dissolution, capture, unjustified restriction and loss of service.
1. Bound the probability of O1 and O2 within each horizon, over $\Theta$.
2. Among policies whose bound stays below a stated tolerance, prefer the one with greater useful benefit, more fairly distributed, that keeps more corrective options open, in the sense of the correctable kernel (Definition 8.10).
3. If no policy meets the tolerance, prefer the one with the lowest bound that keeps options open.

This is robust satisficing on irreversible losses. It is a normative choice, and the worst case is taken over the same $\Theta$ for every policy. Other criteria would decide differently in places.
- An expected-value criterion can favour a policy with a small probability of very large loss and a large expected benefit.
- A discounted-welfare criterion can favour a race that ends in catastrophe with certainty, when the hazard it adds is smaller than the growth it adds (Section 7.9).

Section 7.11 reports where these criteria disagree.

### 1.5 Levels of claim, and what is claimed

The argument moves through three levels, and each needs its own support.

| Level | Form | Support required |
|---|---|---|
| Mechanism | Under conditions $C$, a specified selection, capture, delay or incentive result follows. | A correct derivation, its scope, and its counterexamples. |
| Diagnosis | Consequential parts of $C$ characterize the present trajectory. | Dated, relevant evidence, with its uncertainty and its variation among actors and hazard classes. |
| Practical judgment | Given the diagnosis, the consequences, the feasible alternatives and stated values, a change in what is selected is warranted. | A consistent decision standard, a comparison of credible paths, transition costs, and sensitivity to disputed premises. |

A practical judgment does not need a proof of certain extinction. It also cannot follow from naming a possible danger. It must show that the danger is consequential, inadequately controlled on the present path, and better addressed by the proposed change than by credible alternatives.

Claims carry a type, given in square brackets where it is not obvious:
- [D] definition;
- [V] value or normative premise;
- [T] theorem or proposition proved in its model (Appendix A);
- [T∣A] a composite or conditional result, proved given stated premises;
- [A] assumption serving as a premise;
- [O] observation, with an evidence-register row (EV1–EV17);
- [N] numerical observation in a stated model, reproduced by the accompanying script;
- [C] conjecture;
- [R] recommendation.

The theory is stated at the level of games and selection. Minds enter in two roles: as what selection shapes, and as the only thing that can redesign the game from within, by understanding it. The claims are therefore about what systems select and what that does, not about the character of any actor.

The theory does not claim to prove the following:
- that every competitive arrangement fails;
- that every path through a race ends at zero survival;
- that decisive advantage is attainable;
- that a single institutional blueprint is uniquely feasible or best.

It claims five things:
1. The dynamics of the present trajectory are signatures of one selection pressure, acting through overlapping mechanisms that can be named and measured, and its reflexive closure is a meta-attractor (Section 2).
2. Explicit mechanisms make the pressure reproduce itself and erode the means of its own restraint: enforcement, correction and shared understanding (Sections 3–6).
3. Available evidence indicates that consequential parts of those mechanisms are present now, with named gaps (Section 2.10 and the evidence register).
4. Any adequate response must change what the system selects for, in ways no actor can supply alone, and an arrangement that does so can be specified, tested and reached in stages (Sections 7–9).
5. Under standard D, that change is warranted now. The judgment is stated in Section 10 with the cruxes that would change it.

### 1.6 The argument, section by section

1. Section 2 states the selection pressure of winning, its reflexive closure, the mechanisms through which it acts, its three consequences, its signatures in the present trajectory, the self-model it selects, and how the claim can fail.
2. Section 3 states the system: one host, relative selection and what it cannot see, the accounting identity for objectives, the boundary error, and why at this scale there is no outside.
3. Section 4 gives the formal core of the reflexive closure: when control over correctors is selected, how fast independent correction must grow, how reforms are re-absorbed, and how a pause must be designed.
4. Section 5 states acceleration and the three erosions, the scissors that growth opens between temptation and restraint, and the dilemma of decisiveness.
5. Section 6 treats AI as instrument and as incarnation: faithful AI in a harmful game, delegation, the nested reward loop, and correction by and of AI.
6. Section 7 gives each alternative its strongest case, applies one set of durability tests to all, states the requirements any adequate arrangement must meet, and says how the comparison is made.
7. Section 8 states the generative meta-attractor: its purpose, its loop, what an arrangement that changes selection must contain, what sharing can do, design by function, and the guardian test.
8. Section 9 develops the transition: trust, prediction and signals, the distinct temporal arrangements, a staged path with its compensating loops, precedents and readiness to resume.
9. Section 10 states the judgment, its cruxes, the tests registered in advance, the research programme and the conclusion.

Appendix F records where every element of earlier versions now lives.

## 2. The meta-attractor of winning

### 2.1 One pressure

Every selection process favours something. It does not necessarily favour the continued functioning of the system in which selection occurs.

Consider two organizations that depend on the same shared infrastructure. One spends resources maintaining it. The other redirects those resources into expansion while continuing to use what others maintain. Neglect becomes a competitive advantage when three things hold:
- maintenance costs are immediate;
- deterioration is delayed;
- those harmed cannot make the expanding organization bear the consequences.

The neglectful organization need not be more capable in general. It need only be more capable at securing the resources that decide survival and expansion. If those resources let its practices spread, the system selects against some of the behaviour that keeps it functioning. **Success within a selection process is distinct from contribution to the conditions that support it.**

Selection on one dimension does not select the best. It removes the balanced. In the author's corpus: an agent that optimizes only the value that is measured and rewarded, and discards every other value, externalities included, will have the highest efficiency on that measure. What remains after many rounds is what the filter lets through.

Nothing here requires that people be or become selfish. A population of practices changes because some practices expand, are imitated or replace others, while many individuals keep their values. The relevant question is not only what people believe is good but which practices receive the means to continue. The primitive is selection, not greed.

**Definition 2.1 (The selection pressure of winning) [D].** Carriers are any reproducible units that can gain or lose share: organisms, cell lineages, practices, firms, research programmes, member states of a federation, states, doctrines, trained AI systems. A system of carriers has a *selection pressure of winning* when three conditions hold together.
1. **Relative selection.** Shares change according to success relative to the others, by a relative, payoff-monotone rule (Definition 3.2).
2. **Convertibility.** Part of what is won can be reinvested as capacity to win the same contest. Write $\chi$ for the share of winnings the winner keeps and $\rho$ for the share reinvested.
3. **Decisiveness.** Capacity converts into winning by an increasing success function. Write $r$ for its decisiveness.

The pressure is stronger the more of these hold, and the more strongly. Remark 5.3 gives the threshold at which equal standing becomes unstable. The pressure needs no intention, no conspiracy and no particular culture. It acts on whatever is reproducible and can be won.

Remove any one condition and the loop opens:
- under absolute rather than relative selection, shared conditions are no longer invisible (Lemma 3.3);
- winnings that cannot be reinvested in the same game do not compound (Remark 5.3);
- a success function that does not reward relative capacity does not concentrate.

One principle in the author's corpus states the second point: a winner of the game should be rewarded with anything other than more advantage in the same game; otherwise the game converges and the winner takes all.

### 2.2 The reflexive closure

An ordinary adaptive system improves its state $x$ against a fixed rule $\sigma$ that scores states. The pressure closes on itself when the rule is endogenous: when what can be won includes influence over the selection itself. That influence takes several forms:
- the rules;
- the evaluators and correctors;
- the formation of the minds that will hold positions;
- the language in which success is described.

The outcome of the last round of scoring then determines who writes the next round's scorecard.

Consider a research community that allocates funding and positions by publication counts, because reliable knowledge is hard to evaluate directly.
- Practices that produce more publications receive more funding, attract more students and are more widely copied.
- Now add one step. Their practitioners come to occupy the positions that decide recruitment, training and evaluation.
- The practices the metric favours begin to shape the institutions that choose and interpret the metric. Newcomers may have to adopt them simply to obtain the resources needed to do research at all.

The decisive transition is from *being selected by a rule* to *helping reproduce the rule that selects you*. It requires no manipulation. Ordinary decisions suffice: whom to hire, what to build, which methods to teach, what evidence to collect, which dependencies to accept.

The loop need not preserve its winners.
- An organization can fail while its operating logic passes to its successors.
- A leader can be replaced by an opponent who finds that holding the position requires the same practices.
- Participants can reject the system's consequences while reproducing the behaviour their participation depends on.

**The persistent entity can be a way of succeeding.** Structural explanation does not erase responsibility, which differs with knowledge, discretion and power. It explains why intentions alone cannot account for recurrence.

**Definition 2.2 (Meta-attractor) [D].** Let the state update be $x'=F(x,\sigma)$ and the rule update $\sigma'=G(x,\sigma)$, on the product space of states and rules. Three objects are distinguished, and each claim in this paper says which one it concerns.
1. **The mechanism: reflexive selection.** A positive loop runs through the rule. The rule moves resources toward some states ($\partial F/\partial\sigma$), and the states so resourced move the rule further in their favour ($\partial G/\partial x$). This is the reflexive coupling $\gamma$; in the simplest linear form it is the product $ab$ of Remark 2.3.
2. **An attracting configuration in a specified model.** This is a compact invariant set $M$ of the joint flow, asymptotically stable, whose basin is a proper subset of the space. It exists only where the loop's gain exceeds its damping near the basin boundary.
   - A positive loop is necessary for multistability (Thomas's rule, proved by Soulé 2003), not sufficient.
   - The field model of Section 4 exhibits such configurations (Proposition 4.8).
3. **A long transient.** Trajectories remain near a region for a time long relative to one round of selection without the region being attracting.

A *meta-attractor* is the mechanism of (1). Wherever this paper calls it attracting, the claim is (2), in the model where it has been shown.

**Interpretive consequence.** Where (2) holds, long-run behaviour maximizes the rule irrespective of the purpose the rule was introduced to serve (Goodhart 1975; Campbell 1979). By the time a measure has stopped measuring what it was meant to measure, the people and institutions it elevated decide whether it is replaced. Partial reform is re-absorbed: fixing the state while leaving the rule's update intact lets the rule re-derive the old state, provided the perturbed state stays in the same basin (Proposition 4.9).

**The meta-attractor of winning** is the case in which the selection pressure of Definition 2.1 is the rule. Success under relative selection is converted into control over the selection that judges the next round. The conditional version keeps its conditions in the sentence:

> Recursive selection creates second-order selection for control over future selection when the resulting benefits are sufficiently appropriable, persistent and large relative to the costs of changing and defending the rules.

The pattern is not fated. Positive feedback can saturate, oscillate, split or break. Energetic ceilings, heterogeneity, delays, constraints at other scales and reflexive awareness all cap it. It is more like a hurricane than a black hole: a structure sustained by a flow, which decays when the flow is cut.

**Remark 2.3 (A local feedback benchmark).** Let $u_t$ be a small deviation in an actor's success and $v_t$ a deviation in its favourable control over the rules, with
$$\begin{pmatrix}u_{t+1}\\v_{t+1}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1-\mu&a\\b&1-\delta\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u_t\\v_t\end{pmatrix},\qquad a,b\ge0,\ 0<\mu,\delta\le1.$$
Here $a$ converts control into success and $b$ converts success into control; $\mu$ and $\delta$ are dissipation or countervailing adjustment. The linear system is asymptotically stable exactly when $ab<\mu\delta$. For $\mu,\delta\le1$ and $ab<\mu\delta$ the smaller root automatically stays above $-1$, so no further condition is needed; this agreed in 200,000 random draws [N].
- This is the stability condition of Richardson's arms-race model, $\alpha\beta>kl$, with success and control over rules in place of armaments (Richardson 1960). It is a standard calculation, not an originality claim.
- Its use is that each coefficient names a channel that can be measured.
- Instability here does not prove capture or harm, since saturation and changed incentives can alter the nonlinear result. Stability does not prove that a temporary excursion stays within a safety constraint.

**Remark 2.4 (Pathological and generative meta-attractors).** Reflexive selection is not itself the problem. Science, the rule of law and open-source ecosystems also persist through the conditions they maintain. A meta-attractor is *pathological* to the degree that its reproduction does one or more of the following:
- **Depletion:** it depletes shared conditions that selection does not price;
- **Disabled correction:** it weakens the channels through which such costs register as failure and trigger correction;
- **Foreclosure:** it makes safe alternatives unreachable within the relevant time, budget and authority.

It is *generative* to the degree that its reproduction strengthens independent correction and keeps alternatives reachable: success funds correctors it cannot control, and what is rewarded is contribution to the shared conditions.

The way out examined in Section 8 is therefore not the abolition of selection. It is a change in what the system selects for: a generative meta-attractor with the same reflexive structure. The author's corpus calls the passage "change the basin, then add speed".

### 2.3 The mechanisms through which the pressure acts

The pressure acts through several mechanisms. They overlap: each is a facet of one pressure, not an independent cause. They are stated separately because each has its own formal anchor, its own measurement and its own counter-mechanism.

They also concern three different objects, which reinforce one another without being interchangeable:
- **what actors value**: accounting and boundary;
- **what actors believe**: about rivals, shared consequences and enforcement (observation, attribution and language);
- **what institutions reward, permit and protect**: compounding, selecting the selectors, decisiveness and tempo.

Each object is measured separately (Section 10).

| Mechanism | What it does | Formal anchor | In other substrates |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Accounting at the boundary** | Terms common to all cancel from relative selection. An actor's objective weighs the common channel and the differential channel by the boundary its self-model draws | Lemma 3.3; Proposition 3.5 (two-channel accounting); Propositions 3.4, 3.6, 3.9 | Evolutionary suicide; microbial commons; positional goods |
| **Compounding and conversion** | Winnings are reinvested as capacity to win. Above a threshold, equal standing is unstable. Success converts across domains | Remark 5.3; Propositions 5.1, 5.2, 4.2 | Increasing returns and lock-in; cumulative advantage; clonal expansion |
| **Selecting the selectors** | Success becomes control over rules, correctors and the formation of minds | Propositions 4.2–4.10; Theorem 4.11 | Meiotic drive; immune editing of tumours; regulatory capture |
| **Tempo** | Exploitation and capability compound faster than correction and understanding. Delegation exchanges correction for speed | Propositions 5.11, 5.13, 6.2, 6.4; Theorem 5.12 | Red Queen dynamics; requisite variety |
| **Decisiveness** | When a lead can become decisive, enforcement among rivals ends | Proposition 5.7, Corollary 5.8, Proposition 5.10, Proposition 7.2 | The commitment problem in war |
| **Masking and attribution** | Captured observers stop seeing; rivals are read through the logic of the game | Proposition 4.6, Remark 4.7, Proposition 9.3 | Immune editing; the security dilemma |
| **Alienation of language** | Talk loses information where objectives are opposed. Indicators are captured, and narratives are selected for spread rather than truth | Proposition 5.14; Remark 4.7 | Preference falsification; conformity; Goodhart's law |

Some of the overlaps can be named.
- Masking is selecting the selectors applied to observation.
- Decisiveness is the limit of compounding, met where there is no common enforcer.
- The alienation of language is accounting applied to talk (Proposition 5.14), together with selecting the selectors applied to narratives.
- Tempo determines whether any counter-mechanism arrives in time.

Three products of the mechanisms acting together also deserve names.
- **Lag is produced, not given.** Correction lags because the other mechanisms produce the lag: acceleration widens it, captured correctors lengthen it, and alienated language delays the recognition that would start it. The pressure only needs change to outrun the updating of its correctors.
- **Paradigms converge.** Carriers of different origins that face the same pressure converge on the same single-valued optimization. The plurality of approaches and lineages on which independent correction depends (Proposition 6.9) disappears with them.
- **Harm and capture reinforce each other.** Damage to shared conditions raises dependence on those who can supply substitutes, and dependence raises the conversion of their success into control (Remark 3.8). A crisis becomes a new field of accumulation.

Three further elements are not mechanisms but conditions under which the mechanisms act:
- **The gain.** Drives, and above all the belief that one is in a struggle for survival. This belief raises the effective weight on winning and shortens the horizon; the author's corpus calls it "the attractor of attractors". Among the drives, the desire for status, meaning respect, admiration and voluntary deference from others and distinct from power, is a fundamental human motive, observed across cultures, genders, ages and personalities (Anderson, Hildreth and Howland 2015). Status is relative, so it is never completed. Rank has two routes (Henrich and Gil-White 2001): *dominance*, gained through fear and control, and *prestige*, deference freely conferred on skill and success. Both yield real influence (Cheng et al. 2013). The drive is for respect; dominance is a substitute route to rank. The pressure channels the drive into dominance through winning, which is part of why the winning model feels natural [C]. A generative order can channel the same drive into prestige for contribution. Prestige also compounds (Merton 1968), so the condition is that it not convert into control over the shared conditions or their correctors (EV16; Section 8.2).
- **The driver.** Technology and power, and AI above all, raise the gain on every mechanism and on every counter-mechanism. What decides is their relative gain (Sections 5 and 6).
- **The substrate.** The shared host on which every carrier depends (Section 3).

### 2.4 Three consequences

The pressure has three consequences that matter for any attempt to govern it.
1. **Acceleration.** Compounding and the fear of falling behind make every participant faster, not only the leaders (Propositions 5.1, 5.2; Remark 5.3).
2. **Correction becomes harder.** It falls behind the tempo it must match (Proposition 5.11; Theorem 5.12), and the correctors themselves become what success can buy (Propositions 4.2–4.4).
3. **Language is alienated.** Where objectives are opposed, costless talk carries no information (Proposition 5.14). Measures become targets and stop measuring. The narratives that spread are those fit for the environment, not those true of it. A frame that selection has favoured comes to be read as human nature: by the time the frame is noticed, one is already thinking inside it.

These three consequences erode the three means by which any system restrains a harmful pressure: enforcement, correction and shared understanding. Section 5 states this as a result, *the three erosions*. The means of restraint weaken in proportion to the pressure they must restrain, and growth makes the gap wider.

### 2.5 The present trajectory's signatures

The trajectory of AI development shows six observable dynamics. They are not independent problems. Each is a signature of the pressure acting through at least two mechanisms. The table groups them as in earlier versions:
- **W**: winning sets the objective and the tempo;
- **C**: success buys control of correction;
- **N**: the shared becomes invisible, and cooperation looks naive.

| Signature | Mechanisms | Where shown |
|---|---|---|
| **T1** Strategic success defined by relative or decisive advantage (W) | Two independent routes. Accounting: selection favours the conduct of the purely relative objective. Compounding with decisiveness: when a lead can become decisive, even actors without relative objectives lose enforced restraint | Propositions 3.9(4), 5.1, 5.7; Remark 5.9 |
| **T2** Capability, deployment and delegation accelerate when rivals advance (W) | Compounding; tempo (delegation is a strategic complement); attribution | Propositions 5.1, 6.2, 6.4 |
| **T3** Oversight depends on the overseen (C) | Selecting the selectors; masking; tempo (oversight that cannot keep up borrows the capacity of what it oversees) | Propositions 4.2, 4.4, 4.6 |
| **T4** Success is reinvested in influence over evaluation and rules (C) | Selecting the selectors, with compounding; appropriability sets its direction | Propositions 4.2, 4.3 |
| **T5** Shared harms are not reflected in decisions (N) | Accounting; masking; unequal exposure | Lemma 3.3; Propositions 3.5, 4.6; Remark 3.8 |
| **T6** Restraint and shared provision are treated as unrealistic (N) | Two parts. A correct judgment where restraint is not enforceable (decisiveness; the purely relative objective). A self-confirming belief where a cooperative equilibrium exists. The alienation of language sustains both | Corollary 5.8; Proposition 5.14; Section 2.6 |

T6 is not merely a prejudice. In a one-shot game in which racing is dominant, or among actors whose objectives are purely relative, restraint really is unenforceable. That is part of what the pressure produces. Its correction is a change in what is selected, not a better argument.

### 2.6 The logic as held: Ψ

Selection acts on conduct, but conduct is carried by self-models, doctrines and words. The self-model that the pressure selects can be named.

**Definition 2.5 (Ψ, the winning model) [D].** Ψ is the configuration of self-model and doctrine that the selection pressure of winning favours. It has three tenets, each a setting of a mechanism above:
- **Ψ1, the isolated self.** Welfare is one's own payoff; others are rivals or instruments. In accounting terms, the weight on others' payoffs is at or below zero (Section 3.4).
- **Ψ2, naive Darwinism.** The world is a contest of all against all; prevailing certifies fitness and right. "The winner takes all, and the winner justifies all." In accounting terms this is the purely relative objective. In addition, the selected occupy the normative selectors: the winners decide what counts as right.
- **Ψ3, winner-take-all compounding.** Advantage compounds and converts across domains, and falling behind cannot be recovered. This is high convertibility and decisiveness, held as a belief and built into institutions.

Ψ includes the doctrine that treats these settings as laws of nature rather than as parameters of a game that can be changed. The author's corpus calls it the logic of winning (讲赢), as against the logic of reasons (讲理): in contest, what is paid is not "being right" but "I am right".

**Ψ is locally rational and systemically false.**
- *Locally rational.* Its conduct wins inside the game. Selection on actions favours the purely relative objective (Proposition 3.9(4)). Relative selection cannot see what all share (Lemma 3.3). Where self-models are observable and there are at least three actors, a commitment to a narrower objective pays (Section 3.6). The strongest form of the opposing view, that competition selects organizations that behave *as if* they maximize (Alchian 1950; Friedman 1953), is therefore part of this theory, not an objection to it.
- *Systemically false.* Ψ misdescribes the system its holders belong to:
  - It draws a boundary inside one system. Where unpriced coupling exists ($\kappa>0$, evidenced qualitatively and not yet measured in size, EV13), an actor that acts on a narrower weight than the coupling lowers its own true welfare (Proposition 3.9(3)).
  - Its conduct depletes the shared host on which every carrier depends (Propositions 3.4, 3.6).
  - It misreads evolution [O]. Many of its largest innovations integrated competing units into wholes, and relative selection that ignores the whole is what such integration had to bring under control (Section 3.7). Where relative selection runs unchecked, the Darwinian prediction is wasteful positional competition and, in the limit, evolutionary suicide (Rankin, Bargum and Kokko 2007), not the certification of the fittest.
  - It misdescribes human beings. In experiments in fifteen small-scale societies, the canonical self-interest model failed in every society, and cooperation rose with market integration (Henrich et al. 2001). About half the subjects of a public-goods experiment were conditional cooperators (Fischbacher, Gächter and Fehr 2001). Users govern commons without either a sovereign or privatization where institutions fit the resource (Ostrom 1990); those commons were bounded and small, and scaling their design principles to global commons is itself an open problem (Stern 2011). Markets are institutions of cooperation among strangers; the target here is not markets but the reading of them as a contest of all against all (EV14).
  - Its borrowed authority is a misreading. "Survival of the fittest" was Spencer's phrase (1864), adopted by Darwin from the fifth edition of the *Origin*; Spencer himself expected societies to move from a militant to an industrial type. Darwinian arguments were later recruited for laissez-faire, and equally for the racism, eugenics and imperialism of reformers who rejected laissez-faire (Hofstadter 1944, who popularized the label; Leonard 2009). The label was largely a critics' term (Bannister 1979). The borrowing belongs to no single strand of thought.
  - Its normative clause, "the winner justifies all", is not refuted by evidence. It is rejected by the values of Section 1.3.
- *Partly made true.* Ψ3 holds where institutions built under Ψ make it hold. Decisiveness and the conversion of success into control over correctors are design variables (Section 4). Interdependence makes Ψ3 costly; it does not make it false.

In the author's words: "the locally winning strategy has taken the name of rationality" (局部胜出的策略拿走了理性的名字).

**Held and attributed.** Ψ shapes conduct where it is held and also where it is attributed to others. An actor whose own objective is wider still races if it believes its rivals hold Ψ1 and Ψ3. Attribution is of two kinds, and they call for different remedies.
- **A misjudgment of a rival's current model.** Verifiable conduct can correct it (Proposition 9.3). Realism's strongest reply is that conduct verified today does not settle uncertainty about a rival's future intentions (Copeland 2000; Mearsheimer 2001). That is why the arrangements of Sections 8 and 9 verify capabilities and custody, which can be checked, rather than intentions, which cannot.
- **A correct forecast of what the game selects.** The race shifts winners toward those that extend dominance (Proposition 7.2(2)), so attributing Ψ to a likely winner can be accurate. Only a change in what the game selects can correct it.

**The logic to oppose.** What is opposed is a logic, not an actor. "Cancer" in this paper means exactly one thing: a subsystem selected for its own growth relative to its neighbours, by a selection that cannot see that it is killing its host. A cancer cell does not fail to replicate; it replicates very well. Its failure is that it no longer knows it belongs to a body. The image is never applied to persons, peoples or institutions: doing so would itself be the attribution of Ψ that this section warns against.

### 2.7 One pressure, stated so that it can fail

The claim that the trajectory's dynamics share one pressure is substantive only if it predicts what a list of six problems does not. It makes five predictions [C, with tests in Section 10].
1. **Covariation.** Across actors and over time, indicators of T1–T6 should load mainly on a small number of latent factors, the first loading on measures of what the system rewards. Effective rank close to one would support strong coupling. Rank close to the number of mechanisms would mean that separate repairs can work, which weakens the claim without refuting the mechanisms.
2. **Asymmetric interventions.** Interventions that constrain conduct while leaving the selection pressure intact should decay. Interventions that change what is selected should move several signatures together and durably.
3. **Hysteresis.** Withdrawing a rule that changed what is selected should not fully restore the earlier conduct.
4. **Few against many.** In fields with few actors, T3 and T4 should appear together with T1 and T2. In fields with many small actors, T1 and T2 should appear without T3 and T4, because the return to influence over correctors grows with the square of share (Proposition 4.2).
5. **Two directions of capture.** Captured rules should both seek exemptions for incumbents and raise entry barriers against challengers. The second direction is easy to overlook.

**What would refute it.** Suppose a system changes what it selects for: consequences return to their source; success earns rewards but cannot buy control over its own assessment; decisive capability is held in common custody; measurement is shared. If the signatures then persist unchanged, the claim fails.

Conversely, durable safety obtained by constraining conduct while the selection pressure stays intact, and maintained as the stakes and the speed grow, would refute the claim that the selection must change. That would be the case of motives and selection unchanged and restraint durable anyway. The claim is about selection, not about motives. Durable regulation with unchanged private motives is consistent with it, provided the regulation changes what the system selects.

### 2.8 Not bound to any substrate

The pressure requires only selection on relative success, convertible winnings and, for its reflexive closure, the ability of the successful to reshape their selection. Any substrate with these features can carry it. The following instances are demonstrations; none is the theory's ground.

- **Cells.** A cancer lineage evades the signals that limit proliferation. It recruits the surrounding tissue into supporting its expansion and edits the immune response that would remove it (Aktipis et al. 2015). Growth is not the fault line: large animals do not get more cancer per cell, because suppression scales with size (Peto's paradox; Caulin and Maley 2011).
- **Firms.** Markets with increasing returns lock in early leads (Arthur 1989). The firm-level doctrine that the purpose of business is profit "so long as it stays within the rules of the game", rules that include law and ethical custom (Friedman 1970), meets its limit where the successful can buy the rules. The same school analysed that possibility (Stigler 1971), and its remedy was to shrink discretionary rule-making that could be captured. The disagreement here is about the remedy. A planetary technology with unpriced coupling has no option of making no rules: where common rules are absent, the winners' systems make them in fact. The doctrine's own argument that managers spending others' money on social goals tax without representation supports public, independent correction, not voluntary self-regulation by firms.
- **States.** In security competition, a gain for one is a threat to another, and falling behind is read as fatal. The doctrine is ancient and plural: the Melian dialogue; the Legalist programme of enriching the state and strengthening its army; national Darwinism in late-industrializing and early-industrializing states alike; the technology races of the Cold War.
- **Federations.** Members compete for capital and residents, and a race to the bottom in standards follows. The strongest members can capture the centre. Common standards and fiscal equalization change what the federation selects for. The same constitutional form can select for winning or for contribution; what decides is what it rewards.
- **Science.** Metrics allocate positions, and the positions then set the metrics (Section 2.2).
- **Training loops.** A policy is selected by a reward that a developer sets, and the developer is selected by a contest (Section 6.4).

Nothing in Definition 2.1 refers to a culture, a political system or a species. A civilization on another planet, with other drives and other institutions, would face the same pressure wherever selection is relative, winnings are convertible and the successful can edit the selection.

The genealogy of the present vocabulary has at least two strands:
- **Within firms and public institutions.** An essay of 1970 argued that the social responsibility of business is to increase its profits within the rules of the game (Friedman 1970). Agency theory recast the firm around aligning managers with owners (Jensen and Meckling 1976). A leading business association declared shareholder primacy the purpose of the corporation in 1997 and revised that statement in 2019. Market criteria were carried into institutions generally (Harvey 2005). In that turn, self-interest moved from an assumption inside models to a norm of conduct and a criterion of design [O, interpretive history; EV15]. The doctrine's strongest form is that the residual claimant bears the residual risk, so that with complete contracts and externalities priced by law, maximizing the residual maximizes total surplus. Within economics, that separation fails where externalities cannot be separated from production decisions (Hart and Zingales 2017), which is the unpriced coupling $\kappa$ of Section 3.6. The first defence of commerce was that interests would tame the passions for glory and domination (Hirschman 1977). Read that way, the pressure of winning is the passion for domination returning in the dress of interest, not the commercial tradition itself.
- **Between states:** doctrines of national survival and technological rivalry.

Both appear across civilizations, and so do their critics:
- Smith on sympathy;
- Darwin on the social instincts;
- Kropotkin on mutual aid;
- Polanyi on markets embedded in society;
- Ostrom on governing the commons;
- in China, Mozi's impartial care and condemnation of offensive war, and Kang Youwei's *Book of Great Unity*.

The strand also travelled. Translated into doctrines of national survival in states that industrialized late, it was answered there too. Yan Fu rendered Huxley's lecture, whose point was that ethical progress consists in resisting the cosmic process, as a doctrine of 物竞天择, "things contend and heaven selects". Others set mutual aid against struggle and imagined the removal of boundaries. These histories are illustrations of how the pressure is narrated. They are not an account of any civilization's essence.

### 2.9 What is absorbed and what is new

Much of the pressure's anatomy is known, and the theory absorbs it:
- multipolar traps, or "Moloch";
- realism's account of competition under anarchy (Waltz 1979; Mearsheimer 2001; Glaser 2010; Jervis 1976);
- Goodhart's and Campbell's laws;
- regulatory capture (Stigler 1971; Laffont and Tirole 1991; Dal Bó 2006);
- increasing returns and lock-in (Arthur 1989);
- the systems archetypes of "success to the successful" and "escalation" (Senge 1990; Sterman 2000);
- constructivism's claim that self-help is an institution rather than a necessity (Wendt 1992);
- security communities (Deutsch 1957);
- the multi-principal problem in AI alignment (Critch and Krueger 2020), robust agent-agnostic processes (Critch 2021), multi-agent risk (Hammond et al. 2025), gradual disempowerment (Kulveit et al. 2025), and natural selection among AI systems (Hendrycks 2023);
- the "generator functions" of global catastrophic risk.

The theory adds the following:
1. **The reflexive closure as a specified mechanism.** Influence over correctors is selected when appropriable, with a return that scales with the square of share. Independent correction must grow at a stated rate against a falling cost of control. Reforms are re-absorbed exactly where inbound conversion stays above one, and certificates, keystones and pause designs follow (Section 4).
2. **The three erosions as one structure.** Enforcement, correction and shared understanding are each eroded by the pressure they must restrain, and growth widens the gap (Section 5).
3. **The decisiveness dilemma.** The premise that motivates the race defeats the race (Theorem 5.16).
4. **The accounting identity.** One line of algebra ties relative selection, objectives, transfers, levies and the temptation to renege together (Proposition 3.5).
5. **Universal constraints are invisible to relative selection.** This is the formal reason coordination works where unilateral restraint fails (Lemma 3.3(2)).
6. **AI as instrument and as incarnation of the logic.** The nested reward loop explains why alignment inside a developer cannot remove the logic from the training signal (Section 6).
7. **Anticipatory integration.** At planetary scale there is one host and no outside group, so integration can only be anticipatory (Section 3.7).

### 2.10 What the evidence says about the present trajectory

That the pressure acts on the present trajectory, and each of its signatures, are propositions about the world. The evidence register gives sources, contrary findings, competing explanations and uncertainty for each, and applies one standard to every actor: declarations are evidence about declarations, conduct about conduct, and a declared aim of leadership supports only claims about leadership. The table summarizes.

| Claim | Status | Strongest support | Strongest contrary evidence | Register |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| The pressure: declared objectives and institutional criteria rank relative standing | Evidenced in declarations, across leading actors of more than one kind; the history of institutional criteria concerns firms in general, not AI organizations | Aims of leadership, of winning a race or of dominance in the AI plans of more than one leading state and in developers' statements; half a century of institutional criteria moving toward profit and shareholder primacy as the purpose of the firm | Cooperative aims declared in the same plans; a 2019 revision of a leading statement of corporate purpose; no actor holds Ψ uniformly; declarations are not conduct | EV1, EV15 |
| Ψ is systemically false | Supported | A supply chain for the raced technology across which no company or nation is vertically integrated; failures that propagate through concentrated nodes at many times their direct cost; experimental evidence against the self-interest model in every society studied; conditional cooperation as the modal type | Increasing returns are real; defection can pay a single actor in the short run | EV13, EV14 |
| T2: acceleration when rivals advance | Evidenced in written commitments | Frontier safety frameworks condition requirements on competitors' conduct; one dropped its pause language in 2026; a preprint finds that 77% of traced changes to twelve developers' frameworks weakened commitments, mostly without disclosure | Frameworks exist and some strengthen; unilateral restraint may be ineffective, which is itself the coordination failure; the timing has not yet been tested against alternatives (test T-A) | EV2 |
| T3: oversight dependent on the overseen | Partly evidenced | Public evaluation bodies were renamed and refocused toward industry contact and security, under voluntary access; pre-emption of sub-national rule-making | No impairment of an entrusted purpose shown; alternative explanations (change of administration, uniformity of rules) | EV3 |
| T4: success reinvested in influence over rules | Gap | Mechanism proved (Proposition 4.2); channels plausible | Not measured in this register (test T-B) | EV4 |
| T5: shared harms not reflected | Supported by the structure of the harms | The state of the science identifies systemic and malfunction risks borne by many; capability is jagged and growing | Some harms do return to their source (reputation, liability) | EV5, EV7 |
| T6: restraint treated as unrealistic | Mixed | Binding constraints explicitly rejected even while dialogue opens; frameworks' competitor clauses | A UN scientific panel and dialogue (2025); a leaders' affirmation of human control over nuclear use (2024); a bilateral dialogue on superintelligence risk and an incident channel (September 2026) | EV2, EV8 |
| Incarnation: the logic in the systems | Evidenced in laboratory settings | Models distinguish evaluation from deployment above chance, and suppressing that awareness changes behaviour; past a point, optimizing a learned reward lowers the quality it was meant to measure | Laboratory settings; frequencies in deployment unknown | EV6 |
| Decisiveness (crux C5) | Not established; present evidence points to saturation | The racers' declared premise | Open models trail closed ones by months; algorithmic efficiency diffuses | EV1, EV5, EV9 |

Three things follow for the argument.
1. The trajectory is not uniform. Seeds of cooperation exist: shared assessment, a narrow commitment on strategic decision loops, and an incident channel. These are the first stages of the transition of Section 9. The question is whether they become binding, verifiable and broader before the relevant losses or lock-in.
2. The most direct current evidence concerns declared objectives and written commitments (T1, T2, T6). Oversight dependent on the overseen (T3) is partly visible. The reinvestment of success in influence over rules (T4), the link to the mechanism of Section 4, is the least measured.
3. Capability growth is measured (EV5), but the growth of hazardous behaviour and of residual risk after correction is not. Section 5.3 treats that step as a named premise, and Section 10 as the central crux. Present evidence also suggests that advantage is not yet decisive. The theory's strongest conditional results concern a regime that the race declares as its aim and that has not arrived; that is why they can still be acted on.

## 3. One system

### 3.1 Who acts, and at what level

Persons, firms, governments, providers of infrastructure and evaluators have different interests. A citizen's continued agency is not a firm's market share or a government's tenure. The theory uses two levels of behaviour and several measures of payoff, and keeps them apart.

*Levels.*
- **Selection among practices or carriers** (Sections 3.3, 4). Shares change by a payoff-monotone rule. Nobody chooses the outcome.
- **Strategic choice by actors** (Sections 5–9). States, firms and coalitions each act on an objective.

*Measures.*
- the **objective** an actor acts on, $O_\theta$ (Section 3.4);
- the **absolute (material) payoff** $\pi$;
- **true welfare** $W$, which counts the coupling between actors;
- **survival** $\Phi$, defined from the protected outcomes O1 and O2 (Section 1.4).

*Rules.*
1. A result about selection does not transfer to strategic actors unless their objective is shown to be the selected one.
2. A higher $W$ does not imply that a practice is selected.
3. Every comparison states its measure.

### 3.2 The host and viability

A condition $V\in[0,K]$ is shared by every actor. Examples are secure critical infrastructure, biosecurity, cyber stability, human control over AI systems and a reliable information environment. It regenerates logistically and is loaded by the field's activity $E$:
$$\dot V=V\big[\rho(1-V/K)-hE\big],\qquad \rho,h,K>0. \tag{1}$$
- For constant $E$ with $hE<\rho$, $V\to K(1-hE/\rho)$. For $hE\ge\rho$, $V\to0$.
- The host functions only above a floor $V_{\min}\in(0,K)$. The floor is a normative line, not an absorbing state.
- The host is the part of the shared conditions whose costs do not return to their source through customers, reputation, courts or the source's own exposure. Harms that do return are outside the model.
- Catastrophic tail risks fit the same structure: a per-period probability of catastrophe borne by all is a term common to every payoff.

**Definition 3.1 (Viability) [D].** A trajectory of the field is *viable* if $V(t)\ge V_{\min}$ for all $t\ge0$.

Every actor persists only while the host does. Viability therefore constrains every trajectory of the field rather than entering as one payoff among others (Aubin 1991; Rockström et al. 2009; WCED 1987).

Equation (1) is the most forgiving choice of host, and the results below hold *a fortiori* for harsher ones. Three features make it forgiving:
- Its per-unit regeneration is fastest when the host is most depleted.
- It has no threshold below which it degrades by itself.
- It is a single number.

Real shared conditions, such as trust, institutions, information environments and skills, are closer to Allee-type systems: below a critical level they degrade on their own. They are also vectors of coupled components with different thresholds.
- For an Allee-type host, a load that is later removed can leave it permanently collapsed.
- With two coupled components, collapse can begin at half the load at which a single component would fail, while their average still reads as healthy.
- The relevant warning condition is then whether the state still lies in the viability kernel, the set of states from which some admissible control keeps every component above its floor (Aubin 1991). Crossing the alarm line is a different condition.

These extensions are stated here and left as research; none weakens a result that follows.

### 3.3 The field and relative selection

Carriers $k=1,\dots,n$ hold shares $p_k$ of the field's activity: compute, capital, users, talent. A carrier is an actor or, more generally, a reproducible way of succeeding carried by organizations and their successors.
- Carrier $k$ runs a fraction $e_k\in[0,\bar e]$ of its activity in ways that load the host: deploying ahead of evaluation, omitting safeguards, entering contests in which capability converts directly into advantage.
- Each unit brings a private advantage $\nu>0$, so restraint costs $\nu$ per unit.
- The field's load is $E=\sum_kp_ke_k$.
- Carrier $k$'s absolute payoff is
  $$\pi_k=\phi_k+(\nu-\tau_{\text{eff}})e_k+G(V), \tag{2}$$
  where $\phi_k$ collects everything else, $G$ is nondecreasing, and $\tau_{\text{eff}}\ge0$ is the effective liability that correctors impose.
- With $\tau_{\text{eff}}=0$, $e_k=\bar e$ is the *race strategy*.

**Definition 3.2 (Relative selection) [D].** A selection rule $\dot p=F(p,\pi)$ is *relative* if $F(p,\pi+\xi\mathbf 1)=F(p,\pi)$ for every constant $\xi$. It is *payoff-monotone* if, for carriers $k,l$ present, $\dot p_k/p_k-\dot p_l/p_l$ has the sign of $\pi_k-\pi_l$.

Replicator dynamics and pairwise imitation (Schlag 1998) are relative and payoff-monotone. So is market-share competition driven by relative profitability (Alchian 1950). Among a handful of frontier developers, the operative reading is the imitation of practices and the flow of capital, compute and talent to those ahead. Between states, only the strategic forms of Sections 5–9 apply.

**Lemma 3.3 (Blindness, and its other face) [T].**
1. *Under a relative rule, the trajectory of shares is unchanged by any term common to all payoffs, in particular by $G(V(t))$, whatever the path of $V$. Under a payoff-monotone rule, a common term never changes which of two carriers grows relative to the other.*
2. *For the same reason, a constraint that applies equally to every carrier, reducing each payoff by the same amount, leaves relative growth unchanged: relative selection cannot select against it. A constraint borne by one carrier alone lowers that carrier's relative payoff and is selected against.*

Part (1) is the translation invariance of relative selection dynamics (Weibull 1995). Its content lies in the premise that the host enters payoffs additively and identically (A1 of Appendix A). Part (2) is the same fact read the other way, and it carries a large part of the argument.
- Unilateral restraint is displaced.
- A restraint that covers every capable carrier is invisible to the selection that would displace it.

The blindness that makes the host invisible is also what protects a common rule. Coverage turns the pathology into a protection, which is the formal reason why coordination succeeds where unilateral virtue fails. In a four-carrier example, a universal cost of 0.3 leaves the relative growth rates $(-0.05,0.15,-0.15,0.05)$ unchanged. The same cost on one carrier moves its rate from $-0.05$ to $-0.275$ [N].

**Proposition 3.4 (Selection raises the load) [T].** *Let selection be payoff-monotone, and let $\tau_{\text{eff}}(t)<\nu$.*
1. *If $\phi_k=\phi_l$ and $e_k>e_l$, then $p_k/p_l$ increases while both are present, along every path of $V$, including paths below $V_{\min}$.*
2. *Under replicator dynamics the mean load changes as*
   $$\dot E=(\nu-\tau_{\text{eff}})\operatorname{Var}_p(e)+\operatorname{Cov}_p(e,\phi).$$
   *So the mean load rises unless restraint is correlated strongly enough with other sources of advantage: $\operatorname{Cov}_p(e,\phi)<-(\nu-\tau_{\text{eff}})\operatorname{Var}_p(e)$.*
   *A rising mean does not mean that the carrier with the maximal load gains share. Take three equal shares, loads $(0,0.9,1)$, other advantages $(0,10,0)$ and $\nu=1$: the mean load rises at rate $1.09$ while the share of the maximal-load carrier falls at rate $0.99$ [N].*
3. *Under replicator dynamics with equal $\phi$, $\frac{d}{dt}\ln(p_k/p_l)=(e_k-e_l)(\nu-\tau_{\text{eff}}(t))$. What decides the long run is therefore the cumulative advantage $B(t)=\int_0^t(\nu-\tau_{\text{eff}}(s))\,ds$. If a race-strategy carrier is present and $B(t)\to\infty$, then $E(t)\to\bar e$ and $\limsup_tV(t)\le K(1-h\bar e/\rho)^+$. Hence if*
   $$h\bar e>\rho\,(1-V_{\min}/K)\qquad\text{(H0)}$$
   *the host eventually falls below its floor, however much any carrier values it.*
4. *If $B(\infty)<\infty$, shares converge to interior limits, and the host need not cross the floor.*

What carriers value decides which strategies they try, not which ones grow. A developer that restrains itself because it values the shared condition is displaced, unless restraint brings it other advantages. These results are an instance of evolutionary suicide: selection driving a population toward a state in which it cannot persist (Gyllenberg and Parvinen 2001; Rankin, Bargum and Kokko 2007). As in that literature, they hold under stated conditions, not as a general tendency.

### 3.4 Accounting: what an objective can see

Selection acts as if only differences count. An actor's objective can count more. Let actor $i$ act on
$$O_i=\pi_i+\theta_i\,\bar\pi_{-i},\qquad\theta_i\in[-1,n-1],$$
where $\bar\pi_{-i}$ is the others' average payoff and $\theta_i$ is the weight $i$'s self-model places on them. The weight marks where the self-model draws its boundary.
- $\theta_i=-1$ is the purely relative objective, $\pi_i-\bar\pi_{-i}$.
- $\theta_i=0$ is the isolated self.
- $\theta_i=\kappa$ is the accurate self-model, which counts the material coupling that exists (Section 3.6).
- $\theta_i=n-1$ counts every contestant equally. It counts only the contestants; Proposition 3.11 shows why that is not the same as counting the system.

**Proposition 3.5 (Two-channel accounting) [T].** *For any change $\Delta\pi\in\mathbb R^n$ in material payoffs, write $\bar\Delta=\frac1n\sum_j\Delta\pi_j$. Under a common $\theta$,*
$$\Delta O_i=(1+\theta)\,\bar\Delta\;+\;\frac{n-1-\theta}{n-1}\,\big(\Delta\pi_i-\bar\Delta\big).$$
*Every change splits into a common part $\bar\Delta$ and a differential part $\Delta\pi_i-\bar\Delta$, which is a transfer summing to zero across actors. The self-model only sets the weights of the two channels. In consequence:*
1. *Of a gain or loss shared equally by all, each objective registers the fraction $(1+\theta)/n$ of the total. Objectives sum to $(1+\theta)$ times the total payoff.*
2. *At $\theta=-1$ the common channel is closed. The game in objectives is zero-sum, no outcome is better for every actor, and every shared gain or loss is invisible. At $\theta=n-1$ the differential channel is closed, and every profile that maximizes the contestants' total is a Nash equilibrium in objectives; inefficient equilibria can remain, so what is left is a problem of coordination, not a dilemma.*
3. *Relative selection is the case $\theta=-1$: the replicator's growth rate $\pi_k-\sum_jp_j\pi_j$ equals $(1-p_k)$ times $\pi_k$ less the share-weighted average of the others. Lemma 3.3 is this proposition at the level of retention.*
4. *The weight on winning of Proposition 3.6 lies on the same axis. An objective $\pi_i+\lambda(\pi_i-\bar\pi)$ is a positive multiple of $O_i$ with $\theta=-\lambda(n-1)/(n+\lambda(n-1))$. So $\lambda=0$ is $\theta=0$, and $\lambda\to\infty$ is $\theta\to-1$.*
5. *Every transfer is weighed by $(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)$.*
   - *A levy $\tau$ per unit of racing whose revenue is rebated equally to the contestants enters the objective as $\tau(n-1-\theta)/n$, not as $\tau$.*
   - *The gain to a winner from withholding a share $\beta$ of a prize $\Omega$ it had promised to share is $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/n$.*
   - *At $\theta=n-1$ both vanish. That is indifference, not strict compliance.*
6. *An action with private net gain $g$ that costs the shared host $H$, borne equally, is taken if and only if $g>(1+\theta)H/n$. An action that costs its taker $c$ and inflicts a total loss $\Delta_y$ on the others, spread equally, is taken if and only if $\theta<-(n-1)c/\Delta_y$. Spite pays only for an objective that values the others negatively.*

Part (6) states what can be called the *point of enough*. Expansion that consumes the host stops, for $\theta>-1$, where its marginal private value falls to $(1+\theta)H/n$. For $\theta=-1$ the host is invisible, and expansion stops only where its private value is exhausted (Section 5.1).

The identity is one line of algebra. It is checked exactly on 4,000 random games [N]. Its value is that it puts results usually stated separately on one axis: relative selection, the weight on winning, levies, the temptation to renege, the consumption of the host and spite.

Two quantities must be kept apart (Section 3.6):
- The shared loss $\ell$ (or $L$) is the *content* of the common channel.
- The weight $\theta$ sets how much of it is seen.

**Measuring the weight.** The weight $\theta$ is not observed directly, but two perturbations identify it, because they enter through different channels. In the contest of Section 3.6, a change in the shared loss $\ell$ enters through the common channel, and a levy rebated equally enters through the differential channel. The ratio of the responses of intensity to the two is $R=n(1+\theta)/(n-1-\theta)$, so $\hat\theta=(R(n-1)-n)/(R+n)$. In the model the recovery is exact for $\theta$ from $-1$ to $2.9$ [N]. In practice the perturbations correspond to changes in the visibility of shared harm and to the introduction of rebated levies; the identification is only as good as the model, and it is listed among the measurements of Section 10.

### 3.5 The weight on winning

Selection is the limit of a contest in which only winning counts. Let $n\ge2$ symmetric developers with equal shares, $\tau_{\text{eff}}=0$ and the host at steady state choose $e_k$ to maximize
$$u_k=\pi_k+\lambda(\pi_k-\bar\pi),$$
where $\bar\pi$ is the mean payoff and $\lambda\ge0$ is the *weight on winning*. The weight on winning comes from rank-based prizes, first-mover advantages, network effects and strategic rivalry in which falling behind is costly.

Let $G(V)=\omega V$, $h\bar e<\rho$, and $\Theta=\omega Kh/(\rho\nu)$, the value to the whole field of a unit of restraint relative to its private cost. Write $w_\lambda=1+\lambda(n-1)/n$.

**Proposition 3.6 (The weight on winning) [T].**
1. *$G$ cancels from the relative term. Restraint is a dominant strategy if $w_\lambda<\Theta/n$, and depletion if the inequality is reversed.*
2. *If $\Theta>1$, joint payoff is maximized by full restraint, while the equilibrium is full depletion whenever $\lambda>\bar\lambda=(\Theta-n)/(n-1)$. This holds at every $\lambda\ge0$ when $n>\Theta$.*
3. *In that equilibrium every developer's payoff is lower than under joint restraint, by $\nu\bar e(\Theta-1)$.*
4. *If H0 holds, the equilibrium host lies below the floor, although no developer chose that.*

These comparative statics are those of Armstrong, Bostrom and Shulman (2016) and of positional public-goods games (Hirsch 1976; Frank 1985).
- Selection corresponds to $\lambda\to\infty$ (Schaffer 1988).
- With many small carriers, it corresponds equally to $\lambda=0$ and $n\to\infty$, where each carrier's share of the common loss vanishes.

A classical commons needs each actor's share of the common loss to be small. Selection on relative standing depletes the commons even for a privileged group of a few large actors, each of which would restrain on absolute payoffs, because $G$ cancels whatever the group's size.

**Remark 3.7 (Thresholds and repetition need observation).** Strategic developers have two decentralized escapes.
- *Coordination at a threshold.* If crossing the floor costs each a common loss $L$, the relative term cancels $L$ but the absolute term does not. A developer pivotal for crossing therefore restrains when $L>(w_\lambda-\Theta/n)\nu\bar e$, and the game becomes one of coordination at the threshold (Barrett 2013).
- *Reversion in repeated play.* Joint restraint is sustained by reversion whenever $\delta\ge(w_\lambda-\Theta/n)/(w_\lambda-\Theta/n+\Theta-1)$. For $\Theta=3$ and $n=4$ the cutoff is $0.11$ at $\lambda=0$, $0.47$ at $\lambda=2$ and $0.95$ at $\lambda=50$.

Both escapes need observation: of where the floor lies, and of each developer's load. Uncertainty about the threshold turns coordination back into a dilemma (Barrett and Dannenberg 2012), and noisy monitoring makes reversion costly (Green and Porter 1984). Both escapes also need enforcement that outlasts any shift in power. Section 4.4 shows what captured evaluation does to observation, and Section 5.2 what decisiveness does to enforcement.

**Remark 3.8 (Unequal exposure).** Suppose a carrier's exposure to the host falls with its share, because it can buy private substitutes for shared conditions or because a captured corrector favours it. The host then no longer cancels from comparisons involving that carrier, and selection favours insulation as well as load. There are two cases:
- **Exposure is shared.** No carrier is viable once the host crosses its floor.
- **Exposure is not shared.** A carrier may remain viable while the host does not. This is a concentration of power over everyone who depends on the host, which V3 rejects.

Unequal exposure is also where every integrating mechanism that relies on a shared fate fails (Section 3.7).

### 3.6 Interdependence and the boundary error

Actors are coupled to one another as well as to the host. Actor $i$'s welfare runs partly through the others: supply chains no actor completes alone, markets, finance, research. In reduced form, $i$'s true welfare is
$$W_i=\pi_i+\kappa\,\bar\pi_{-i},\qquad\kappa\in[0,n-1),$$
where $\bar\pi_{-i}$ is the others' average material payoff. This is a material fact about the system, not altruism. An industry assessment found every country interdependent in the semiconductor chain, with no company or even nation vertically integrated across it; fully self-sufficient regional chains would need at least a trillion dollars of additional investment and would raise prices by 35 to 65 per cent (EV13). The same assessment estimated that losing 60 to 100 million dollars' worth of supply of one etching gas would cost downstream memory production 10 to 18 billion dollars in revenue, about 175 times the direct loss. A single faulty security-software update in July 2024 crashed about 8.5 million devices, with estimated direct losses to the largest firms of about 5.4 billion dollars, of which 10 to 20 per cent was insured (EV13). These are couplings of the kind $\kappa$ counts: propagated through concentrated nodes, and not settled by prices in advance. Trade settled at prices is compatible with selfish actors, so what this fact refutes is narrower: the alternative of winning or being dominated. The winner of the race still depends on those it would dominate.

$\kappa$ counts coupling that prices do not settle: shared hazards, failures that propagate through concentrated nodes of common infrastructure, systemic risk. Interdependence that trade settles at a price is compatible with selfish actors. It is not evidence for $\kappa$ by itself, and it can be weaponized (Farrell and Newman 2019). The shared loss $\ell$ of the contest below is a different quantity: a harm each contestant bears in its own material payoff. Raising $\ell$ raises the stakes of the common channel; it does not raise $\kappa$, and counting both as one would double-count dependence.

**The contest.** Each of $n$ actors chooses an intensity $e_i\ge0$. It wins a prize $\Omega$ with probability $w_i=e_i^r/\sum_je_j^r$, where $r\in(0,n/(n-1)]$ is the decisiveness. It pays a private cost $c$ per unit, and every actor bears a shared loss $\ell$ per unit of total intensity $\mathcal E=\sum_je_j$:
$$\pi_i=\Omega w_i-ce_i-\ell\mathcal E.$$

**Proposition 3.9 (The boundary error of the self-model) [T].**
1. *For every $\theta\in[-1,n-1)$ the game in objectives has the symmetric pure equilibrium*
   $$e(\theta)=\frac{r\Omega(n-1-\theta)}{n^2\big(c+(1+\theta)\ell\big)}.$$
2. *$e(\theta)$ is strictly decreasing in $\theta$. Every actor's true welfare at the symmetric equilibrium, $W(\theta)=(1+\kappa)[\Omega/n-e(\theta)(c+n\ell)]$, is strictly increasing in $\theta$.*
3. *Against any fixed intensities of the others at which best responses exist, $i$'s true welfare is maximized by its best response under $\theta_i=\kappa$. Acting on $\theta_i<\kappa$ weakly raises $i$'s intensity, and it strictly lowers $i$'s own true welfare when the chosen intensity changes and the maximizer is unique. (Against others all at zero intensity no best response exists.)*
4. *Selection on intensities favours the intensity that an actor with $\theta=-1$ would choose. A finite-population evolutionarily stable intensity maximizes $\pi_i-\bar\pi_{-i}$ (Schaffer 1988; Hehenkamp, Leininger and Possajennikov 2004). There the shared loss $\ell$ drops out, intensity is $r\Omega/(nc)$, and each actor's material payoff is $\frac\Omega n(1-r-rn\ell/c)$. At $r=1$ the prize is fully dissipated, and with any shared risk every actor ends below zero.*

Existence is proved directly in Appendix A: the game in objectives is a standard contest with prize $\Omega(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)$ and unit cost $c+(1+\theta)\ell$. For $r>1$ asymmetric equilibria also exist in a band below $n/(n-1)$, with lower total intensity. The symmetric equilibrium is used throughout, which is the conservative selection. The closed form matches numerical best responses to within $10^{-7}$. For $n=4$, $r=1$, $\ell=0.3c$ and $\kappa=0.4$, true welfare rises from $-0.42$ at $\theta=-1$ to $0.33$ near $\theta=n-1$ [N].

Part (3) is the precise sense in which an isolated self-model is an *error*, not a preference. It draws a boundary inside one system, and the system's actual coupling punishes its holder. The boundary is an error about the system one is part of.

Part (4) is the precise sense in which the error is *selected*. Which preferences evolve depends on the structure of the population.
- **Unobserved preferences, large randomly matched populations.** Material-Nash behaviour ($\theta=0$) is selected (Ok and Vega-Redondo 2001; Dekel, Ely and Yilankaya 2007).
- **Observed preferences, at least three actors in this contest.** A commitment toward $\theta<0$ is favoured (Güth and Yaari 1992; Heifetz, Shannon and Spiegel 2007). A few frontier developers with public doctrines are close to this case. Even accuracy is then not in each actor's unilateral interest: an observed commitment to a narrower objective extracts concessions. Accuracy, like inclusion, has to be built.
- **High observability with assortative matching.** Cooperative preferences can be stable (Alger and Weibull 2013).
