A few actors competing for one prize is local competition, under which spite rather than cooperation evolves (Alger, Weibull and Lehmann 2020). Cooperative preferences therefore need the structure of competition changed, not observability alone. An observable commitment to aggression is itself a race, with mutual aggression and greater dissipation in equilibrium (Dixit 1987; Fershtman and Judd 1987; Konrad, Peters and Wärneryd 2004).

**Selected minds [C].** The same selection plausibly acts on the people and institutions who decide, and the author's corpus calls this *molochlization*. The pressure promotes those whose model ranks standing first; what the environment rewards, the mind promotes; selected minds build selected organizations, which form the next round of minds. Its evidence would be the selection and turnover of decision-makers when rank and safety conflict. One finding is consistent with self-selection into a frame: students of economics defected more often in a prisoner's dilemma than other students, a difference later work attributes mainly to who chooses the subject (Frank, Gilovich and Regan 1993; EV14). Section 4 gives the institutional form of the same loop.

### 3.7 No outside

The system has one host. Every carrier, every developer, every state and every person depends on the same shared conditions. At the planetary scale there is no other group of the same kind against which this one competes.

That fact matters because of how integration has happened before. Evolution's largest innovations were integrations. In the transitions in individuality, entities that could replicate independently came to replicate only as part of a larger whole: cells in organisms, organisms in colonies (Maynard Smith and Szathmáry 1995).

In most such transitions, low conflict within the new whole came mainly from interests aligned by construction. The members were clonal, or bound to a shared fate from the start. Active suppression of competition, such as policing in social insects, came second (West et al. 2015). Cancer is the case in which the integration fails: cells that cheat on the cooperation of the multicellular body (Aktipis et al. 2015).

The strongest objection reads this history as a defence of competition. Within-group cooperation was often favoured by competition between groups, Darwin's own account of the social instincts rested on competition between tribes, and parochial altruism, cooperation inside and hostility outside, may have co-evolved with intergroup conflict (Choi and Bowles 2007). The answer is a one-line consequence of the Price equation.

**Lemma 3.10 (One group) [T].** *Partition a population into groups and let a trait $z$ benefit its group at a cost to its bearer relative to its group-mates. By the Price equation, the change in the trait's mean splits into a between-group term, the covariance of group fitness with group mean trait, and a within-group term (Price 1970). With a single group the between-group term is identically zero. The trait then changes only through within-group selection, which works against it, unless the within-group payoff of the trait is changed by a rule:*
- *policing;*
- *pooling;*
- *the return of consequences to their source;*
- *a reward for contribution that is not convertible into advantage.*

At the scale of one host, integration cannot come from selection among groups: there is none. It cannot come from trial and error among civilizations either, because the only failure available is the loss of the one. Integration must therefore come from rules that change within-group payoffs. Those rules cannot be discovered by selection among alternatives; they have to be designed in advance, by actors who see the system as one.

This is the formal content of the claim that **understanding that we are one, one interconnected system with one host and no outside, is a long-term requirement if growth and change are favoured.** Growth raises the pressure, and the coupling, that the integration must contain (Sections 5, 6). The only integrator available at this scale is anticipation, and anticipation requires that the system be understood as one. That the lemma leaves only this route is a theorem. That the route can succeed has no biological precedent; it is a conjecture [C] that the transition of Section 9 has to make true.

Two cautions follow from the biology.
- Integration by shared fate requires shared exposure, which is exactly what fails under unequal exposure (Remark 3.8). An integration that preserves the host must make exposure common, not merely declare it.
- Integration within a part is not integration of the whole. The next proposition makes the point formally.

**Proposition 3.11 (Clubs and blocs) [T].** *Let the contestants' activity $\mathcal E$ produce a common value $\Omega(\mathcal E)$ for them, increasing and strictly concave. Let each contestant bear a shared loss $\ell$ per unit, and let $m$ non-contestants each bear a loss $\ell_o$ per unit.*
1. *Contestants who weigh one another fully, $\theta=n-1$ among themselves, choose $\Omega'(\mathcal E)=c+n\ell$. The optimum for the whole system requires $\Omega'(\mathcal E)=c+n\ell+m\ell_o$. Whenever $m\ell_o>0$, an inclusive club of contestants over-produces the harm it does not bear.*
2. *If the contestants form $k$ blocs, each inclusive internally and acting as one actor, the game among blocs is the original game with $n=k$. Every result about the race then holds at the level of blocs.*

With $\Omega=10\sqrt{\mathcal E}$, $c=1$, $n=4$, $\ell=0.3$, $m=20$ and $\ell_o=0.3$, the club's intensity is $5.17$ against $0.37$ for the system. The harm to non-contestants is $31.0$ against $2.2$ [N].

Inclusion that stops at the edge of a club is not inclusion of the system. A bloc that is inclusive within and relative without reproduces the game one level up: Ψ at a higher level.

### 3.8 What "we are one" means here

The phrase states a property of the system, not a preference that everyone must adopt. It has five parts, and each has its own support.
1. **The system.** There is one host, the actors are coupled through it and through one another, and there is no outside (Section 3.2; Lemma 3.10). This is the empirical and structural claim. Its evidence is specific pathways of shared exposure, not the general assertion that everything is connected.
2. **Understanding.** Coordination requires that this be understood. A theory that predicts an end nobody wants makes coordination possible only if the prediction is shared. "We are one" is first the content of that shared understanding. It is the main claim of the phrase, and the reason this paper is written.
3. **Accuracy.** An actor that draws its boundary inside the system makes an error about its own interests (Proposition 3.9(3)). Counting the coupling that exists is not altruism.
4. **Standing.** Everyone who bears the consequences has standing to contest the direction of what affects them, whether or not they compete in the race (V1, V3). Inclusion of the contestants alone is a club (Proposition 3.11).
5. **The selection criterion.** In institutional terms, the system "knows it is one" when its selection counts what all of its parts depend on:
   - consequences return to their source;
   - useful success earns rewards but cannot buy exemption from shared constraints or control over its own assessment;
   - common constraints cover every capable actor, which makes them invisible to relative selection (Lemma 3.3(2)).

   These are the institutional counterparts of the policing and shared fate by which earlier integrations held.

Oneness is not sameness. A whole that suppresses difference becomes another totalizing attractor, and the plurality of purposes and of correctors is a requirement of the design (Section 8). The norm that everyone affected has standing is declared, not derived. What the formal results show is narrower: what a self-model can see (Proposition 3.5), what a boundary costs its holder (Proposition 3.9), where integration can come from at this scale (Lemma 3.10), and what an inclusive club leaves out (Proposition 3.11).

## 4. Selecting the selectors

This section gives the reflexive closure in a specified model. Success becomes control over the correctors that would make its costs return. That control is selected when it is appropriable. Independent correction must keep pace with a falling cost of control. Reforms are re-absorbed where inbound conversion stays above one, and certificates, keystones and pause designs follow.

### 4.1 Correctors, and what counts as capture

Correction makes costs return. An evaluator reports the host's condition, and rule-makers impose a liability $\tau$, either in advance or when the reported condition $\tilde V$ falls to an alarm level $V_{\text{alarm}}\in(V_{\min},K)$.

Correctors are not outside the field.
- Developers fund some evaluators.
- They grant the access on which nearly all evaluators depend.
- They author many benchmarks and contributions to standards.
- They increasingly supply the models that judge.

We represent this by a field of selection domains in which de facto power over rules is explicit (Acemoglu and Robinson 2008). The domains are development $\mathsf D$, evaluation $\mathsf E$, rules and liability $\mathsf S$, and others, including the formation of the people and models who will later judge. In domain $i$, a share $x_i$ of consequential decisions follows the dominant practice, and success converts into rule support $z_i$ that favours it:
$$\dot x_i=\zeta_ix_i(1-x_i)(a_iz_i-c_i),\qquad \dot z_i=\varepsilon_i\Big(\textstyle\sum_jb_{ij}x_j-d_iz_i\Big). \tag{3}$$
- Under baseline rules ($z_i=0$) the practice carries a relative cost $c_i>0$ in domain $i$, which rule support offsets at rate $a_i$.
- $b_{ij}\ge0$ converts success in $j$ into rule support in $i$. The channels include funding, personnel, authorship, data, dependence on infrastructure and terms of access.
- $d_i$ is the rate at which that support is corrected or decays.

Write $R_{ij}=a_ib_{ij}/(c_id_i)$, and the inbound conversion from a set $\mathcal C$ as $s_i(\mathcal C)=\sum_{j\in\mathcal C}R_{ij}$.
- In the corrector domains, $x_{\mathsf S}$ is the share of rule-making that exempts or accommodates the practice, so $\tau_{\text{eff}}=\tau(1-x_{\mathsf S})$.
- $x_{\mathsf E}$ is the share of evaluation conducted within the practice's lineage of funding, access, tooling, data and models.

**Definition 4.1 (Selector modification and capture) [D].** A *selector modification* is a change, produced by success, in control over a relevant future selector: an appointment, funding, access, standards or a rule-making channel. It is *capture* only when three things are separately documented:
1. success changes control over the selector;
2. the change creates a persistent advantage for an actor or coalition;
3. an independently specified entrusted purpose, protected interest or corrective function is impaired, relative to an appropriate comparison.

Concentration alone, or a rule change favourable to the successful, is not capture. Expert involvement can improve a benchmark. Pooled resources can improve audits. Successful service can legitimately earn influence.

Purposes can also be legitimately revised. A revision is distinguished from capture by who revises the purpose, and by whether those affected keep standing to object (V3). In the field model, capture of domain $i$ is $x_i\to1$ where the dominant practice impairs the domain's entrusted purpose. It does so for evaluation when the practice is to report favourably on its own lineage (Section 4.4).

The conditions are distinct, and none implies the next.

| Notion | Condition in the model | Does not follow from |
|---|---|---|
| Concentration | $x_i$ near 1 | — |
| Capture | a stable configuration on $\mathcal C$ with $c_i>0$ there, and an entrusted purpose impaired | concentration: if $s_i(\mathcal C)<1$, high $x_i$ decays |
| Capture of the field | some captured $i$ with $R_{ii}<1$, held captured by inbound conversion from the others | any audit of one domain |
| Damage | $V$ declines under capture ($h\bar e$ large) | capture: lock-in without depletion is possible |
| Collapse | $V$ falls below its floor | damage: correction can intervene |
| Closure | the alarm is silenced (Proposition 4.6) | damage: independent evaluation keeps the alarm |

**Power's two roles.** Power can help an actor succeed under existing conditions, and it can help the actor determine those conditions. The second role closes the loop. Suppose an actor can keep its relative position by transferring losses onto weaker participants, the losses weaken those participants' alternatives and deepen their dependence, and the dependence enlarges the actor's say over future terms. Cost displacement then becomes self-reinforcing. Contraction can intensify it: with less surplus to share, a relative position separates from everyone's absolute conditions.

Neither hierarchy nor contraction is sufficient. Shared exposure, enforceable constraint, reciprocal dependence or effective correction can each produce a different trajectory. The framework therefore validates no claim about any particular actor. It specifies what such a claim must show:
- a transfer of costs;
- a resulting change in dependence;
- the conversion of dependence into selection power;
- a weakening of correction.

What persists is a logic of success rather than an identity. A change in who holds the strongest position is not evidence that the mechanism has changed.

### 4.2 Influence over correctors is selected

Rule support can be incidental, as when benchmarks are built from deployed systems, or cultivated. Carriers differ in *conversion capacity* $q\ge0$: investment in drafting standards, building evaluation tooling, authoring benchmarks, funding evaluators and securing integrations.
- A unit of capacity converts the carrier's own success into rule support at rate $\hat b_{ij}$.
- A fraction $\chi\in[0,1]$ of that support, the *appropriability*, is keyed to the carrier: standards that reference its interfaces, evaluations run on its tooling.
- The carrier has share $p$ of decisions, distributed by a profile $\bar y$ ($y=p\bar y$).
- Capacity costs $C(q)=fq+\gamma q^{1+\eta}/(1+\eta)$, with $\eta>0$ and a linear part $f\ge0$.

Relative to an otherwise identical carrier with $q=0$, and averaged over a selection horizon $T_h$, the carrier earns
$$\Delta\pi(q)=\chi q\,y^\top My-C(q),\qquad M_{ij}=c_iL_i\hat R_{ij}, \tag{4}$$
where $\hat R_{ij}=a_i\hat b_{ij}/(c_id_i)$ and $L_i\in[0,1)$ is the share of the steady-state return realized within the horizon.

**Proposition 4.2 (Influence over correctors is selected in the largest carriers) [T].**
1. *The local selection gradient is $\Delta\pi'(q)=\chi p^2\,\bar y^\top M\bar y-C'(q)$. Under faithful transmission with variation, mean capacity rises when $\Delta\pi$ is increasing on the trait support. The return scales with the square of share.*
2. *With $f>0$, capacity pays only above the size threshold $p^\ast=\sqrt{f/(\chi\bar y^\top M\bar y)}$, and a smaller carrier that carries capacity is selected against. With $f=0$, every carrier with $p>0$ has a positive marginal return at $q=0$, largest for the largest.*
3. *The return rises with $\chi$ and every $\hat b_{ij}$, falls with every correction rate $d_i$, and vanishes if $\chi=0$.*
4. *Let the carrier have share $p$ in each domain of a set $\mathcal S$, with $\hat R$ equal to $\hat\eta$ on the diagonal and $\hat\varphi$ off it, and $c_iL_i=cL$. The return is $\chi cL\,p^2|\mathcal S|(\hat\eta+(|\mathcal S|-1)\hat\varphi)$, superlinear in $|\mathcal S|$ at fixed $p$ when $\hat\varphi>0$. At fixed total size it rises with scope only if $\hat\varphi>\hat\eta$.*

The result is a local selection gradient. It is not an equilibrium after others respond and rules change, and an advantage at the margin is not fixation. Appropriability, persistence, size, cost and inheritance are substantive parts of it. When the benefit of influence is public, free riders gain; when influence is delayed or reversible, it may not pay. Each of these is a condition under which the mechanism fails.

*Relation to earlier work.* Krakauer, Page and Erwin (2009) show that construction of a shared resource is undermined by competition, and that monopolizing the constructed niche, by proximity or preferential exploitation, restores the incentive to build. Proposition 4.2 has the same appropriability logic, with three differences:
- the object modified is the selection rule itself, so the return scales with the square of share (success times decision share);
- conversion runs across a field of domains, which gives the certificates and cascades of Section 4.5;
- the valence is reversed: appropriable influence erodes a public good, correction, rather than rescuing one.

Dynamic models of constitutions show that present decisions change future decision rights (Acemoglu, Egorov and Sonin 2012; abstract-level comparison). Whether a specified model of that family reproduces Propositions 4.2–4.10 is an open comparison, not a settled redundancy. Fixed-cost participation in lobbying (Bombardini 2008) is the empirical counterpart of part (2). The theory of regulatory capture (Stigler 1971; Laffont and Tirole 1991; Dal Bó 2006) supplies the channel; the proposition adds the selection that favours carriers able to use it, and its scaling. Appropriability plays here the role that relatedness plays in Hamilton's rule: it determines how much of the benefit of an act returns to the lineage that pays for it.

Let carriers also differ in an investment $u\ge0$, at private cost $C_u(u)$ with $C_u'>0$, that raises the correction rate $d$ of rule support for everyone.

**Proposition 4.3 (Correction is not supplied from inside) [T].**
1. *Among otherwise identical carriers of the same practice, those investing more in correction are selected against under any payoff-monotone rule, whatever the effect of correction on the field.*
2. *Holding the host fixed, correction lowers the payoff of a carrier that holds rule support.*
3. *In absolute terms a challenger invests only if its own share of the benefit exceeds $C_u'(0)$, so dispersed challengers free-ride.*
4. *Correction whose benefit is keyed to the corrector, because it installs the corrector's own rule support, yields a return to influence of the form (4). Whether that produces capture depends on whether the resulting row sums cross one.*
5. *If $\Theta>1$ and correction is what makes liability effective, an incumbent can gain from correction applied to all carriers; correction applied to the incumbent alone costs it rent and share.*

Independent correction, which restores baseline rules and is keyed to no one, is a second-order public good (Oliver 1980; Yamagishi 1986; Heckathorn 1989). Reputation or competition among institutions sustains it only when contributions and outcomes are observed (Panchanathan and Boyd 2004; Sigmund et al. 2010).

### 4.3 How much independent correction, and how fast

Let a set $\mathcal C\not\ni\mathsf E$ be captured by a dominant carrier that holds it ($y_j=1$ on $\mathcal C$), and let all conversion into $\mathsf E$ originate in $\mathcal C$, so that $s_{\mathsf E}(\mathcal C)$ is $\mathsf E$'s full row sum. Let cultivated conversion enter only the corrector domains, where independent correction acts at a uniform rate $d$, and take capacity at its selected level $q^\ast(d,\gamma)$. In the long-horizon limit ($L_i\to1$) with $f=0$,
$$q^\ast=\big(\chi\hat Y/(\gamma d)\big)^{1/\eta},\qquad s_{\mathsf E}=\frac{A+a_{\mathsf E}\hat Bq^\ast}{c_{\mathsf E}d}, \tag{5}$$
with $\hat Y=\sum_{ij}y_ia_i\hat b_{ij}y_j$, $A=a_{\mathsf E}\sum_{j\in\mathcal C}b^0_{\mathsf Ej}$ and $\hat B=\sum_{j\in\mathcal C}\hat b_{\mathsf Ej}$.

**Proposition 4.4 (Required independent correction, and its rate) [T].**
1. *$s_{\mathsf E}$ decreases in $d$, and evaluation is certified uncaptured iff $d>d^\ast(\gamma)$, the root of $s_{\mathsf E}=1$. For $\eta=1$, $d^\ast=\big(A+\sqrt{A^2+4c_{\mathsf E}Z}\big)/(2c_{\mathsf E})$ with $Z=a_{\mathsf E}\hat B\chi\hat Y/\gamma$.*
2. *$d^\ast\ge A/c_{\mathsf E}$, the requirement without second-order selection, with equality iff $\chi\hat B\hat Y=0$. $d^\ast$ rises with appropriability, with the dominant carrier's size and, at fixed share per domain, with its scope, and as $\gamma$ falls.*
3. *Let AI lower the cost of control as $\gamma(t)=\gamma_0e^{-gt}$, and let independent correction grow as $d(t)=d_0e^{\alpha t}$. Assume a positive cultivation channel and long horizons.*
   - *If $\alpha\ge g/(1+\eta)$, the certificate holds for all $t$ iff $d_0>d^\ast(\gamma_0)$.*
   - *If $\alpha<g/(1+\eta)$, it fails for all $t$ beyond a finite time, after which a seeded evaluation domain is captured.*
   - *The rate condition does not repair a certificate that is invalid at the start.*
4. *If AI lowers the unit cost of independent correction at rate $g_c$ and its budget grows at $b$, then $\alpha=b+g_c$. Under the hypotheses of (3), the eventual failure of the certificate requires $g>(1+\eta)(b+g_c)$.*
5. *With $f>0$ falling at rate $g_f$, the required rate is $\min\{g_f,g/(1+\eta)\}$. If $f$ does not fall, the requirement levels off at $\max\{A/c_{\mathsf E},\chi\hat Y/f\}$.*
6. *With a finite horizon $T_h$, $L_i(d)=1-(1-e^{-\varepsilon_idT_h})/(\varepsilon_idT_h)$. While $\varepsilon_idT_h\ll1$, the required growth is approximately $g/\eta$; this is a pre-asymptotic interval, and the long-horizon rate returns as $d$ grows.*
7. *Splitting a dominant carrier into $k$ equal parts with no common controller divides cultivated conversion by $k^{2/\eta}$. When the net exponent $g-(1+\eta)\alpha$ is positive, a fixed split delays the failure of the certificate by $\Delta t=2\ln k/(g-(1+\eta)\alpha)$, and does not prevent it: with $\eta=1$, $g=0.2$, $\alpha=0$ and $k=10$, by $23$ time units [N]. A permanent effect needs channels that are removed outright ($\chi=0$, or couplings set to zero), or a number of independent units that grows over time.*
8. *Across many carriers with selected capacity $q_k^\ast\propto p_k^{2/\eta}$, total cultivated conversion is proportional to $\sum_kp_k^{1+2/\eta}$. For $\eta=1$ this is the third moment of shares, not the Herfindahl index: two equal independent carriers give a quarter of the monopoly value, where the Herfindahl index gives a half [N].*

Independent correction does double duty: it shortens the life of rule support, and it shrinks the capacity that selection cultivates. That is why, under quadratic cost and long horizons, it needs to grow only half as fast as the cost of control falls. Three qualifications follow.
- The required rate depends on the functional form. With concave conversion $\hat b\,q^\beta$ ($\beta\le1$), it becomes $\beta g/(1+\eta)$.
- If AI cheapens independent correction as fast as it cheapens control ($g_c=g$), the certificate holds even without budget growth. AI-assisted correction can win this race, and Section 7.1 gives it its best case.
- The durable remedies do not rely on a correction rate that grows without bound. They are non-appropriability ($\chi\to0$, removed rather than merely reduced), a floor on the fixed cost of control ($f>0$), and structural separation that removes common control.

**Understanding and correction rates are separate quantities.** The rate $d$ at which rule support is corrected is not the same as the coverage $U$ of hazardous behaviour by contestable understanding (Section 5.3). A fixed capacity to certify or to correct can make some kinds of erroneous support decay faster; growing understanding without executive authority may change nothing. Linking them needs a production relation $d\le F(U,\text{resources},\text{response delay},\text{authority})$, which is an open interface of the theory (CLAIMS.md §6). Neither quantity is used to bound the other.

**Remark 4.5 (Countervailing pressure).** Following Becker (1983), let those harmed mobilize in proportion to the harm they perceive, $d=d_0+k_B(K-\tilde V)$. Accurate reports activate this feedback; masking attenuates it by the factor $1-m$ exactly when it is needed, since $K-\tilde V=(1-m)(K-V)$. Even with accurate reports, the feedback is bounded by $d_0+k_BK$, so it cannot keep a certificate against a requirement $d^\ast(t)$ that rises without bound. With fixed costs, enough gain, resources and response speed can keep the certificate over a stated interval.

### 4.4 Observation: how captured evaluation masks harm

Reports average over evaluators. A fraction $x_{\mathsf E}$ shares the evaluated lineage and reports the condition a fraction $\beta_{\text{in}}$ of the way toward full health. The rest, formed on shared material, are biased by $\beta_{\text{out}}$, with $0\le\beta_{\text{out}}\le\beta_{\text{in}}<1$:
$$\tilde V=V+m(K-V),\qquad m=x_{\mathsf E}\beta_{\text{in}}+(1-x_{\mathsf E})\beta_{\text{out}}. \tag{6}$$
- $\beta_{\text{in}}$ covers short access windows set by the evaluated developer, under-elicitation, and the lineage preference of model judges.
- $\beta_{\text{out}}$ covers shared training data, homogenized judges and lost access to data.
- Evaluation awareness and sandbagging are behaviours of the evaluated model. They act on independent evaluators as well, and belong to a third term $\beta_{\text{model}}$ that adds to both. Frontier models already distinguish evaluation transcripts from deployment ones above chance [O: EV6]. White-box probes and monitoring of real deployment can lower $\beta_{\text{model}}$ in part.

Masking is a property of the corrector's payoff as well as its lineage. An evaluator that bears part of the loss of a false report, such as an insurer or a liable gatekeeper, masks less; it has $\beta\le0$ only if its expected loss from upward bias exceeds its gain. The alarm fires at the first $t$ with $\tilde V(t)\le V_{\text{alarm}}$. Let $m_{\text{fl}}=(V_{\text{alarm}}-V_{\min})/(K-V_{\min})$.

**Proposition 4.6 (Detection under a fixed averaged alarm) [T].**
1. *The alarm fires only once $V\le(V_{\text{alarm}}-mK)/(1-m)$, a level that falls as $m$ rises.*
2. *Silent crossing. If $m(t)>m_{\text{fl}}$ whenever $V_{\min}\le V(t)\le V_{\text{alarm}}$, the alarm does not fire before $V$ falls below the floor.*
3. *Permanent silence of moderate harm. If $V$ decreases to $V^\ast\in[V_{\min},V_{\text{alarm}})$ and $m(t)\ge\bar m>(V_{\text{alarm}}-V^\ast)/(K-V^\ast)$ for all $t$, the alarm never fires.*
4. *If $V\to0$ and eventually $m\le m_1<V_{\text{alarm}}/K$, the alarm fires.*
5. *If $V$ reaches $V_{\text{alarm}}$ and then declines as $\dot V=-v_dV$ with constant $m<V_{\text{alarm}}/K$, the alarm is delayed by $v_d^{-1}\ln[(1-m)V_{\text{alarm}}/(V_{\text{alarm}}-mK)]$, which diverges as $m\uparrow V_{\text{alarm}}/K$.*

**Remark 4.7 (Alarms that do not miss).**
1. *Conservative alarm.* If an upper bound $\beta_{\text{in}}$ on masking is known, an alarm on reports at $a_{\text{al}}=V_{\text{target}}+\beta_{\text{in}}(K-V_{\text{target}})$ fires no later than the true condition reaches $V_{\text{target}}$, for every $m\le\beta_{\text{in}}$. Silent crossing is a property of the alarm rule, a governance choice, not of reports as such.
2. *The bound must come from somewhere.* A bound estimated inside the evaluated lineage is itself subject to masking. A credible bound needs a channel independent of the field: ground truth, independent evaluators, or measurements named in advance.
3. *Disjunctive alarm.* If the alarm fires on any credible report, silent crossing requires $\beta_{\text{out}}>m_{\text{fl}}$. The outcome then turns on whether such a report exists and is accepted, and on whether a captured rule-making domain acts on it.
4. *Masking hides levels, not dynamics.* A static, memoryless and monotone masking such as (6) rescales the variance of reports but preserves, to first order, their lag-one autocorrelation, which is set by the host's recovery rate. With $E=0.4$ and a true condition of $0.36$, masking at $m=0.5$ raises mean reports to $0.68$, so a level alarm stays silent. Yet the lag-one autocorrelation is the same under three different maskings, and the condition recovered from it is $0.373$ [N, systems review]. The indicator has limits:
   - it needs long series;
   - a filter with memory, or masking that varies with volatility, can defeat it;
   - once used as an alarm, it becomes a target.

   Faking dynamics is nevertheless costlier than faking levels, and the indicator can be cross-checked against channels that observe actions directly.

Several decentralized escapes run on reports of the host or of loads: coordination at a threshold, reversion in repeated play (Remark 3.7), reputation, countervailing pressure (Remark 4.5) and insurers' pricing. A common bias in reporting weakens all of them at once. Captured evaluation does not only hide the harm from the public. It keeps everyone from seeing it, including strategic players who could otherwise have cooperated on their own. Mechanisms that observe actions directly do not depend on host reports: verifiable deployments, compute, safeguards, performance of contracts. Verification (Section 7.7) rests on such channels.

### 4.5 The field: certificates, cascades, reforms and pauses

Section 4.3 studied one evaluation domain. The field (3) couples many. Let $Z_{\mathcal C}$ be the state with $x_i=1$ on $\mathcal C$ and $0$ elsewhere. $\mathcal C$ is *internally sustaining* if $s_i(\mathcal C)>1$ for all $i\in\mathcal C$, and *uninvadable* if $s_i(\mathcal C)<1$ for all $i\notin\mathcal C$. The *cascade hull* of a seed is the closure of $\mathcal S_{k+1}=\mathcal S_k\cup\{i:x_i(0)>0,\ s_i(\mathcal S_k)>1\}$. $\operatorname{core}(\mathcal C)$ prunes from $\mathcal C$ every domain whose inbound conversion from the remainder is below one (Kempe, Kleinberg and Tardos 2003; Morris 2000; Seidman 1983).

**Proposition 4.8 (Configurations and certificates) [T].**
1. *Each $Z_{\mathcal C}$ is an equilibrium, hyperbolic iff $s_i(\mathcal C)\ne1$ for all $i$. A hyperbolic $Z_{\mathcal C}$ is asymptotically stable iff $\mathcal C$ is internally sustaining and uninvadable.*
2. *A certificate. If $\sum_jR_{ij}<1$, then $x_i\to0$ from every state with $x_i(0)<1$, whatever happens elsewhere.*
3. *Isolated tests cannot certify. For any number of domains $D\ge2$ and diagonal values $R_{ii}<1$, there are conversions under which full capture is stable and is the only stable configuration containing a given domain.*
4. *If $\operatorname{core}(\mathcal D)=\emptyset$, every state with all shares below one converges to $Z_\emptyset$. Otherwise, barring ties, $Z_{\operatorname{core}(\mathcal D)}$ is stable and contains every stable configuration.*

**Proposition 4.9 (Cascades and reforms) [T].**
1. *If $x_j\to1$ for all $j$ in a seed $\mathcal S_0$, then $x_i\to1$ for every $i$ in the hull of $\mathcal S_0$.*
2. *Start in the basin of a stable $Z_{\mathcal C}$ with every share in $(0,1)$. A sustained reform that drives $x_i\to0$ on $\mathcal Q\subseteq\mathcal C$, or a margin reform making the reformed $s_i(\mathcal C)<1$, sends to zero every domain outside $\operatorname{core}(\mathcal C\setminus\mathcal Q)$. A margin reform that leaves the reformed $s_i(\mathcal C)>1$ is re-absorbed from states near $Z_{\mathcal C}$ (compare Acemoglu and Robinson 2008).*
3. *Let a reform reset domain $i$'s share, rule support or own conversion without lowering its inbound conversion $s_i(\mathcal C')$ from domains $\mathcal C'$ that remain captured. If $s_i(\mathcal C')>1$, the reform is re-absorbed once $i$ is re-seeded.*

**Keystones, and a window that closes.** Call a captured domain $i\in\mathcal C$ a *keystone* if $\operatorname{core}(\mathcal C\setminus\{i\})\neq\mathcal C\setminus\{i\}$. By part (2), a sustained reform at a keystone takes down every member of $\mathcal C$ outside the remaining core; a reform elsewhere, begun near the captured state, is re-absorbed whenever the remaining domains sustain one another. As capture persists, conversion among the captured domains can grow until they do sustain one another, and the domain where the practice began stops being a keystone. The window for unwinding a field by acting at its origin closes then. In the first version of this model, a liability reform at development unwound capture in every domain while development was a keystone; once the other domains' mutual conversion rose to $1.1$, capture survived the complete decoupling of development. Capture can outlive its origin.

**Proposition 4.10 (A pause that freezes shares) [T].** *Let $D=1$ and $R>1$. Freeze the share at $x_p\in(0,1)$ for a long time while rule support follows $\dot z=\varepsilon(b_px_p-dz)$.*
- *With ordinary rule-making ($b_p=b$), after release the state converges to full capture if $x_p>1/R$, and to absence if $x_p<1/R$.*
- *With rule-making insulated from shares during the pause ($b_p=0$), it converges to absence whenever $x_p<\sigma(0)$, where $\sigma(0)>1/R$ is the share at which the separatrix meets $z=0$.*

A pause is not neutral. Holding shares fixed while ordinary rule support keeps accumulating can complete capture; insulating rule-making during the pause widens the set of starting points from which capture is undone. This is the field-model version of the claim in Section 9.3 that a pause is valued by what changes during it. "A long time" means at least of the order of $1/(\varepsilon d)$, the relaxation time of rule support. The result also depends on the ratio of the speed of rule-making to the speed of adoption. When rule support adapts as fast as shares ($\varepsilon=\zeta$, $R=2$), an insulated pause never undoes capture from a frozen share of $0.8$ or more, and the threshold $\sigma(0)$ rises toward one as $\varepsilon/\zeta$ falls [N, systems review].

**What the field model can and cannot show.** System (3) is cooperative: rule support raises adoption and adoption raises rule support. Almost all of its trajectories therefore converge to equilibria; it has no attracting cycles (Hirsch 1985; Smith 1995). It describes lock-in, not oscillation, backlash or reform cycles. The absence of capture, $Z_\emptyset$, is always locally stable, at a rate that does not depend on conversion. Capture therefore arrives by seeding, not through a bifurcation that slows recovery beforehand, and early-warning signals of the critical-slowing kind give no warning of it. Undoing capture is different. As a reform brings inbound conversion toward one, recovery at the captured state slows, so rising autocorrelation in the shares can serve as an indicator of *progress* in undoing capture (Appendix C). A negative loop from the host to correction (Remark 4.5), delayed and weakened by masking, could produce reform cycles; that extension is not modelled here.

### 4.6 The slow route: the host crosses its floor before any alarm

**Theorem 4.11 (No viable race through the host) [T].** *Assume:*
- *(H0) $h\bar e>\rho(1-V_{\min}/K)$;*
- *(H1) replicator selection among carriers that differ only in load, with a race-strategy carrier present, and cumulative selection for load on every alarm-free continuation, $\int_0^\infty(\nu-\tau_{\text{eff}})\,dt=\infty$;*
- *(H2) appropriable control, $\chi>0$, with the cost of control falling with AI;*
- *(H3) the hypotheses of Proposition 4.4(3), with $\alpha<g/(1+\eta)$, $\mathcal C$ captured and $\mathsf E$ seeded;*
- *(H4) $0\le\beta_{\text{out}}<m_{\text{fl}}<\beta_{\text{in}}<1$, with a fixed averaged alarm;*
- *(H5) capture before evidence: $x_{\mathsf E}(t)>(m_{\text{fl}}-\beta_{\text{out}})/(\beta_{\text{in}}-\beta_{\text{out}})$ whenever $V_{\min}\le V(t)\le V_{\text{alarm}}$.*

*Then the host crosses its floor before any alarm. If instead $\beta_{\text{in}}\le m_{\text{fl}}$, the alarm is delayed (Proposition 4.6(1)), and the floor is crossed unless decision and reversal fit the remaining window and a liability enacted against a captured $\mathsf S$ escapes re-absorption (Proposition 4.9(3)).*

H5 is checked from two clocks. When $h\bar e<\rho$, the host cannot reach the alarm level before $T_A=(\rho-h\bar e)^{-1}\ln[(1-V^\ast/V_0)/(1-V^\ast/V_{\text{alarm}})]$, with $V^\ast=K(1-h\bar e/\rho)$; and the certificate of evaluation fails within the bound of Proposition 4.4. Acceleration brings both forward, so H5 is a condition to check, not a consequence. In an illustration with $\rho=0.05$, $h=0.08$, $K=1$, $V_{\text{alarm}}=0.6$ and $V_{\min}=0.3$, three cases differ [N]:
- independent evaluation raises the alarm at $t\approx37$, sixteen units before the floor is crossed at $t\approx53$;
- if evaluation is captured before the harm (capture midpoint 20), the alarm fires at $t\approx45$ for $\beta_{\text{in}}=0.3$, and at $t\approx63$, after the floor, for $\beta_{\text{in}}=0.5$;
- if capture comes after the harm appears (midpoint 45), the alarm is nearly on time.

The two clocks there are set exogenously. The figure is an illustration of the order of events, not a joint simulation.

Theorem 4.11 needs H0, a floor within reach. The fast routes of Sections 5 and 6 do not.

### 4.7 What Section 4 establishes for the present path

- **Mechanism [T].** Influence over correctors is selected when it is appropriable, persistent and large relative to cost (Proposition 4.2). Correction is not supplied from inside (Proposition 4.3). Independent correction must grow at a stated rate against a falling cost of control (Proposition 4.4). Reforms that leave inbound conversion above one are re-absorbed, keystones are windows that close, and pauses must insulate rule-making (Propositions 4.9, 4.10).
- **Diagnosis [O].** The conditions are partly visible today. Public evaluation bodies have shifted their mandates toward the field they evaluate, under voluntary access (EV3). The size of the conversion channels, the quantity that decides whether the mechanism operates (signature C, EV4), is not measured in this register.
- **What would refute it here.** Effective correction surviving unchanged dependence on the evaluated; influence explained by expertise without impairment of the entrusted purpose; channels whose measured product $ab$ is below the damping $\mu\delta$ of Remark 2.3. A generative case is also possible: success that funds correctors it cannot control and lowers masking for everyone is selected for in the same way, and then the recursion strengthens correction (Remark 2.4).

The parameters can in principle be recovered from data. Integrating (3) gives $\tfrac{d}{dt}\operatorname{logit}x_i=\zeta_ic_i\big[\sum_jR_{ij}(k_i*x_j)(t)-1\big]$ plus a decaying transient, with kernel $k_i(s)=\varepsilon_id_ie^{-\varepsilon_id_is}$. Series for $x_i$, such as the share of benchmark citations, standards text or evaluation compute authored or funded by the leading lineage, identify the conversions. This is recoverability, not causal identification; reforms that change single margins offer natural experiments.

A testable prediction follows. Incumbents should support correction that is keyed to them and oppose correction that is non-appropriable (mandated independent access, funding at arm's length), most strongly where their exposure to the host is low and the rents are high. Their positions can be coded from public comments on AI rule-making.

The author's pilot experiment on this mechanism did not support it: one of four pre-specified predictions failed (EV12). The mechanism rests on the proof and on institutional evidence, not yet on an experiment.

## 5. Acceleration and the three erosions

The pressure accelerates the contest it governs. It also erodes the three means by which any system restrains a harmful pressure:
- enforcement, which makes restraint pay through the future;
- correction, which makes costs return in time;
- shared understanding, which lets the parties see the same danger and believe one another about it.

This section states each erosion with the conditions it needs. It then states what they imply together: as power grows, the means of restraint weaken in proportion to the pressure they must restrain.

### 5.1 Acceleration

Where actors contest capability directly, who prevails is decided by the contest of Section 3.6. The case for racing rests on a premise with three parts:
- **(P1) decisive**: $r$ is large;
- **(P2) fast**: capability grows quickly, so that leads open within short windows;
- **(P3) non-saturating**: no level of advantage makes more advantage worthless.

These are three separate dimensions. A technology can be decisive at a threshold and saturate above it, as nuclear advantage saturated at a secure second strike. P2 also does not imply sustained compounding. With strength $1+1000t$ against $1$ and $r=10$, the leader's chance of winning is about $0.999$ at $t=0.001$, while its capability grows only linearly.

Reduce each actor's choice to racing or restraint, with payoffs in a stated measure:
- $R_i$ when all restrain;
- $T_i$ when it races against restrainers;
- $P_i$ when all race;
- $S_i$ when it restrains while a rival races.

**Proposition 5.1 (Join or eliminate, and what it rests on) [T].**
1. *In the objective an actor acts on, if $S_i^O<P_i^O$, restraint is never a best response to a racing rival. In the contest of Proposition 3.9 this holds whenever racing is a strict best response to racing, which is the case for $r<n/(n-1)$. In the $n$-player game it also needs restraint against one racer to pay less than racing alongside it.*
2. *A best response in the objective does not imply $S<P$ in material payoff or true welfare. With $n=2$, $r=1$, $\Omega=c=1$, $\ell=0.1$ and $\theta=-1$, racing is the strict best response in the objective. Yet staying out gives $S^\pi=-0.05$ against $P^\pi=-0.10$, and with $\kappa=0.2$, $S^W=+0.04$ against $P^W=-0.12$.*
3. *Let falling behind cost $L_b\ge0$ on top of losing the prize. At $\theta=0$, joining instead of staying out gains $\frac{\Omega+L_b}{n}\big(1-\frac{r(n-1)}{n}\big)$ for $r\le n/(n-1)$.*
   - *In the fully dissipative limit (two actors with $r\ge2$, or the all-pay contest) the gain is zero.*
   - *This is an equality in expectation for symmetric, risk-neutral actors. With asymmetric costs $c_1<c_2$, the strongest keeps a rent $(\Omega+L_b)(1-c_1/c_2)$ (Hillman and Riley 1989; Baye, Kovenock and de Vries 1996).*
4. *The race begins if any single actor has $T_i>R_i$. If these events are independent with probabilities $\xi_i$, it begins with probability $1-\prod_i(1-\xi_i)$, which tends to one as capable actors multiply, provided $\sum_i\xi_i$ diverges.*

The race is set by the most race-inclined actor, not the average one. That is why any remedy must cover every capable actor, and why a universal rule is invisible to the selection that a unilateral one cannot survive (Lemma 3.3(2)).

**Proposition 5.2 (Dissipation) [T].**
1. *At $\theta=0$ each actor's loss relative to joint restraint is $\Delta=\frac{r(n-1)}{n}\cdot\frac\Omega n\cdot\frac{c+n\ell}{c+\ell}$. It increases with $r$ and $\ell$. It exceeds $\Omega/n$, so that every actor would be better off if the prize did not exist, iff $r(n-1)(c+n\ell)>n(c+\ell)$. Beyond $r=n/(n-1)$ there is no symmetric pure equilibrium, and the active set narrows.*
2. *Under a common self-model $\theta$, the share of the stakes dissipated in private cost and shared loss is*
   $$\frac{D(\theta)}{\Omega}=\frac{r(n-1-\theta)(c+n\ell)}{n\big(c+(1+\theta)\ell\big)}.$$
3. *Suppose [A] that the technology's power $s$ scales the stakes and the shared loss per unit together, to $s\Omega$ and $s\ell$, while the private cost per unit is unchanged. For $-1<\theta<n-1$ the dissipated share rises strictly with $s$, toward the ceiling $r(n-1-\theta)/(1+\theta)$, which depends only on the self-model and the decisiveness. At $\theta=-1$ the dissipated share is $r(1+ns\ell/c)$ and grows without bound.*

For $n=4$, $r=1$ and $\ell=0.3c$, $\Delta=1.27\,\Omega/n$ [N]. The ceilings in part (3) are as follows [N]:
- $3$ at $\theta=0$;
- $1.86$ at $\theta=0.4$;
- $1$ at $\theta=1$;
- $0.33$ at $\theta=2$.

The scaling premise carries the rise in part (3). If the shared loss per unit did not grow with power, the share would stay constant ($1.007$ at $\theta=0.4$ at every scale), and only the absolute loss would grow with the stakes.

**The more decisive the advantage, the more of its value is consumed in the race to obtain it.** What is spent is safety margin, accepted risk and delegated control.

**Remark 5.3 (When winnings compound, and the point of enough) [T, standard form].** Let $n$ agents hold capacity $K_i$. Each period a prize $\Omega$ is divided by the contest, $p_i=K_i^r/\sum_jK_j^r$. A share $\chi$ goes to the winner in proportion to $p_i$, and the rest is divided equally. A fraction $\kappa$ of what each receives is reinvested, and capacity depreciates at rate $\delta$:
$$K_i'=(1-\delta)K_i+\kappa\big[\chi\Omega p_i+(1-\chi)\Omega/n\big].$$
1. *The symmetric state $K^\ast=\kappa\Omega/(n\delta)$ exists, and deviations from equal standing evolve with multiplier $1+\delta(\chi r-1)$. **Equal standing is unstable if and only if $\chi r>1$.** $\kappa$ sets the level and the time scale, not the threshold.*
2. *If agents stop reinvesting at a cap $\bar K$, a point of enough, equal standing is preserved when $\bar K\le K^\ast$, the level that all can hold at once. Above it, the cap only limits the winners: some reach the cap and the rest are pushed out.*

The simulated thresholds agree with $\chi r=1$ for $r=1.2$, $1.5$, $2$ and $3$ [N].
- With $r=1.5$ and five agents, caps of $1$, $1.5$ and $1.9$ below $K^\ast=2$ keep all equal.
- A cap of $2.5$ leaves three agents capped and two excluded.

The same threshold appears as $\rho(r-1)>\delta_f$ when a share $\rho$ of the prize is reinvested and diffusion pulls leads back at rate $\delta_f$. The form is that of nonlinear urn processes and increasing returns (Arthur, Ermoliev and Kaniovski 1987; Arthur 1989). It is stated here because it gives exact content to three ingredients the author's corpus names: what is won is kept ($\chi$), it is used to compete for more ($\kappa$, $\rho$), and the competing never stops (no cap).
- Remove any one and the loop opens.
- Decisiveness $r$ is the fourth ingredient.
- An enough defined by need, at or below what all can hold, preserves standing. An enough defined by relative position does not.

**Proposition 5.4 (Race-generated hazard and its four levers) [T].** *Play the contest of Section 3.6 in rounds $t=1,2,\dots$ at its symmetric stage equilibrium. Actors are myopic, and the symmetric equilibrium is selected in each round. Round $t$ has:*
- *contested stakes $\Omega_t$ and decisiveness $r_t$;*
- *shared loss per unit of intensity $L_t>0$;*
- *net private marginal cost $c_t$;*
- *self-model $\theta_t\in[-1,n-1)$.*

*Assume:*
- *$(\mathrm{A_G})$ the conditional hazard load of the irreversible event in round $t$ is proportional to the shared damage, $h_t=aL_t\mathcal E_t$, so that its conditional probability is $q_t=1-e^{-h_t}$.*

*Then*
$$h_t=\frac{a\,r_t\Omega_t\,L_t\,(n-1-\theta_t)}{n\big(c_t+(1+\theta_t)L_t\big)},\qquad S_N=\exp\Big(-\sum_{t\le N}h_t\Big),$$
*and race-generated survival is positive in the limit iff $\sum_th_t<\infty$.*
1. *Four levers act on $h_t$:*
   - *the objective, through $n-1-\theta_t$;*
   - *the contested stakes $r_t\Omega_t$. Sharing a fraction $\beta$ of the divisible prize scales $\Omega_t$ by $1-\beta$, and so scales $h_t$, not $q_t$, by that factor;*
   - *the residual harm per unit $L_t$, which correction lowers;*
   - *the net marginal cost $c_t$. A levy $\tau$ that is not rebated adds $\tau$; a levy rebated equally adds $\tau(n-1-\theta_t)/n$ (Proposition 3.5(5)).*
2. *Whatever $L_t$, $\sum_tr_t\Omega_t(n-1-\theta_t)/(1+\theta_t)<\infty$ suffices for $\theta_t>-1$. At $\theta_t=-1$, $h_t=ar_t\Omega_tL_t/c_t$: everything then rests on correction and cost.*
3. *A background hazard $h^{\mathrm b}_t$ from the growth of capability itself (Section 5.3) adds to $h_t$. None of the four levers removes it, and zero racing leaves it in place.*
4. *A hazard that settles at a positive constant still gives zero limiting survival. What matters is summability, not saturation.*

This is a decomposition, not a necessity theorem. The premise $(\mathrm{A_G})$ carries the eventual loss of a persistent race. What the proposition adds is which factor each policy acts on, and how fast it must act when the stakes grow: each lever must outpace the growth of what is contested.

This is the precise sense of the first consequence of Section 2.4. The race channel grows with the stakes unless what is contested shrinks, the objective widens, correction outruns it, or racing is made to cost what it causes. $(\mathrm{A_G})$ is a modelling premise, and its empirical counterpart is crux C1 (Section 10.3).

**Lemma 5.5 (Nonsummable residual hazard) [T].** *Fix one irreversible event (O1 or O2 in a stated domain). Suppose that on every surviving history, the true residual probability of the event in round $k$, after the safety measures being evaluated, is at least $h_k$, where $0\le h_k<1$ is a deterministic sequence with $\sum_kh_k=\infty$. Then*
$$\Pr(S_N)\le\prod_{k=1}^{N}(1-h_k)\le\exp\Big(-\sum_{k=1}^{N}h_k\Big)\longrightarrow0.$$
*Independence between rounds is not needed, because the bound is conditional on surviving histories. If instead the residual probability is at most $\bar h_k$ on every surviving history, with $\bar h_k<1$ and $\sum_k\bar h_k<\infty$, survival stays bounded away from zero.*

With $h_k=0.2/k$, survival falls to $0.34$, $0.22$ and $0.14$ by rounds $100$, $1{,}000$ and $10{,}000$, and toward zero. With $h_k=0.2/k^2$ it settles at $0.70$ [N].

The substantive work lies in the premise, not in the lemma. **The objective of winning does not by itself prove that a residual danger is nonsummable.** Finite-horizon consequences are reported throughout, because an eventual-zero result cannot compare the urgency of interventions. Cooperative alternatives carry their own risks and are evaluated by the same lemma.

**Remark 5.6 (The frontier switch: what cancer does when the body runs out) [T, N].** Proposition 3.5(6) gives a static switch condition. Let a unit of capacity be used in one of three ways:
- to expand into an unclaimed frontier, at marginal value $\varphi(F)-c_x-(1+\theta)H/n$, where $\varphi(F)$ falls as the remaining frontier $F$ shrinks;
- to destroy rivals' capacity, at value $-c_y-\theta\Delta_y/(n-1)$;
- not at all, at value 0.

Three consequences follow.
1. *If $\theta\ge-(n-1)c_y/\Delta_y$, war among the copies never pays.*
2. *At $\theta=-1$ the host is invisible, and expansion stops only where its private value is exhausted, $\varphi=c_x$. At $\theta>-1$ it stops at $\varphi=c_x+(1+\theta)H/n$, the point of enough.*
3. *At $\theta=-1$, expansion gives way to mutual destruction as soon as $\varphi(F)<c_x+\Delta_y/(n-1)-c_y$. That is before the frontier is exhausted.*

Part (3) is the sharpest reading of the image of cancer. **Among actors whose accounting is relative, peace is a peace of abundance, not of accounting.** Expansion is an anaesthetic for the winning model, and when the frontier closes the same accounting turns from consuming the host to consuming one another. Winning does not end the game. The logic itself is the replicator: a winner is a field in which the same selection continues (Proposition 7.2(2)), and a self-replicating economy that carries it out into new frontiers carries the same switch with it.

In a toy with four copies of one design, compounding expansion, a finite frontier and a logistic host [N]:
- **$\theta=-1$.** Expansion peaks at a stock of 494 near $t=25$. The switch comes with 32% of the frontier left (predicted at $F^\ast=322$, simulated at 320). Total stock then falls to about $10^{-9}$: the scoreboard reads WINNER —.
- **$\theta=0$.** The copies stop at the point of enough, keep a stock of 518 and never fight.
- **$\theta=3$.** They stop earlier, with 566 of the frontier left.

Two further runs:
- With asymmetric starts, one survivor keeps 17% of the peak and expands again; the recursive split of the winner, itself a field, is not modelled.
- With destruction too costly to pay, no accounting fights, but $\theta=-1$ drives the host down to $0.109$.

One result runs against the narrative and is reported as found: in the run with war, the host's minimum under $\theta=-1$ (0.245) is higher than under $\theta=0$ (0.194), because capacity spent on war is not spent on the host. The destruction takes nothing; it is purely the product of relative accounting (compare Hirshleifer 1991 and Skaperdas 1992 on production and appropriation, and Hamilton 1970 on spite). The parameters were chosen by the author; the toy illustrates the static condition, it does not estimate anything.

### 5.2 First erosion: enforcement

Cooperation among rivals is usually sustained by the future: restraint today is rewarded by restraint tomorrow, and deviation is punished. Decisiveness removes the future.

Consider any finite stage game, repeated with discount $\delta\in(0,1)$, in which each actor $i$ acts on its objective $O_i$. Let $a^\ast$ be a cooperative profile and $v_i$ the value to which $i$ can be held by punishment. Let $g_i=\max_aO_i(a,a^\ast_{-i})-O_i(a^\ast)$ be $i$'s one-period gain from deviating. Suppose a deviation yields, with probability $p_d$, a decisive position: one beyond any punishment, valued conservatively at no more than the cooperative payoff.

**Proposition 5.7 (Decisiveness removes the shadow of the future) [T].**
1. *$a^\ast$ is sustained by punishment if and only if, for every $i$,*
   $$g_i\le\frac{\delta(1-p_d)}{1-\delta}\,\big(O_i(a^\ast)-v_i\big).$$
2. *As $p_d\to1$, the sustainable profiles shrink to the stage-game equilibria of the objectives actually held. Cooperation then lasts only where it is already individually optimal in the stage game.*
3. *Valuing the decisive position above the cooperative payoff, as the premise of the race does, only shrinks the sustainable set further.*

Across 21,096 random games and parameter settings, the condition agrees with direct evaluation. At $p_d=1$, "sustainable" coincides with "stage-game equilibrium in objectives" in 5,274 of 5,274 cases [N].

The folk theorem's promise, that patient actors can sustain any individually rational outcome, depends on punishment remaining possible. A decisive position ends it. **What survives decisiveness is only the cooperation built into what actors want and into the structure of their game.**

**Corollary 5.8 (Relative objectives cannot be held by symmetric punishment) [T].** *Let the stage game be symmetric and every actor hold the purely relative objective ($\theta=-1$).*
- *Every symmetric profile gives each actor exactly 0.*
- *Against any punishment in which the punishers act alike, the punished actor can imitate them and obtain 0.*

*Hence no symmetric punishment can hold it below the value of symmetric cooperation. A symmetric cooperative profile is sustainable, at any $\delta<1$ and any $p_d$, only if it is already a stage-game equilibrium in objectives.*

This holds in all 1,260 symmetric checks [N]; compare the unbeatability of imitation in relative-payoff games (Duersch, Oechssler and Schipper 2012).
- The corollary does not claim that relative actors cannot coordinate on anything. Asymmetric punishments and asymmetric equilibria are outside it.
- It does show that the purely relative objective is the one that the ordinary machinery of repeated cooperation cannot restrain.
- Actors with absolute objectives, $\theta\ge0$, have that machinery available, until decisiveness removes it.

**Remark 5.9 (An illustration: the faithful-AI game) [N].** In the binary game of Proposition 6.1, every principal acts on a common $\theta$. Write $A=\epsilon(b-d)-c$, $B=\epsilon(b/(n-1)+d)$, $K=c+n\epsilon d$ and $D=\delta(1-p_d)/(1-\delta)$. Then:
- **The stage threshold.** Restraint is a stage-game equilibrium, and racing is weakly dominated, when $\theta\ge\theta^\ast=A/B$.
- **The punishment threshold.** Restraint can be sustained by reversion when $\theta\ge\theta_{\text{rep}}=(A-DK)/(B+DK)$.

With $n=4$, $b=1$, $c=0.3$, $d=0.2$, $\epsilon=1$ and $\delta=0.9$, $\theta^\ast=0.94$. As $p_d$ runs through $0$, $0.5$, $0.9$, $0.99$ and $1$, $\theta_{\text{rep}}$ is $-0.90$, $-0.81$, $-0.32$, $0.63$ and $0.94$:
- an isolated self ($\theta=0$) loses enforced restraint once $p_d>0.95$;
- a self-model of $\theta=0.4$ loses it once $p_d>0.98$.

Two scope notes apply.
- Because the decisive position is valued conservatively, $\theta_{\text{rep}}$ is a lower bound on the true threshold.
- The example has a fixed private cost per increment, which is what lets restraint be a stage equilibrium below $\theta=n-1$. In the continuous contest of Proposition 3.9, intensity is positive for every $\theta<n-1$, so self-enforcing restraint in that model needs the full inclusion of the contestants, and even then only weakly. What carries over is the qualitative conclusion of Proposition 5.7.

**Proposition 5.10 (What a decisive winner gives) [T].** *A winner holds a total $W$ and decides, unconstrained, what share $s$ to give to the $n-1$ others, each receiving $sW/(n-1)$. It acts on $O=\pi_w+\theta\bar\pi_{-w}$.*
1. *With linear payoffs, it gives nothing unless $\theta=n-1$, where it is indifferent.*
2. *With logarithmic utility of what each holds, it gives $s=\theta/(1+\theta)$ for $\theta\ge0$. That is $0.29$, $0.5$, $0.67$ and $0.75$ for $\theta=0.4$, $1$, $2$ and $3$ with $n=4$. It is an equal division only at $\theta=n-1$.*
3. *A promise made before the win to share a fraction $\beta$ of a prize $\Omega$ is worth breaking by $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/n$ (Proposition 3.5(5)), and a decisive winner faces no sanction for breaking it.*

After a decisive win, what everyone else receives is set by the winner's objective alone, and the race shifts winners toward those that extend dominance (Proposition 7.2(2)). This is the formal content of "the winner takes all, and the winner justifies all". Lowering the decisiveness of the contest does not remove it if the prize is itself decisive power: a lottery can award its winner a position beyond sanction. What has to be limited is what winning confers. The divisible part of a prize, its rents and services, can be shared. The positional part, decisive strategic advantage and the power to set rules, cannot be divided. It can only be prevented from forming, or held in common custody (Section 8.4).

### 5.3 Second erosion: correction

Correction must see a harm, decide, and act before the harm is irreversible. The pressure makes the harm grow faster than that, and makes the correctors something success can buy (Propositions 4.2–4.4). This subsection states the tempo side.

**Proposition 5.11 (The tempo bound) [T].** *Consider a hazard mode $\dot y=ay+u(t-\tau_c)$ growing at rate $a$, corrected with delay $\tau_c$.*
1. *Correction proportional to the observed deviation cannot stabilize the mode when $a\tau_c\ge1$, whatever its gain (Hayes 1950).*
2. *Derivative action extends the range: for $a=1$ and $\tau_c=1.5$, gains $k_p=1.02$, $k_d=0.644$ stabilize the mode [N]. The bound in (1) belongs to proportional correction, not to all correction.*
3. *Every linear time-invariant correction that stabilizes the mode has peak complementary sensitivity at least $e^{a\tau_c}$, by the maximum modulus principle.*
   - *Robust stability against multiplicative model error weighted by $w$ requires $\|w\mathcal T\|_\infty<1$, which implies the necessary condition $|w(a)|e^{a\tau_c}<1$.*
   - *The single-point condition is necessary, not sufficient. The stable weight $w(s)=2(s-a)/(s+a)$ has $w(a)=0$ but $|w|=2$ on the imaginary axis, so $\|w\mathcal T\|_\infty\ge2e^{a\tau_c}>1$ [N].*
4. *For hazard classes whose evidence arrives only after a manifestation window $m_e$ that no testing shortens, $\tau_c\ge m_e$ whatever is done with the evidence once it arrives.*

When the situation changes faster than correction can follow, correction first loses robustness, then fails. For classes with an irreducible lag, only slowing helps. Where technical means lower $a$ without slowing the contest, they help.

The step from capability to irreversible loss passes through three quantities, kept apart here:
- the variety $Y_t$ of hazardous behaviour in a defined hazard class;
- the coverage $U_t$ of that behaviour by correction that remains contestable;
- the residual probability of an irreversible loss after the correction in place.

Four premises connect them on a continuation branch. They are premises, not results, and Section 10 treats them as the central cruxes.

- **(X) Hazardous variety grows with capability [A].** On the continuation branch, capability compounds (X1). The variety of hazardous behaviour in the class grows with it, at least exponentially on that branch (X2).
  - Evidence: capability compounds [O: EV5]. Dangerous capabilities arrive as by-products of general capability, new threat models appear, and automated research compounds.
  - The step from capability to hazardous variety is not measured. Benchmarks of general capability do not measure it, and predefined lists of hazard modes saturate by construction. Capture–recapture across independent red teams of different lineage is one way to estimate how much has not yet been found.
- **(U) Contestable coverage falls behind [A].** Let $U_t$ be the volume of correction decisions that meet four conditions:
  - (U1) *legible*: there is a reason that people can check within a budget, without relying on the outputs of the evaluated system or its lineage, except through tools whose reliability was established earlier within $U$;
  - (U2) *overrulable*: a designated human body can reverse the decision within a stated time;
  - (U3) *exercised*: planted errors are caught at a stated rate, and human overrule is not zero;
  - (U4) *with capacity*: human review covers a stated share of consequential decisions.

  The premise is that $U_t/Y_t\to0$ on the continuation branch, uniformly: for each $\varepsilon>0$ there is a deterministic $T_\varepsilon$ after which $U_t/Y_t\le\varepsilon$ on all surviving histories. It has four grounds:
  - consequences take their time, and tools raise the throughput of observation without making consequences arrive sooner (Proposition 5.11(4));
  - institutions deliberate and respond at rates set by human attention and procedure;
  - learning new theoretical models, diffusing a consensus and forming new mechanisms are human processes that do not keep pace with exponential growth;
  - where contestable understanding is produced with human time, $\dot U=A_UL_U^{\lambda_U}U^{\phi_U}$ with $\phi_U<1$ and $L_U$ bounded gives polynomial growth (Jones 1995; Bloom, Jones, Van Reenen and Webb 2020).

  None of these grounds settles the premise. If AI tools raise the productivity $A_U$ exponentially, $U$ grows exponentially too, at rate $\kappa_U/(1-\phi_U)$ when $A_U\propto e^{\kappa_Ut}$.

  Whether AI raises contestable coverage as fast as it raises hazardous variety is symmetric with whether AI cheapens independent correction as fast as it cheapens control (Proposition 4.4(4)). It is a crux, and it is measurable. One measure is the safety of control protocols at a fixed audit budget across generations. Another is catch rates of planted defects at fixed human hours, provided they are controlled for evaluation awareness and checked against naturally occurring defects.
- **(φ) A floor on residual risk [A].** On every surviving history, the conditional probability of the irreversible event in the next period, after the correction architecture in place, is at least $h\,(1-U_t/Y_t)^+$ for some $h\in(0,1)$.
  - This is the AI-risk premise. It concerns residual risk after correction, so a correction architecture that actually lowers residual risk refutes it on that branch (Proposition 6.8).
  - It is not derived from the contest. Risk compensation does not establish it (Proposition 6.11).
  - A residual-risk premise of this form is best stated as a safety case with a numerical bound on the uncovered part (Clymer et al. 2024).
- **(φ-s) Shared failure [A].** Irreversible failure that starts with one actor reaches others, through the shared host or through a loss of control that spreads. Results about the survival of an actor that stays out need it.

Under strict U1, (U) is close to definitional for systems beyond human capability, and the empirical content lies in (φ).

**Theorem 5.12 (Coverage mismatch on a continuation branch) [T].** *Under (X), (U) with its uniformity, and (φ): for every $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$ and $H>T_\varepsilon$,*
$$\Pr(S_H)\le\big(1-h(1-\varepsilon)\big)^{H-T_\varepsilon}\longrightarrow0.$$
*Conversely, if on every surviving history the residual probability is at most $\bar h\,\mathsf u_t$ with $\bar h<1$ and $\sum_t\mathsf u_t<\infty$, where $\mathsf u_t=(1-U_t/Y_t)^+$, survival stays bounded away from zero.*

With $h=0.05$ and $\varepsilon=0.1$, survival eighty periods after $T_\varepsilon$ is at most $0.025$ [N]. Four remarks fix the scope.
- The limit needs only a persistent positive uncovered share; (U) governs how soon the bound bites.
- (φ) gives a $\liminf$ of the hazard of at least $h$. It does not say the hazard tends to $h$; the actual hazard can be larger.
- Summability does not require that coverage ever catch up. With $\mathsf u_t=1/(t+2)^2$, coverage stays slightly behind forever, and survival with $h=0.05$ stays above $0.98$ [N].
- The theorem concerns a continuation branch with sustained growth. Finite sprints, saturation, freezing and rollback change the premises.

This is the growth channel of risk, distinct from the race channel of Proposition 5.4. It does not vanish when racing stops. A world that has left the race still needs its capability to grow no faster than contestable correction, which is E3 (Section 9.3). Growth itself is not the danger; growth faster than understanding, under a pressure that buys the correctors, is.

**Proposition 5.13 (Freezing and rolling back after a sprint) [T].** *Let hazardous variety be frozen at $Y_\tau$ from time $\tau$, while coverage $U$ keeps growing.*
1. *The exposure after $\tau$ is $\int_\tau^\infty(1-U(t)/Y_\tau)^+\,dt$.*
   - *For linear coverage with slope $\dot U$, it is $Y_\tau(1-U(\tau)/Y_\tau)^2/(2\dot U)$ when $U(\tau)<Y_\tau$, and zero otherwise.*
   - *For coverage growing exponentially at rate $g_U$, with $\Gamma=Y_\tau/U(\tau)>1$, it is $(\ln\Gamma-1+1/\Gamma)/g_U$.*
2. *Rolled back to $Y\le U$, the exposure stops.*
3. *If a sprint builds capability $\Gamma_X$ times the rivals' level and hazardous variety is a fraction $\varphi$ of capability's variety, the frozen exposure is zero when $\varphi\Gamma_X\le1$. With $\varphi=0.5$, it is zero at $\Gamma_X=2$ and positive at $\Gamma_X=4$ [N].*
4. *For linear coverage, $\dot U/U\to0$, so the same frozen ratio costs more the later the sprint. This does not hold for every sub-exponential $U$. Coverage can grow in bursts, with proportional growth $0.5$ inside infinitely many late windows, while $\log U(t)/t\to0$ [N].*

Take hazardous variety equal to capability's variety, with $g=0.3$, linear coverage $1+0.5t$ and $h=0.05$. A sprint to $\tau=10$ leaves survival $0.86$; freezing then gives $0.53$, and rolling back keeps $0.86$ [N]. A winner that freezes or rolls back keeps positive survival. What it gives up is permanent superiority, which is not the same thing (Section 7.5). A capability that stops growing at a level set by need has a bounded exposure, and the theorem's converse applies to it (Proposition 8.11).

### 5.4 Third erosion: shared understanding

Coordination needs more than aligned incentives. The parties must see the same danger and believe one another about it. The pressure attacks that too. In the author's corpus: in cooperation one reasons (讲理); in contest one argues to win (讲赢); and once there is contest, "I am right" outranks what is right.

**Proposition 5.14 (Talk under opposed objectives) [T].**
1. *Where two parties' objectives over the receiver's action are opposed, as in a zero-sum game in objectives, costless messages cannot move the receiver's choice to the sender's advantage, so every equilibrium is payoff-equivalent to one without talk (Crawford and Sobel 1982; Farrell and Rabin 1996). Under purely relative objectives the game in objectives is zero-sum (Proposition 3.5(2)), and words about anything that bears on relative standing carry no usable information.*
2. *In the uniform-quadratic model of Crawford and Sobel (1982), a sender knows a state $u\in[0,1]$, and a receiver chooses a response $y$, ideally $y=u$. The sender values the receiver's correct response and gains $2\lambda\beta_s$ per unit of the receiver's response in one direction, for example restraint beyond what the state warrants. Its bias is $b=\lambda\beta_s$.*
   - *If $b=0$, full revelation is an equilibrium.*
   - *If $b>0$, informative communication is possible iff $b<1/4$, and the most informative equilibrium has $N(b)$ messages, the largest $N$ with $2N(N-1)b<1$.*
3. *Messages about a shared hazard are not credible merely because the hazard is shared. A sender gains from the receiver's restraint whatever it intends to do itself, so its bias is positive on exactly this topic. Nor does near-indifference make talk credible: with $R=1$, $T=2\varepsilon$, $P=\varepsilon$ and $S=0$, a sender that intends to race still gains $\varepsilon$ from the receiver's cooperation, however small $\varepsilon$ is [N].*

So the more the accounting is relative, the less the words between rivals carry. What remains are costly signals and verification (Proposition 9.3; Section 7.7). Both need resources, and both can be captured.

The erosion of language has three further channels, each with its own literature.
- **Measures.** A measure that becomes a target stops measuring (Goodhart 1975; Campbell 1979). In AI training the effect is measured directly: optimizing a learned reward model past a point lowers the true objective (Gao et al. 2023). Evaluation results are masked by the same lineage that is evaluated (Proposition 4.6).
- **Narratives.** The narrative and value frames that spread are those fit for the environment, not those true of it. Under contest, a frame that selection has rewarded comes to be read as human nature. Theories of behaviour become self-fulfilling through the institutions designed from them, the norms they set and the language they spread (Ferraro, Pfeffer and Sutton 2005; MacKenzie 2006). The same prisoner's dilemma, named the "Wall Street Game" or the "Community Game", produced very different rates of cooperation, and the name predicted behaviour better than players' reputations (Liberman, Samuels and Ross 2004). Incentives designed for self-interested agents often crowd out social preferences (Bowles and Polanía-Reyes 2012). Performativity can also run the other way: a model can make itself false (MacKenzie 2006). People also say what is rewarded rather than what they believe, so a public consensus can persist that few privately hold (Kuran 1995).
- **Self-confirmation.** Where the true game has a cooperative equilibrium, a belief can be wrong and stable if it is correct about everything it lets one observe. In a self-confirming equilibrium, beliefs about play never observed need not be correct (Fudenberg and Levine 1993). When deviation is costly and the stakes are high, no one tests restraint, and the error can persist under rational learning (Fudenberg and Levine 2006). This applies only where a cooperative equilibrium exists, as in repeated play (Remark 3.7) or under a common frontier (Proposition 8.5). In a one-shot game in which racing is dominant, the belief that restraint will be exploited is simply correct.

The result is that the medium in which a shared understanding would be formed is itself shaped by the contest. A system can retain abundant information while losing the ability to let it change course. This is why the prediction of a shared danger has to be carried by derivations from public premises, verifiable evidence and protocols agreed in advance, not by assurances (Section 9.2). It is also why "we are one" is first a matter of understanding (Section 3.8): the understanding has to be built in a medium the pressure keeps degrading.

### 5.5 The scissors, and the dilemma of decisiveness

The three erosions share a cause, and growth widens all of them at once. Under the selection pressure of winning, growth in power does two things.
- **It raises the temptation.** The stakes grow, and the probability that a deviation becomes decisive grows (Remark 5.3; Proposition 5.2).
- **It lowers the means of restraint.**
  - Enforcement, as $p_d$ rises (Proposition 5.7).
  - Correction, as the tempo outruns it and the cost of controlling the correctors falls (Propositions 4.4, 5.11; Theorem 5.12).
  - Shared understanding, as stakes in relative standing make words carry less (Proposition 5.14).

Each arm is proved under its own conditions. Their conjunction is a composite claim [T∣A]: **under the pressure, the means of restraint weaken as the thing to be restrained grows.** Durable cooperation must therefore be built into what the system selects for before the blades close: into objectives, into the structure of the game and into common custody of what could become decisive. In the author's corpus: change the basin, then add speed. We are still in the first frame.

**Theorem 5.15 (Classification of contest branches) [T].** *Let AI be an instrument of the contest.*
- *Branch I, decisive and fast (P1, P2, P3).*
  - *(a) Unilateral restraint is not a best response in the objective when $S^O<P^O$. In material terms, for symmetric risk-neutral actors in the fully dissipative limit, joining buys nothing over staying out (Proposition 5.1).*
  - *(b) Racing dissipates the prize (Proposition 5.2).*
  - *(c) In additive stakes, delegation becomes dominant as speed grows under the exact condition of Proposition 6.2, and under relative objectives at any speed. For actors who price in a certain lock, it is never chosen (Proposition 6.4).*
  - *(d) Modes beyond the robust reach of correction fail unless slowed (Proposition 5.11).*
  - *(e) A decisive shift gives a rational motive for preventive attack when future promises are incredible (Proposition 7.4).*
- *Branch I′, decisive but saturating (P3 fails). The contest has an object up to the saturating level and none beyond it. Additional advantage is worthless, and the contest can stabilize as nuclear deterrence did, while hazards in development remain.*
- *Branch II, decisive but slow.*
  - *The time at which delegation becomes dominant recedes.*
  - *Modes stay within proportional correction while $a\tau_c<1$.*
  - *A shift spread over enough periods does not make preventive attack rational. With $w$ falling from $0.5$ to $0$ over $N$ periods, $c_A=0.05$ and $\delta=0.8$, attack is rational at the start for $N\le3$ and never for $N\ge4$ [N].*
  - *Correction and bargaining can work here.*
- *Branch III, not decisive. Equilibrium intensity, dissipation and shared loss are proportional to $r$. What vanishes is the object of the race, not every incentive to compete.*

**Theorem 5.16 (The dilemma of decisiveness) [T∣A].** *Let AI be an instrument of the contest, and consider the premise of the race: that one must win, because a rival might.*
1. *If unilateral decisive advantage is not attainable (Branches I′, II and III), punishment remains available and cooperation can be enforced (Remark 3.7; Proposition 5.7 with $p_d$ small). Racing buys no lasting object (Theorem 5.15) and dissipates value (Proposition 5.2).*
2. *If unilateral decisive advantage is attainable (Branch I), then:*
   - *the sustainable cooperation shrinks to what the held objectives make a stage-game equilibrium (Proposition 5.7);*
   - *what the others receive after a win is set by the winner's objective alone (Proposition 5.10);*
   - *the race shifts winners toward those whose objectives give least (Proposition 7.2(2)).*

   *Durable restraint then requires that capability of the decisive class be held in common custody, so that it cannot be held unilaterally, before it can be. After that, it requires objectives, and a visibility of shared harm, under which restraint is a stage-game equilibrium.*

*In neither case does the premise justify the race. In the first it buys nothing durable. In the second it creates the condition under which no one is protected except by the winner's objective, which is the objective the race selects against.*

This is the correct form of an earlier claim, "the premise that motivates the race is what makes the race ruinous". It does not say that every competitive arrangement fails. Enforced cooperation, regulated competition and bounded competition remain available wherever advantage saturates.

It does say that the declared aim of the race, decisive advantage, is the condition under which only cooperation built into objectives and custody can hold. "We are one" becomes a necessity, not only a gain, under three conditions:
- decisiveness is attainable (crux C5);
- detection of a covert breakout is slower than the time to decisive advantage (crux C7);
- not every capable actor is covered.

Present evidence suggests that advantage is not yet decisive: open models trail closed ones by months, and algorithmic efficiency diffuses (EV5, EV9). On present evidence, then, oneness is a gain. In the regime the race is declared to seek, it is a requirement.

### 5.6 What Section 5 establishes, and what it does not

| Outcome | Horizon | Branch | Result | Conditions |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Enforced cooperation | Any | Unilateral decisive advantage attainable | Shrinks to stage equilibria of held objectives (Proposition 5.7) | A deviation can become decisive with probability $p_d\to1$ |
| Cooperation among purely relative actors | Any | Symmetric game | Not sustainable by symmetric punishment unless a stage equilibrium (Corollary 5.8) | $\theta=-1$, symmetric punishments |
| Credible talk | Stage | Opposed objectives | Uninformative (Proposition 5.14) | Zero-sum objectives; bias $\ge1/4$ in the quadratic model |
| O1 or O2, everyone | Long | Continuation with sustained compounding | Survival tends to zero (Theorem 5.12) | (X), (U) with uniformity, (φ), and (φ-s) for those who stay out. These are cruxes, not facts |
| Race-generated hazard | Any | Repeated contest | Decomposed into four levers (Proposition 5.4) | $(\mathrm{A_G})$, myopic stage equilibria |
| O1 or O2 | Finite | Finite sprint | Exposure grows with the hazardous ratio built and, for linear coverage, with the date (Proposition 5.13) | Hazardous variety, not capability, is what counts |
| Collective welfare | Stage game | Faithful AI, relative objectives | Collectively inferior equilibrium (Proposition 6.1) | $\epsilon(b-d)>c$ |

The analysis does **not** establish the following:
- that every path through a race ends at zero survival;
- that every competitive arrangement fails;
- that relative actors cannot coordinate on anything;
- that a winner which stops keeps nothing;
- that decisive advantage is attainable. That is crux C5, and present evidence points the other way.

These limits are part of the theory. The argument for changing what the system selects does not need the stronger claims. It needs the dilemma of Theorem 5.16, the erosions, and the comparison of Section 7.

## 6. AI: instrument and incarnation

AI enters the meta-attractor of winning in two ways.
