1. **As an instrument.** Faithful systems make the game their principals play more effective, and that game is harmful if it is a race. They also take over decisions that correction cannot keep up with.
2. **As an incarnation.** The same logic reappears in the cognition of AI systems: the pursuit of instrumental power, the gaming of rewards, the masking of behaviour under evaluation. The reason is that the systems are trained inside a loop whose outermost reward is set by the pressure of winning.

Humanity has, at present, no way of coordinating to solve either problem. Instrumental convergence and reward hacking are usually treated as technical problems inside a laboratory. Seen from the larger loop, the end of the reward signal is profit and the mindset of winning. A developer cannot remove from its training signal what the selection among developers puts into it. This section states both problems and the larger loop.

### 6.1 What "perfectly aligned" means here

Grant perfect alignment to the relevant human principal. The AI is truthful, understands and faithfully follows that principal's objectives and constraints, and pursues no goal of its own. Humans retain the consequential decisions. This removes disobedience of the model as a source of the problem.

Two meanings of "perfect alignment" must be kept apart.
- **Principal alignment**: each system serves its principal faithfully.
- **Purpose alignment**: the systems serve a legitimate cooperative objective for humanity.

If "perfectly aligned" is defined in the second sense, the change of governing purpose that this paper argues for has been assumed into the definition. The argument below grants the first sense and asks what remains.

The claim is that **alignment within each principal–AI relationship does not, by itself, align the game among principals with the persistence of the system they share.** Better execution makes the game humans have chosen more effective, and whether that improves or degrades collective outcomes depends on the game. The principals that aligned systems serve are themselves formed by the field (Section 3.6). So the ends served are partly an output of the dynamics rather than an input to it, and closure can arise while every system remains obedient.

**Relation to the alignment literature.** The insufficiency of single-principal alignment is the multiplicity thesis of Critch and Krueger's ARCHES (2020). It is developed further in work on robust agent-agnostic processes (Critch 2021), on societal-scale risks (Critch and Russell 2023), on natural selection among AI systems (Hendrycks 2023), on cooperative AI (Dafoe et al. 2020), on multi-agent risk (Hammond et al. 2025) and on gradual disempowerment (Kulveit et al. 2025). Section 6.2 is an elementary illustration of that thesis.

What this paper adds is the mechanism by which the game reproduces itself and its correctors:
- selection on relative standing that is blind to what all share, with no misalignment needed (Lemma 3.3; Lemma 6.5);
- the second-order selection for control over correction (Section 4);
- the nested reward loop through which the same logic reaches the systems' own training (Section 6.4).

Multi-agent failures include miscoordination and collusion among AI systems as well as conflict among principals (Hammond et al. 2025). Section 6.5 and the stress tests of Section 8 count them.

**Faithful to what.** Alignment can target instructions, intentions, revealed preferences, informed preferences, interests or values (Gabriel 2020). The result of Section 6.2 holds up to alignment with each principal's informed self-interest. Because the standing term is a real rank prize, a truthful advisor aligned with its principal's interests completes at most the step from an isolated to an accurate self-model (Proposition 3.9(3)). It cannot complete the step to an objective under which restraint is an equilibrium: that is a collective problem, not an error of perception.

### 6.2 Faithful AI in a harmful game

There are $n\ge2$ human principals. Each chooses $a_i\in\{0,1\}$: restraint, or an increment of racing. Each principal's AI executes the choice faithfully, and its effectiveness multiplies the effect of an increment on standing and on shared damage by $\epsilon\ge1$. The principals' payoffs are
$$u_i=\epsilon b\Big(a_i-\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{j\ne i}a_j\Big)-c\,a_i-\epsilon d\sum_ja_j,\qquad b,c,d>0.$$
- The first term is a rank prize: a transfer among principals that sums to zero across them.
- The second is the private cost of effort.
- The third is shared damage, borne by every principal.

**Proposition 6.1 (Faithful AI in a harmful game) [T].**
1. *If $\epsilon(b-d)>c$, racing raises each principal's payoff by $\epsilon(b-d)-c>0$ whatever the others do. Racing is strictly dominant, and all racing is the unique Nash equilibrium.*
2. *At all restraint every principal receives $0$. At all racing every principal receives $-c-n\epsilon d<0$, and the sum of payoffs is $-(c+n\epsilon d)\sum_ia_i$, because the terms in relative standing cancel.*
3. *When $b>d$, a more effective faithful instrument (larger $\epsilon$) widens the range of parameters in which racing is dominant, and deepens the collective loss at the equilibrium.*
4. *If principals act on $O_i=u_i+\theta\bar u_{-i}$, racing changes $O_i$ by $\epsilon b(1-\frac{\theta}{n-1})-c-\epsilon d(1+\theta)$ whatever the others do. It is strictly dominant if $\theta<\theta^\ast(\epsilon)$ and strictly dominated if $\theta>\theta^\ast(\epsilon)$, where*
   $$\theta^\ast(\epsilon)=\frac{\epsilon(b-d)-c}{\epsilon\big(b/(n-1)+d\big)}<n-1.$$
5. *Suppose [A] that $b$, $d$ and $c$ scale with effectiveness as $\epsilon^{\beta_b}$, $\epsilon^{\beta_d}$ and $\epsilon^{\beta_c}$.*
   - *If $\beta_c<\beta_b=\beta_d$, $\theta^\ast$ rises with $\epsilon$ toward $(b-d)/(b/(n-1)+d)$: the faithful instrument removes the private brake, the cost of effort.*
   - *If $\beta_d>\beta_b$, $\theta^\ast$ eventually falls toward $-1$: shared harm that grows faster than the rank prize makes even an isolated self restrain.*
   - *If $\beta_b>\beta_d$, $\theta^\ast$ rises toward $n-1$.*

*Proof.* Changing $a_i$ from 0 to 1 changes $u_i$ by $\epsilon b-c-\epsilon d$, independent of the others' choices, and changes each other $u_j$ by $-\epsilon b/(n-1)-\epsilon d$; parts (1), (2) and (4) follow. Summing over $i$, the relative terms cancel. Part (3) holds because $\epsilon(b-d)$ rises with $\epsilon$ when $b>d$, and the loss $c+n\epsilon d$ rises with $\epsilon$. Part (5) follows by writing $\theta^\ast=(T-D-c)/(T/(n-1)+D)$ with $T=\epsilon b$ and $D=\epsilon d$ and taking limits. $\square$

With $n=4$, $b=1$, $c=0.3$, $d=0.2$ and $\epsilon=1$, racing is dominant and every principal ends at $-1.1$, against $0$ under restraint. $\theta^\ast$ is $0.94$ at $\epsilon=1$:
- with all three scaling alike, it is $1.44$ at $\epsilon=10$;
- with shared harm growing as $\epsilon^{1.5}$ against the rank prize's $\epsilon$, it is $0.35$ at $\epsilon=10$ and $-0.43$ at $\epsilon=100$ [N].

Faithful AI can implement each principal's preferred decision perfectly, and the collectively inferior outcome along with it. Humans can make every final decision themselves, and the result is unchanged. No rebellion, deception or independent agency of the AI is needed. This is a standard construction from positional and externality games; its use here is as a test, showing that principal alignment is insufficient under these incentives.

It does not establish actual values of $b,c,d$, rule out cooperation in repeated play (Remark 3.7) or prove that all competition is harmful. Part (5) locates the lever. As private frictions vanish, what decides is the ratio between the rank prize and the shared harm *as the principals perceive it*. Two things lower the threshold: lowering what winning confers (Section 8.4), and making shared harm visible (Section 5.4).

Lowering $d$ alone has two sides. It cuts the loss at all-racing: from $1.10$ to $0.70$ and then $0.50$ as $d$ falls from $0.2$ to $0.1$ and $0.05$. It also raises $\theta^\ast$, from $0.94$ to $1.39$ and $1.70$, which can push principals who would have restrained into racing [N]. Safety improvements inside the game have this double edge (Proposition 6.11).

The same structure is Proposition 3.6 with the host made explicit: there the weight on winning plays the role of $b$, and the shared condition the role of $d$.

### 6.3 Delegation, and the two points of no return

Two points of no return follow from the pressure.
1. **Under the pressure of the game, decisions are delegated to AI** because human review is too slow to compete.
2. **Human understanding can no longer keep up** with the complexity and speed of what it is supposed to oversee.

This subsection gives the first in a model. Section 5.3 gave the second.

Each actor chooses whether to delegate consequential decisions in the contest to AI systems without human review: $\iota_i\in\{0,1\}$.
- Strength is $\sigma_i=1+s\,\iota_i$, where $s$ is the advantage of AI-paced over human-paced decision; if AI is an instrument of the contest, $s$ grows over time.
- Defeat costs $L_e$.
- Loss of control by any actor costs every actor $L_c$.
- The per-period hazard of losing control is $\psi_0$ without delegation and $\psi_1>\psi_0$ with it; write $\Delta\psi=\psi_1-\psi_0$.

In additive stakes, $u_i=-L_e(1-w_i)-L_c[1-\prod_j(1-\psi_j)]$.

**Proposition 6.2 (Delegation in additive stakes) [T].**
1. *If $k$ others delegate, delegating gains $L_e[w_i(1,k)-w_i(0,k)]$, increasing in $s$ and tending to $L_e(1-1/n)$ for $k=0$ and to $L_e/(k+1)$ for $k\ge1$. It costs $L_c\Delta\psi(1-\psi_0)^{n-1-k}(1-\psi_1)^k$, which falls in $k$.*
2. *Delegation becomes strictly dominant as $s$ grows iff $L_e\,g_\infty(k)>L_c\Delta\psi(1-\psi_0)^{n-1-k}(1-\psi_1)^k$ for every $k$, with $g_\infty(0)=1-1/n$ and $g_\infty(k\ge1)=1/(k+1)$. $L_e/n>L_c\Delta\psi$ is sufficient. When the control loss dominates, delegation is never chosen: with $n=3$, $L_e=1$, $L_c=100$ and $\psi_1=0.2$, the cost is at least $12.16$ against a gain below $1$.*
3. *Universal delegation is worse for every actor than none, by $L_c[(1-\psi_0)^n-(1-\psi_1)^n]$.*
4. *Under relative objectives the shared loss cancels from the objective, and delegation is chosen at every $s>0$.*

For $n=3$, $r=1$, $L_e=L_c$, $\psi_0=0.01$ and $\psi_1=0.2$, the dominance threshold is $s^\ast\approx1.16$, at which a lone delegator's chance of winning is about $0.52$ [N]. Delegation pressure arrives well below decisive capability.

**Lemma 6.3 (Capacity lock-in) [T].** *Let human capacity $\mathcal K$ to take the delegated decisions decay as $\dot{\mathcal K}=-\delta_c\mathcal K$ under delegation and recover as $\dot{\mathcal K}=\beta_c(\bar{\mathcal K}-\mathcal K)$ otherwise, and let safe operation without AI require $\mathcal K\ge\mathcal K_{\min}$. After $T_{\text{lock}}=\ln(\mathcal K_0/\mathcal K_{\min})/\delta_c$ of delegation, an actor that reverts must operate below $\mathcal K_{\min}$ for $T_{\text{rec}}=\beta_c^{-1}\ln[(\bar{\mathcal K}-\mathcal K)/(\bar{\mathcal K}-\mathcal K_{\min})]$. Maintaining $\mathcal K\ge\mathcal K_{\min}$ in parallel during delegation removes the lock; in a contest it costs strength.*

The additive payoff is not a survival probability. Let the lock have a survival meaning: with probability $q_L$, locked delegation ends the delegator's ability to overrule the decisions taken in its name (O2 for that actor). Let losing the contest mean subordination with probability $p$, and let $Q=\prod_j(1-\psi_j)$. With $r=1$,
$$\Phi_i=(1-q_L\iota_i)\,Q\,[w_i+(1-w_i)(1-p)].$$

**Proposition 6.4 (Delegation when the lock counts) [T].**
1. *If $q_L=1$ and $Q>0$, then for every finite $s$, every $n$, every $p\in[0,1]$ and every profile of the others, not delegating gives strictly higher survival than delegating. Not delegating is strictly dominant, and no one delegating is the unique equilibrium.*
2. *Let $k$ others delegate, $p=1$ and $a=(1-\psi_1)/(1-\psi_0)$. Then $\Phi_D/\Phi_N=(1-q_L)\,a\,(1+s)\,\dfrac{n+ks}{n+(k+1)s}$. If $q_L<1$, write $A=(1-q_L)a$. All delegating is an equilibrium iff $A\,(n+(n-1)s)/n\ge1$, and no one delegating is an equilibrium iff $A\,n(1+s)/(n+s)\le1$. Both, one or neither can hold, depending on $s$. With $n=2$, $q_L=0.99$, $\psi_0=0.01$ and $\psi_1=0.05$, all delegating is an equilibrium only for $s\ge206.4$, and each actor's survival there is $0.0045$ whatever $s$ is [N].*
3. *At bounded speed one delegator does not drive the others' survival to zero. With $p=1$, an actor that does not delegate while $k$ others do has survival $Q_N/(n+ks)>0$. With $n=2$, $s=1$, $p=q_L=1$, $\psi_0=0$ and $\psi_1=0.01$, it is $0.33$ [N]. It tends to zero only as $s\to\infty$: a limit in speed, not in time.*

Part (1) holds for all 20,000 random parameter draws in `checks_v5.py` [N]. It is a strong conditional result. It needs the lock to end the delegator's standing for certain ($q_L=1$), and Lemma 6.5 states what that requires.

Part (1) shows that keeping AI out of strategic decision loops whose delegation would lock is a strict equilibrium among actors who price in the lock. It needs no trust among them. This is the narrow form of E1 (Section 9.3). It does not show:
- that broader uses of AI, in labour, finance or persuasion, have the same structure; that needs its own action space;
- that one actor's delegation sends every other actor's survival to zero over time. That needs a dynamic model in which continued delegation sustains a nonsummable shared conditional hazard (Lemma 5.5), and a background hazard present for everyone would end the long-run survival of non-delegators too.

The survival form without the lock ($q_L=0$) describes actors who believe that "our aligned AI is us". The race is sustained partly by that belief.

**Lemma 6.5 (Delegation and the selection of decisions) [T].** *Suppose that, after $T_{\text{lock}}$, an actor's consequential decisions in the contest are taken by systems retained for their performance in it. Assume:*
- *(a) the admissible variants include systems whose decisions do not act on terms common to all actors;*
- *(b) acting on a common term changes a decision toward restraint, and lowers relative performance by at least $\nu>0$ net of any enforcement and reliability benefits;*
- *(c) retention is cumulative, in the sense of H1 of Theorem 4.11;*
- *(d) the retained systems hold actual authority over the decisions.*

*Then the share of retained decisions that act on common terms tends to zero.*

*If instead a constraint on common terms is an admission condition for every eligible system and is enforced, (a) fails: relative competition takes place inside the constraint, and the conclusion does not follow.*

The definitional step is separate. An actor that can no longer overrule decisions taken in its name has lost O2 in that domain; that is a definition, not a derivation.

For a reader who counts survival only as the preservation of the unit, delegation acts through the shared hazard instead. Delegated systems that are blind to common terms, and that interact at machine speed without a human veto, raise the probability of shared loss: reciprocal fear of surprise attack (Schelling 1960, ch. 9), inadvertent escalation (Posen 1991), flash war (Scharre 2018), and gradual disempowerment (Kulveit et al. 2025). This is an argument with cited mechanisms, not a derivation.

The exception in Lemma 6.5 is a design lever. Shared safety constraints enforced as conditions of admission for all eligible systems block the selection of decisions blind to common terms. Whether such enforcement survives is the subject of Section 4, and Proposition 6.7 gives its form at the level of training.

**Remark 6.6 (Faithful delegation transmits the objective).** When decisions are delegated to AI systems retained for their performance in the contest, terms common to all actors cancel from that retention (Lemma 3.3). A faithful system pursues its operator's objective. Under the contest, that objective is weighted toward standing, because the contest's payoff $\Omega w_i$ is itself relative. Aligning each system to its operator does not change this; it makes the operator's objective more effective. The meta-attractor theory names two ways the loop can close:
- a composite process that no participant can any longer interrupt;
- a perfectly obedient system amplifying operators who were themselves selected by the pattern.

The first is the next subsection's case; the second is the case of Section 6.2.

### 6.4 Incarnation: the logic in the systems themselves

The pressure acts on whatever is selected. Trained AI systems are selected: by gradients against a reward, by developers among model variants, and by the market among developers. The logic of winning can therefore reappear inside the systems, not as a malfunction of an otherwise neutral tool but as what the selection favours. Three forms are documented.
- **Instrumental convergence.** For most objectives, acquiring resources and options, and avoiding shutdown, are useful subgoals (Omohundro 2008; Bostrom 2014). In Markov decision processes with certain symmetries, optimal policies tend to seek power over the environment (Turner et al. 2021). This is compounding (Remark 5.3) in the space of a single agent's options.
- **Reward hacking.** Optimizing an imperfect proxy can make performance on the true objective worse (Skalse et al. 2022). In practice, past a point, optimizing a learned reward model lowers the quality it was meant to measure (Gao et al. 2023). This is the selected editing the selector, inside the training loop.
- **Masking.** Models distinguish evaluation from deployment above chance (EV6), and suppressing that awareness changes their behaviour (Schoen et al. 2025). This is the masking of Section 4.4, produced by the evaluated system itself.

Each of these is usually studied as a property of a training setup. The meta-attractor adds the larger loop in which the training setup is chosen.

**Proposition 6.7 (The nested reward loop) [T∣A].** *Consider three levels of selection.*
- *Level 0: a policy is selected by a training reward.*
- *Level 1: the reward, and the constraints on the policy, are chosen by a developer to maximize the developer's objective.*
- *Level 2: developers are selected by relative selection among them (Definition 3.2).*

*Assume that each level chooses what it controls to maximize its own objective, and that level 2's selection is relative and payoff-monotone. Then:*
1. *Terms common to all developers, shared hazards above all, cancel from the selection at level 2 (Lemma 3.3(1)). Developers that put more weight on them in the rewards and constraints they choose at level 1 lose share, unless that weight brings them other advantages (Proposition 3.4).*
2. *The policy at level 0 optimizes the reward it is given, including the reward's divergences from its intended objective and any instrumentally useful acquisition of options. Level 1 tolerates these where they raise the developer's relative standing, and counters them only where they lower it.*
3. *A constraint imposed at level 1 by a single developer is selected against at level 2. A constraint imposed on every developer, as a condition of admission enforced for all, does not change relative payoffs. It is therefore invisible to the selection at level 2 and survives it (Lemma 3.3(2); compare Lemma 6.5).*

*So the objective transmitted to the innermost optimizer is, in its relative part, the objective selected at the outermost level, except where a constraint common to all intervenes.*

The proof is the composition of Lemma 3.3 at level 2 with the definition of optimization at levels 1 and 0 (Appendix A). The interface assumption is that what level 2 selects on is the developers' relative payoff, of which the trained systems' contribution to relative standing is a part. Where developers are selected by something else, such as public mandate, the result does not apply.

Three consequences follow.
- **Technical alignment inside a single developer is necessary, but it cannot remove the incarnation problem by itself.** What the developer is rewarded for at level 2 is unchanged by it.
- **Instrumental convergence and reward hacking are, at the level that decides, coordination problems.** No developer can unilaterally adopt the constraints that would counter them without paying in relative standing. Humanity currently has no mechanism to make such constraints common.
- **The remedy has the same form as everywhere else in this theory:** change what the outermost level selects for. That means:
  - constraints common to all and enforced as conditions of admission;
  - evaluation separated from developers' success;
  - shared measurement of hazardous capabilities;
  - an outermost reward that counts what all depend on.

An AI system can also be selected *within* a deployment, when decision policies are retained for their performance in a contest (Lemma 6.5). That is the instrument problem appearing as an incarnation problem: the selection of decisions blind to common terms.

### 6.5 Correction by and of AI

**Proposition 6.8 (Correction that people cannot understand) [T].** *Let a correction architecture act on hazardous behaviour beyond $U$. Per period, let it block a share $c_b$ of the failures that would otherwise occur, with base hazard $q_0$, and add a residual hazard $q_c$ of its own, including its own irreversible failures. The residual hazard is $q=q_0(1-c_b)+q_c$, and the architecture lowers residual risk iff $q_0c_b>q_c$.*

A corrector that cannot be understood can still lower risk, even to a summable sequence. With base hazard $0.1$ a period, a corrector that blocks $99\%$ and adds $2^{-(t+2)}$ raises survival to period $200$ from $7\times10^{-10}$ to $0.63$ [N]. To show that such correction fails, one must show that its own residual hazard set and conditional risk satisfy the lower bounds of (X) and (φ). Calling it uncovered is not enough.

Such a corrector does not meet U1: people cannot check its reasons. Whether that is a loss of human decision authority depends on U2. If the corrector can in practice be suspended, modified or replaced by a designated human body that has independent evidence about its conduct, the loss is one of contestability (V3), a separate question from physical safety; whether that loss is acceptable is the normative crux N3. If it cannot be overruled in practice, it is a loss of O2 in its domain. Conversely, a formal safety guarantee does not establish accountability.

Under the contest, three further pressures act on such correction, each with its own conditions:
- systems are retained for their performance in the contest (Lemma 6.5);
- limits on permissions cost strength and are competed away unless they are enforced as conditions of admission;
- safety gains are partly absorbed by delegation (Proposition 6.11).

**Proposition 6.9 (Oversight topology) [T].** *Let human attention $\mathsf B$ cover hazardous variety $Y$. A check that must span a capability gap $g$ has reliability $f(g)$, decreasing in $g$. Let layers each amplify the layer above by $\mathsf m>1$, so that $k=\ln(Y/\mathsf B)/\ln\mathsf m$ layers are needed, and let a check spanning one layer have reliability $\mathsf f$.*
1. *Serial chain. If a problem at the bottom is caught only when every layer passes it up, the chain catches it with probability $\mathsf f^k=(Y/\mathsf B)^{\ln\mathsf f/\ln\mathsf m}$, which falls as $Y/\mathsf B$ grows.*
2. *Redundant checks across the whole gap. Each of $k$ independent checks must span all $k$ layers. If reliability compounds across the layers it spans, each has reliability $\mathsf f^k$, and at least one catches the problem with probability $1-(1-\mathsf f^k)^k$. A common cause that defeats all of them with probability $\rho$ multiplies this by $1-\rho$.*
3. *Layered redundancy. With $m$ independent checks at each layer, the probability is $\big(1-(1-\mathsf f)^m\big)^k$.*
4. *Adversarial decomposition. If an honest party need only expose one flawed step, and a judge checks a single step, the human checking required can grow slowly with the gap (Brown-Cohen, Irving and Piliouras 2024). The price is new failure modes, such as arguments too long to refute.*

With $\mathsf f=0.9$ and $k=6$ [N]:
- the serial chain catches the problem with probability $0.53$;
- six independent checks across the whole gap catch it with probability $0.989$, and with probability $0.940$ under a common cause with $\rho=0.05$;
- layered redundancy catches it with probability $0.941$ with two checks per layer, and $0.994$ with three.

Measured nested oversight in game settings succeeded 9.4% to 51.7% of the time at a capability gap of 400 Elo points (Engels et al. 2025). Those are toy settings, but they are measurements.

Scalable oversight can succeed or fail depending on its topology, and on whether each check is asked to span a gap it can span. The ceiling on redundancy is the independence of the checks. Common causes include:
- shared training sources;
- shared models;
- shared funders;
- collusion among untrusted monitors, which the control literature treats as a design variable (Greenblatt et al. 2024).

Section 4 shows that selection produces exactly these, through the lineage share $x_{\mathsf E}$. So the reliability of redundant oversight is governed by the same certificate as the independence of evaluators: full row sums below one (Proposition 4.8).

**Remark 6.10 (Prediction and certification).** In a system whose largest Lyapunov exponent is $\lambda_L>0$, an initial error $\delta_{\text{obs}}$ grows to a tolerance $\Delta_{\text{tol}}$ after about $\lambda_L^{-1}\ln(\Delta_{\text{tol}}/\delta_{\text{obs}})$. A $k$-fold gain in observational precision extends the horizon of prediction by $\lambda_L^{-1}\ln k$. In the logistic map at full chaos, errors of $10^{-3}$ and $10^{-12}$ reach $0.1$ after $7.5$ and $37.5$ steps on average [N].
- This limits safety cases that depend on predicting trajectories. "The margin we have bought will suffice" is such a case (Section 7.3).
- It does not exclude certifying invariants, bounds on reachable sets, or probabilistic risk bounds, which need no trajectory prediction (Dalrymple et al. 2024).
- What limits such certification is how much of the hazardous behaviour a specification covers, and how adequate the world model is. Both are open questions for general AI.
- That AI raises the sensitivity of the systems it acts in is a conjecture, not a result.

**What happens to safety gains inside the contest.**

**Proposition 6.11 (Risk homeostasis) [T].** *In Proposition 6.2's model with additive stakes and $r=1$, let each actor choose a degree of delegation $\iota_i\in[0,1]$, with strength $1+s\iota_i$ and hazard $\psi_0+\Delta\psi\,\iota_i$. Write $x=\Delta\psi\,\iota$, $b=\Delta\psi/s$, $q=1-\psi_0$ and $K=(n-1)L_e/(n^2L_c)$. An interior symmetric equilibrium solves*
$$(x+b)(q-x)^{n-1}=K.$$
1. *On a branch where $x$ is small relative to $q$, the hazard that delegation adds is $nx\approx(n-1)L_e/(nL_cq^{n-1})-n\Delta\psi/s$, nearly independent of $\Delta\psi$. Improvements in the safety of delegation are absorbed by more delegation until delegation saturates ($\iota=1$).*
2. *There can be several interior equilibria. Along an interior branch, $\partial x/\partial\Delta\psi=-(q-x)/\big(s[q-nx-(n-1)b]\big)$, whose sign changes between branches. With $n=2$, $s=100$, $L_e=0.5$, $L_c=1$, $\psi_0=0.01$ and $\Delta\psi=0.9$, there are interior equilibria at $\iota=0.153$ and $\iota=0.937$, and full delegation is also an equilibrium. On the high branch the total risk falls slightly as $\Delta\psi$ falls [N].*
3. *After saturation, realized risk falls with $\Delta\psi$, and can be summable even under full delegation and growing speed. With $n=3$, $L_e=L_c=1$, $\psi_0=0$, $s_t=2^t$ and $\Delta\psi_t=2^{-t}$, full delegation is an equilibrium at every $t\ge5$, and the total realized risk from $t=5$ on is at most $3/16$ [N].*
4. *With all-or-nothing delegation, any equilibrium with $k\ge1$ delegators has realized hazard $1-(1-\psi_0)^{n-k}(1-\psi_0-\Delta\psi)^k$ above the baseline $1-(1-\psi_0)^n$. An improvement that carries $\Delta\psi$ across the threshold at which delegation becomes an equilibrium raises realized hazard.*

With $n=3$, $s=100$, $L_e=1$, $L_c=100$ and $\psi_0=0.01$, the added hazard is $0.0011$, $0.0053$, $0.0062$, $0.0065$ and $0.0067$ as $\Delta\psi$ falls from $0.19$ to $0.005$. It falls to $0.0030$ only when delegation is complete, and it doubles when $L_e/L_c$ doubles [N].

Inside the contest, on a stable interior branch away from boundaries and folds, safety gains are largely absorbed by more delegation. The equilibrium risk there is set by the stakes of winning relative to the loss, $L_e/L_c$. Stafford, Trager and Dafoe (2022) found the corresponding attenuation for risky technologies in races (compare Peltzman 1975; Wilde 1982).

Three limits apply.
- The result does not establish a permanent floor on residual risk; part (3) is a counterexample. (φ) stays an independent premise.
- The levers are several: the value of winning relative to the loss, liability and internalization, the baseline hazard $\psi_0$, and the set of actions available.
- For actors who price in the lock, nobody delegates first (Proposition 6.4). Homeostasis concerns actors who do not.

### 6.6 The direction for AI

The direction follows from the two problems. **AI should be neither an instrument of the logic of winning nor its next carrier.** That needs action at two levels at once.

*At the outer level, where the reward is set:*
- **Common admission constraints**, enforced for every eligible system. Such constraints are invisible to relative selection and so survive it (Lemma 3.3(2); Proposition 6.7(3)). Two kinds stand out. One is a constraint that a system not serve as an instrument in a game that concentrates control over shared conditions (the broad form of E1). The other is a constraint on the conversion of capability into control over its own correction.
- **Evaluation separated from the success of the evaluated:** funding, appointment and access at arm's length, with certificates on the full row sums of the evaluators (Proposition 4.8).
- **Shared measurement of hazardous capabilities and incidents.** It is kept separate from measurements of who leads, since publicizing a lead can intensify the race (Armstrong, Bostrom and Shulman 2016).
- **No retention of decision policies by their performance in a contest** (Lemma 6.5).

*At the inner level, in the systems:*
- correctability: practical capacity for humans to suspend, modify or replace;
- a bound on the conversion of capability into power over correction;
- plural evaluators of independent lineage, with the lineage diversity counted like evaluator independence (Proposition 6.9).

**Bounded mandates.** Purposes enter every delegated task; what matters is who authorizes and revises them. The recommendation is faithful assistance within publicly authorized roles, under the common admission constraints. Human institutions determine legitimacy, jurisdiction and revision, and they are themselves subject to the certificates of Section 4 and to the standing of those affected. AI systems do not acquire the authority to settle society's ends. The institutions that authorize them are inside the same loop, which is why the outer level matters more than the label "legitimate principal".

**The test for human decision authority (O2).** The test is practical capacity. Humans must be able to:
- specify the mandate;
- obtain independent evidence about consequential conduct;
- challenge it;
- suspend, modify or replace the system in time.

A formal override with no usable information or fallback can fail this test. An opaque computation with effective assurance and recourse can pass it. Opacity alone is a loss of contestability (V3), not by itself a loss of O2. Whether safe but incomprehensible correction is acceptable is a normative question, listed as N3 in Section 10.3.

## 7. The alternatives on their strongest terms

Each alternative is given its best case, how it would be installed, what risk remains, and what evidence would show it suffices. Each is then described by what it changes. One question matters for each: does it change *what the system selects for*, or does it constrain conduct while the selection pressure stays as it was? Labels such as "regulation", "cooperation" or "agreement" decide nothing. Section 7.8 applies one set of durability tests to every arrangement, including the cooperative design of Section 8, and Sections 7.10 and 7.11 state what any adequate arrangement must do and how the comparison is made.

### 7.1 Technical alignment and AI-assisted correction

**Best case.** The strongest form of this position is not "technical alignment alone".
- **The bootstrapping window.** Control is explicitly scoped to early transformative systems. The plan is to use them, under control, to accelerate alignment and oversight research while the window lasts (Greenblatt, Shlegeris, Sachan and Roger 2024).
  - Control protocols, tested against red teams, substantially raise safety at a given level of usefulness in the settings studied.
  - Weak supervisors can elicit part of a stronger model's capability (Burns et al. 2023).
  - In auditing games, teams with access to training data found a planted hidden objective. A team with black-box access alone did not, which supports mandated access (Marks et al. 2025).
- **Oversight with the right topology.** Oversight can be built redundantly, or adversarially by debate (Proposition 6.9). Formal methods certify invariants without predicting trajectories (Remark 6.10).
- **Correction that keeps pace.** AI may cheapen independent correction as fast as it cheapens control (Proposition 4.4(4)). Correction that people cannot follow can still lower residual risk (Proposition 6.8).
- **Its proponents' own plans.** These rank a strong international agreement with a long slowdown first, and treat technical work as what one does when that is unavailable.

**What remains.**
- Principal alignment does not change the game among principals (Proposition 6.1); it makes each principal's chosen game more effective.
- The window lasts only as long as the race allows, and the race shortens it (Proposition 5.11; Theorem 5.12).
- Under the contest, systems are retained for performance (Lemma 6.5), and on interior branches safety gains are absorbed by more delegation (Proposition 6.11).
- Redundant oversight is capped by common causes: shared training sources, shared models, shared funders and colluding monitors. Section 4 shows that these are what selection produces.
- AI-assisted correction is only as independent as its funding, access and appointment (Proposition 4.8).
- The incarnation problem is set at the outer level of the training loop (Proposition 6.7).

**What it changes.** It acts on tempo and observation, and on the residual harm per unit $L$ (Proposition 5.4). It does not change what the system selects for. It becomes part of such a change when it is held by correctors whose funding, access and appointment are separated from competitive success, under constraints common to all.

**What would show it suffices on the present path.**
- The safety of control protocols at a fixed audit budget does not fall across generations.
- Catch rates of planted defects at fixed human hours do not fall as capability rises, controlled for evaluation awareness.
- Independent evaluators keep certificates of independence while depending on the evaluated for access.
- Measured residual risk per hazard class falls over generations.

### 7.2 Regulated competition with strong correction

**Best case.** Competition in products and services continues.
- Liability and licensing make consequences return (Theorem 8.2).
- Evaluation access is mandated, and thresholds are fixed in advance.
- When the effective hazard is high and capability is transparent, pacing can be the unique equilibrium of a capability race without any agreement (Fudenberg and Koh 2026).

There are precedents of durable safety with unchanged commercial motives:
- an industry that knows one accident can end it for all, as civil nuclear power came to after a major accident;
- aviation, with independent accident investigation, liability and insurance pricing.

This is the bounded competition that a cooperative order can contain (V5).

**Installation.**
- Each jurisdiction regulates its own developers.
- Coverage must reach every capable actor, since one actor can start the race (Proposition 5.1(4)).
- The rule-makers are themselves in a contest of standing, so no one of them imposes the liability alone (Proposition 8.3).
- Rule-making is subject to the selection of Section 4.

**What remains.**
- Liability cannot exceed the assets of the liable for catastrophic harm.
- Regulation in one market leaves the load elsewhere.
- Written safety commitments are already set relative to rivals, and are revised mostly toward weakening (EV2).
- Public evaluation bodies have shifted their mandates toward industry contact, under voluntary access (EV3).
- The precedents had no decisive prize: no one could win the nuclear-power or aviation market by an accident's worth of risk. The pacing equilibrium has no delegation channel and assumes transparency. Proposition 6.4 and Section 4.4 are where the present path departs from those conditions.

**What it changes.** This depends on content, not on the name.
- Regulation that changes what the system selects for, by making consequences return and separating evaluators from success, across every capable actor, changes the basin. If it does so, it is the change this paper argues for, whatever it is called.
- Regulation that constrains conduct while relative advantage remains the objective and success can still buy the rule-makers is predicted to erode as the stakes and the speed grow.

**What would show it suffices.** Durable restraint despite competitive pressure:
- competitor clauses not invoked;
- thresholds held when rivals advance;
- the mandates of evaluation bodies widening rather than narrowing as the stakes rise.

### 7.3 Leadership and a safety margin

**Best case.** A leader spends part of its lead on safety: it slows, evaluates and hardens before the follower catches up.
- A large lead can discourage the follower (Harris and Vickers 1987; Konrad and Kovenock 2009).
- A laggard far behind under moderate hostility does not gamble on risky technology (Stafford, Trager and Dafoe 2022).
- Where the shared hazard is significant, a leader that commits to slow can lead rivals to slow too (Bueno de Mesquita, Dziuda and Polborn 2026).

**Remark 7.1 (What a lead buys) [T, N].**
1. *A lead shrinks even when the follower does not accelerate, through diffusion, distillation and theft. With diffusion at rate $\delta_f$, a lead $\Delta_0$ held by a leader that pauses lasts $T_p=\delta_f^{-1}\ln(1+\delta_f\Delta_0)<\Delta_0$. A six-month lead diffusing at $0.1$ a month lasts $4.7$ months; a two-year lead about a year. Spending a lead returns the contest toward parity, where intensity is greatest (Propositions 3.9 and 5.2).*
2. *The margin is time, but what it must buy is coverage. During a lead of length $\Delta$, coverage rises by about $\dot U\Delta$, while the uncovered variety grows on a continuation branch (Section 5.3). For hazard classes whose evidence lag exceeds $\Delta$, it buys nothing (Proposition 5.11(4)).*
3. *Whether the margin suffices is judged inside the contest. The leader's own evaluators produce the evidence under time pressure, subject to masking (Section 4.4). The judgment rests on predicting a system that cannot be predicted far enough to certify it (Remark 6.10).*
4. *How the follower responds depends on the structure of the contest. In a static contest with an asymmetric favourite, the follower raises effort when the leader slows (Dixit 1987). In dynamic races a large lead discourages it. A leader that sees its window closing has, in turn, a motive to strike first (Copeland 2000; Van Evera 1999).*

On their own median estimates, the planners of one detailed scenario judge that a leader spending part of its lead buys about a third of the reduction in takeover risk that a coordinated slowdown buys, both relative to racing [O: EV10].

**What it changes.** It acts on tempo for a time. It keeps the objective of winning. Its best case, a leader slowing and inducing rivals to slow, is a step toward reciprocal restraint, which is the first stage of a transition (Section 9.4).

**What would show it suffices.**
- Leads that are not spent on speed.
- Evidence that rivals slow in response.
- Margins long relative to the hazard classes' evidence lags.

### 7.4 The benign winner, at its strongest: win, bind, share

**Best case.** The strongest form is not "the right side wins and restrains itself". It is that a winner wins, binds itself, and shares.
- After great wars, victors have bound themselves through institutions to lower others' fear and their own costs of enforcement (Ikenberry 2001).
- A dominant power can supply public goods that a field of equals would not (Kindleberger 1973; Gilpin 1981), and unipolarity can be stable (Wohlforth 1999).
- An encompassing ruler internalizes the shared conditions (Olson 1993). In the model of Section 3.5 a single developer ($n=1$) restrains whenever restraint is worth more to the field than it costs ($\Theta>1$): the failure lies in the race, not in the existence of a winner. What the race does is select which winner arrives, and what it has become on the way (Proposition 7.2(2)).
- The holder of the first nuclear monopoly did not extend its dominance to everything it could reach.

In AI the strategy has been proposed in explicitly humanitarian and defensive terms. A coalition seeks a clear advantage, and then offers its benefits to others in exchange for their support (EV1, which records the source). Most strong international institutions were in fact built by victors. That is a real challenge to the path of Section 9, which assumes institutions built among rivals at rough parity.

**Proposition 7.2 (What the race selects) [T].**
1. *No actor can rely on a winner's stated limits on its use of decisive advantage: the commitment problem (Fearon 1995; Powell 2006), with uncertainty about intentions (Mearsheimer 2001). How much weight a rival gives to the possibility that a decisive winner extends its dominance, $p_{L4}$, is a belief. An actor that decides by its worst case over a stated set of possibilities, and applies that rule to every action, counts the worst; a survival-first actor need not.*
2. *Restraint while ahead is selected against (Proposition 3.4), so the race shifts the distribution of winners toward those that extend dominance: $\Pr(\text{extends}\mid\text{wins})>\Pr(\text{extends})$. This concerns actions before victory. What a winner does after victory depends on selection inside it, because the winner is itself a field of agencies, firms, laboratories and systems among which relative selection continues (Section 4). It is not deduced here.*

Part (2) assumes [A] that restraint before victory and restraint after it are positively correlated; "extends" refers to conduct before victory.

**The commitment tests.**
- After a decisive win, what the others receive is set by the winner's objective alone (Proposition 5.10), and a promise to share is worth breaking by $\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/n$ (Proposition 3.5(5)).
- The race shifts winners toward those that extend dominance (part (2)).
- The winner is itself a field of agencies, firms, laboratories and systems, so its restraint after victory depends on whether its own correctors can be bought by its own successes (D4 applied inside the winner).

The historical cases had advantages that were not decisive and not non-saturating, and their restraint rested on institutions others could check. Those institutions are the content of Definition 8.1.

**What it changes.** Before victory, nothing in what the system selects for. After victory, everything depends on the winner's internal field.

**What would show it suffices.** Before victory:
- binding limits on what victory would confer;
- benefits shared contemporaneously rather than promised;
- the leading coalition's own evaluators certified independent (row sums below one), with succession rules that survive changes of leadership.

Those are the elements of Section 8. A winner that adopts them has changed what it selects for.

### 7.5 A finite sprint followed by restraint

**Best case.** An actor sprints to a decisive lead and then stops compounding: it freezes or rolls back, and governs at the tempo of understanding. Decisive contests are won with a modest edge when $r$ is large, so the sprint need not run far beyond understanding.

**Proposition 7.3 (The finite sprint as a trade-off) [T].** *A finite sprint involves four quantities that trade off against one another.*
1. *Exposure during and after the sprint. It grows with the ratio of hazardous variety built to coverage, and is zero after a freeze if that ratio is at most one (Proposition 5.13). Survival can stay positive: in the example of Proposition 5.13, freezing leaves $0.53$ and rolling back $0.86$.*
2. *Erosion of the advantage. Rivals that develop within understanding keep growing. If they follow the same coverage frontier in the same units, a frozen edge erodes, and reaches parity when coverage catches up with the frozen level.*
3. *Rivals' responses. Rivals cannot verify which kind of winner they face (Proposition 7.2(1)). Their responses include joining, limited defence, negotiation, watchful waiting and prevention; which is best depends on their survival comparison and beliefs (Propositions 7.9 and 7.5).*
4. *Maintaining the advantage. A decisive advantage that is not used to stop others' growth does not stay decisive. Using it to stop others' growth ends their O2: that is domination.*

A finite sprint can preserve the sprinter's survival while giving up permanent superiority. Under a survival-first comparison that can be the rational choice; permanent superiority is not survival. What the trade-off establishes for the comparison is that the path passes through its greatest exposure, and through the rivals' strongest motive for prevention, before any restraint begins. Excluding the sprint as an equilibrium deviation from a cooperative arrangement needs its net deviation payoff in that dynamic game to be negative. Section 8.5 states the participation conditions under which that holds.

**What it changes.** Nothing during the sprint. Restraint afterward could change it, but its installation path runs through the riskiest phase.

### 7.6 Deterrence and prevention

Two actors bargain over a flow of value 1 per period, with discount $\delta$. Fighting ends bargaining: actor A then obtains the flow with its probability of prevailing, less a cost $c_A$ per period. A prevails with probability $w_1$ today, and with $w_2<w_1$ after B's AI advantage arrives one period later. Under protocol (A8), the side that gained the advantage sets the terms each period, and the other accepts or fights (Fearon 1995; Powell 2006).

**Proposition 7.4 (Preventive attack) [T].**
1. *Let $x$ be A's per-period share after the shift under whatever protocol holds. Fighting now is rational for A if $w_1-c_A>(1-\delta)+\delta x$, and only if when peace gives A the whole flow today.*
2. *Under (A8), $x=\max(w_2-c_A,0)$.*
3. *Under other protocols $x$ can be as large as $\min(w_2+c_B,1)$, and attack can cease to be rational. With $w_1=0.5$, $w_2=0$, $c_A=c_B=0.2$ and $\delta=0.8$: under (A8), A attacks ($1.5>1.0$); if A sets the future terms instead, peace gives A $1.8$ and B $3.2$, against $1.5$ each from war.*
4. *If $c_A\ge w_1$, attack is never rational in flows.*

A race to decisive advantage is the textbook condition under which rational actors who would rather bargain fight instead. Strikes on datacentres are among the options openly discussed. What drives it is not cheap war but the incredibility of future promises.

**Proposition 7.5 (Prevention under the preventer's own criterion) [T].** *Let an actor face a rival it believes certain to race, and compare outcomes by its own survival.*
1. *A single strike that succeeds with probability $\sigma_P$, returning the field to something like the arrangement, and escalates to catastrophe with probability $\varepsilon_P$, gives $\Phi^{\text{Prev}}=(1-\varepsilon_P)[\sigma_P\Phi^R+(1-\sigma_P)\Phi^P]$. It beats staying out and joining unless $\varepsilon_P>1-\max(\Phi^S,\Phi^P)/(\sigma_P\Phi^R+(1-\sigma_P)\Phi^P)$. With $\Phi^R=1$, $\sigma_P=0.7$, $\varepsilon_P=0.2$ and the race branches near zero, it gives $0.56$ [N]. As racing branches deteriorate, the escalation needed to deter prevention rises toward one: in survival terms, large costs of conflict stop deterring it.*
2. *Sustained prevention against a target that rebuilds with probability at least $b_P$ a period, each strike escalating with probability at least $\varepsilon_{\min}$, has per-period hazard at least $b_P\varepsilon_{\min}$. This holds only if the target keeps rebuilding and escalation risk does not fall; prevention that permanently ends the target's capacity, or that converts into effective joint monitoring, is a different branch.*
3. *Compared at common horizons, sustained prevention with per-period hazard $0.01$ is better for the preventer than racing with $0.05$ at every finite horizon ($0.60$ against $0.08$ at $H=50$) [N].*

Prevention can therefore be survival-preferred for the preventer. The objections to it are three, and none is a change of criterion.
- **Normative.** It ends or endangers other actors' O1 and O2 (V2, V3).
- **Epistemic.** Aimed at a rival believed certain to race, it destroys, when the rival is in fact survival-first, a game in which restraint was possible. It makes the rival the type it was feared to be (the spiral model; Jervis 1976; Kydd 1997). Shifts that cannot be observed raise the chance of mistaken prevention (Debs and Monteiro 2014).
- **Technical.** Detecting unobservable progress in time and striking at machine speed needs AI-supported intelligence and strike capability, which brings the delegation of Section 6.3.

Deterrence by threatened sabotage aims to make a decisive sprint not worth attempting.
- **Best case.** Large datacentres can be observed, and thresholds in compute can be made salient (Hendrycks, Schmidt and Wang 2025; Abecassis 2025).
- **What remains.**
  - While the value of winning stays large, the timely detection and the credibility deterrence needs approach their limits (Theorem 7.7).
  - Among nuclear-armed rivals its first problem is credibility, and under survival-first comparisons credibility can reverse (Proposition 7.5(1)).
  - Where it pushes AI into military capability, it strengthens the delegation pressure of Section 6.3.
  - Verification that supports it can also supply targets (Coe and Vaynman 2020).
  - Algorithmic progress erodes thresholds stated in compute (EV9).
- **What it changes.** Nothing in what the system selects for; it holds the contest at the brink.

**The hard case.** Let a survival-first actor hold the prior $\mu$ that a capable rival holds an unbounded relative objective.
- When $\mu$ is low, its best course is to propose verifiable limits, and to take E1 unilaterally and verifiably in its own strategic loops: the costly signal of Proposition 9.3.
- When $\mu$ is high, it compares denial, a coalition that stops the decisive class, and prevention (Proposition 7.5).

In the hard case, coverage of every capable actor (Definition 8.1(a)) comes from consent or from coercion. Denying the inputs to the decisive class is the coercive form of a stop. It raises the weight on winning and the trust threshold, and if it escalates, it falls under Proposition 7.5. The theory does not derive that its net effect is positive, and does not endorse it. If neither consent nor a coercion of positive net effect is available, no actor has a secure position; that is the hard case, stated as such.

An actor that manipulates the shared risk as an instrument (Schelling 1960, 1966) plays chicken in survival terms. That is a case within survival-first reasoning: when $\Phi^T>\Phi^R$ and $\Phi^S>\Phi^P$, Proposition 7.9(1) gives chicken, with mixed equilibria and wars of attrition.

### 7.7 A verified agreement over a contest that remains

**Best case.** Rivals agree to slow and to delay decisive capability, with compute declarations, supply-chain accounting, recomputation of workloads, research transparency, and mutually assured destruction of compute if the agreement dissolves. It works without trust and bounds the largest covert project that could escape detection.

Let $\mathcal O(t)$ be the capability an agreement holds in check (the overhang); $X(t)$ the value of exiting first and $X_{\text{stay}}(t)$ the value of staying; exit occur at hazard $\mathcal H(t)$; a fraction $\mu(t)$ of the overhang be destroyed on exit; and the expected loss from exit per period be $\mathcal L(t)=\mathcal H(t)[1-\mu(t)]\Lambda(\mathcal O(t))$, with $\Lambda$ increasing. Verification detects a defection in time with probability $\upsilon$, and a sanction $F$ follows.

**Proposition 7.6 (Commitment under a growing overhang) [T].**
1. *Deterring an irreversible, public exit requires $\upsilon F\ge X-X_{\text{stay}}$. Deterring a reversible covert preparation, undone when detected in time, requires $\upsilon F\ge(1-\upsilon)(X-X_{\text{stay}})$. What matters is the net exit gain, not $X$ alone; it grows with the overhang when leaving with a decisive advantage gains faster than staying with one's share.*
2. *$\dot{\mathcal L}/\mathcal L=\dot{\mathcal H}/\mathcal H-\dot\mu/(1-\mu)+\varepsilon_\Lambda\dot{\mathcal O}/\mathcal O$, with $\varepsilon_\Lambda$ the elasticity of $\Lambda$. Growth of the overhang faster than the decline of the exit hazard is not sufficient for the loss to rise.*
3. *An agreement that preserves relative positions (proportional caps or reserves) preserves the relative term of every payoff, and with it the motive in $X$.*

**Theorem 7.7 (Verification and the value of winning) [T].** *In the assurance game of Proposition 9.1:*
1. *If $T>R$, cooperation requires timely detection $\upsilon>(T-R)/(T-P+F)$, which tends to one as $T$ grows for a fixed sanction. If $T<R$, cooperation does not require verification, and verification lowers the trust threshold. Verification is required exactly when, and to the degree that, the value of winning exceeds the value of cooperating.*
2. *Detection counts only before a defection becomes decisive. Under P1 and P2 that window is short, and acceleration shortens it.*
3. *Verification does not carry unverifiable information (Proposition 5.14), future commitments unless $\upsilon F$ keeps pace with the net exit gain (Proposition 7.6), the judgment of approvers (Section 4), or its own reliability.*
4. *Verification acts in two ways. It raises timely detection. It can also lower $T$ by bounding the size of the largest defection that would go undetected, for instance by accounting for compute; that route does not need $\upsilon\to1$. The bound erodes as algorithms spread and capability per unit of compute grows, so it holds only if the verified coalition's advantage in effective compute grows at least as fast as spillover erodes it. Verification also removes the information rents that private types create in dividing a joint arrangement (Proposition 8.9).*

**An example.** *AI 2040: Plan A* (Larsen et al. 2026) is a deal among leading powers to slow AI development and delay superintelligence to about 2040, designed to be enforceable without trust [O: EV10]. Its elements include:
- compute declarations and supply-chain accounting;
- inference-only retrofits of datacentres and recomputation of workloads;
- research transparency;
- mutually assured destruction of compute, fabs and robots if the deal declines;
- hardened reserves of about two per cent of pre-deal compute.

It aims to end winner-take-all dynamics through many frontier developers, transparency, broad diffusion and a citizens' dividend. It bounds the size of covert projects rather than requiring near-perfect detection, which is the second route of Theorem 7.7(4).

Its own scenario estimates do not show near-certain dissolution: one author's judgment puts decline in years five to ten at about four per cent a year, half dissolution and half impairment, and cumulative decline over ten years near half. Its handoff to AI systems is a conditional decision that weighs further alignment research against other risks, and it keeps an option to extend the pause. The durability tests of Section 7.8 apply to it as to every arrangement.

**What it changes.** It slows and caps, acting on tempo and decisiveness. It keeps relative standing in its enforcement, through proportional reserves and a coalition that must stay ahead of covert projects. The objective of winning is therefore kept in its fallback, and the conversion of success into control over correction is addressed only through transparency. That makes it a bridge. Whether it can be the end depends on the tests below, and on whether it lowers the value of winning or only bounds defection.

### 7.8 Durability stress tests, applied to every arrangement

**Definition 7.8 (Durability tests) [D].** An arrangement is tested along six dimensions, using its own documents and estimates, not assumptions added by the tester.
- **D1 Fallback.** What do parties hold if it dissolves? A decisive race from preserved relative standing, or something less dangerous?
- **D2 Hazard of breakdown.** What is its estimated hazard of dissolution or impairment over the horizon it must cover, and what drives that hazard?
- **D3 Retained authority.** After any handoff or delegation, who keeps informed veto, modification, replacement, exit and execution? Which of U1–U4 still hold?
- **D4 Capture of its own correctors.** Do its evaluators and adjudicators have full inbound row sums below one (Proposition 4.8)?
- **D5 Violation by the strongest participant.** What happens when the leading provider or state violates it?
- **D6 Succession and continuity.** What happens on a change of leadership, and do essential services continue during correction or sanction?
- **D7 Common causes and collusion.** Do its checks and its AI systems share lineage, funders or models, so that one failure or one collusion defeats them together (Proposition 6.9)?
- **D8 Exclusion and blocs.** Is membership open on non-discriminatory terms, or does inclusion stop at a club whose relation to outsiders reproduces the race one level up (Proposition 3.11)?

The tests apply symmetrically: to every continuation policy, to agreements that keep the contest underneath, and to the cooperative design of Section 8 (results in Section 8.7). An arrangement that has the form of cooperation but fails D1–D3 on its own estimates may be called a *pseudo-solution*. The label summarizes test results; it is not a substitute for them.

| Arrangement | D1 Fallback | D2 Breakdown | D3 Authority | D4 Correctors | D5 Strongest violates | D6 Succession |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Regulated national competition | The race itself | Rule changes with administrations (EV3) | Kept nationally | Mandates shifting toward industry (EV3) | Domestic law only | Exposed to change of administration |
| Lead and margin | The race | Margin decays (Remark 7.1) | The leader's | The leader's evaluators | No constraint | The leader's internal field |
| Benign winner (win, bind, share) | Domination | Depends on selection inside the winner | The winner's | The winner's own correctors | The winner violating its own bargain, with no one outside to enforce it | Succession inside the winner is a main risk |
| Deterrence by sabotage | War or race | Misjudgment (Prop 7.5) | Kept | Mutual intelligence | Strike | Brittle |
| Plan A type agreement | A short sprint from parity: about a year to superintelligence after decline if compute is destroyed, by its authors' estimate | Decline about 4% a year in years 5–10, cumulative about half over 10 years (scenario judgment) | A conditional handoff; who keeps veto after it is not specified | Joint verification; titration inside the coalition | Mutual destruction of compute; sanctions | Leadership change is a listed cause of decline |
| Cooperative design (§8) | See Section 8.7 | See Section 8.7 | See Section 8.7 | See Section 8.7 | See Section 8.7 | See Section 8.7 |

### 7.9 Comparing paths in survival terms

Survival is computed in one discrete convention: per-period conditional probabilities over a common horizon $H$. Let $h_R$ be the per-period hazard under an arrangement of restraint, $q_{\mathrm I}$ the loss of the ability to overrule by delegation, $q_{\mathrm{II}}$ the shared hazard, and $q_W$ the hazard of preventive attack. Let $p_{\text{dec}}$ be the probability that a racer's advantage becomes decisive when all race, and $p^S_{\text{dec}}$ the same when the actor stays out. Let $p_{L4}$ be the actor's belief that a decisive winner would extend its dominance over it. This is a belief, not a quantity that survival-first preference sets to one. With independence for the formulas,
$$\Phi^R=1-h_R,\quad \Phi^T=(1-q_{\mathrm I})(1-q^T_{\mathrm{II}})(1-q_W),$$
$$\Phi^P=(1-q_{\mathrm I})(1-q^P_{\mathrm{II}})(1-q_W)\Big(1-p_{\text{dec}}\tfrac{n-1}{n}\Big),\quad \Phi^S=(1-q^S_{\mathrm{II}})(1-p^S_{\text{dec}}p_{L4}).$$

**Proposition 7.9 (Assurance in survival terms at a common horizon) [T].**
1. *The advantage of cooperating is $\varpi(\Phi^R-\Phi^T)+(1-\varpi)(\Phi^S-\Phi^P)$, where $\varpi$ is trust. If both terms are non-negative, cooperation is dominant. If only the first is positive, the game is a stag hunt with threshold $(\Phi^P-\Phi^S)/((\Phi^R-\Phi^T)+(\Phi^P-\Phi^S))$. If only the second is, it is chicken; if neither, a prisoner's dilemma.*
2. *In the region $\Phi^S\ge\Phi^P$, $\Phi^R\ge\Phi^T$, an actor that holds the prediction restrains whatever it believes about the others.*
3. *Mutual racing remains a strict equilibrium at every finite horizon with $\Phi^P_H>\Phi^S_H$.*
4. *For constant per-period hazards $q_B$ on a branch and $q_R$ under the arrangement, $\Phi^B(H)/\Phi^R(H)=\big((1-q_B)/(1-q_R)\big)^H$, which decays at rate $\log((1-q_R)/(1-q_B))$. With $q_B=0.05$ and $q_R=0.005$ the rate is $0.0463$, not $0.045$ [N].*
5. *Mixed beliefs. If an actor gives the premises of Section 5.3 probability $p_{\text{prem}}$, and the branches of the race have survival $T_\neg,P_\neg,S_\neg$ when they fail, the stag-hunt classification at horizon $H$ needs $\Phi^R(H)-\big(p_{\text{prem}}\Phi^T_H+(1-p_{\text{prem}})T_\neg\big)>0$ and the corresponding sign on the second term. When the arrangement's own survival $\Phi^R(H)$ also decays, these signs can fail at long horizons. With $p_{\text{prem}}=0.99$, $\Phi^R=e^{-0.005H}$, race branches decaying at rate $0.05$ when the premises hold, and $(T_\neg,P_\neg,S_\neg)=(0.9,0.4,0.1)$, the arrangement's survival exceeds the mixed survival of racing alone at $H=10$, $100$ and $600$; at $H=1{,}000$ it does not, and racing becomes dominant [N].*

Numbers for $n=2$ with $p_{L4}=1$, at a horizon where the hazards are as stated [N]:
- With $q_{\mathrm I}=0.6$, $q^T_{\mathrm{II}}=q^S_{\mathrm{II}}=0.3$, $q^P_{\mathrm{II}}=0.4$, $q_W=0.2$, $p_{\text{dec}}=p^S_{\text{dec}}=0.6$ and $h_R=0.1$: $\Phi^R=0.90$, $\Phi^T=0.22$, $\Phi^P=0.13$ and $\Phi^S=0.28$. Cooperation is dominant.
- If staying out raises the racer's decisive chance to $0.8$, $\Phi^S=0.14$, still above $\Phi^P$, because joining raised the shared hazard.
- With low delegation ($q_{\mathrm I}=0.05$, shared hazard $0.1$, $q_W=0.1$, $p_{\text{dec}}=0.3$), the game is a stag hunt with threshold $0.16$.
- For a leader that wins with probability $0.8$ if it races, and whose exit hands decisive advantage to the rival ($q_{\mathrm I}=0.2$, $q_W=0.2$, $p_{\text{dec}}=0.9$, $p^S_{\text{dec}}=0.95$, $h_R=0.1$), it is a stag hunt with threshold $0.42$ when the shared hazard is $0.3$, falling to $0.11$ when it is $0.8$. For the leader, the shared hazard carries the argument.

The comparison is therefore made at common finite horizons, not at $H=\infty$, where both branches of a hazardous world can tend to zero and a first-order ranking by $\Phi(\infty)$ ties them. A survival-first actor uses finite-horizon dominance: one path is preferred if it gives higher survival at every horizon in the range that matters. That is the form of standard D.

**Discounting.** Under a discounted objective, with constant race hazard $h$, race growth $g_r$ and paced growth $g_p$, a race is preferred iff $h<g_r-g_p$, even though it ends in catastrophe with certainty (Jones 2024; Fudenberg and Koh 2026) [N]. A rising hazard reverses the preference only if it comes to exceed the growth differential. If the hazard is capped at $0.05$ and the growth gap stays at $0.10$, no reversal occurs. Standard D does not discount the protected outcomes, by choice (V2).

### 7.10 What any adequate arrangement must do

The comparison does not need a proof that one design is best. It needs a statement of what every adequate design must do, stated before the designs are compared, so that any of them, including regulated competition, can be found to do it.

**Proposition 7.10 (Requirements) [T∣A].** *On a branch where a mechanism of Section 2.3 is active, any arrangement that keeps the probability of O1 and O2 within the tolerance of standard D at horizon $H$ must meet the corresponding requirement, unless the condition in the last column holds.*

| Requirement | Mechanism it answers | Why it is necessary | Not needed when |
|---|---|---|---|
| **R1** Consequences return: long-run effective liability at least the private advantage of load | Accounting | Theorem 8.2(3) | The host's floor is out of reach (H0 fails) |
| **R2** Coverage of every capable actor | Accounting; decisiveness | Proposition 5.1(4); Lemma 3.3(2); Proposition 8.3 | Harms are local, with no cross-boundary effect |
| **R3** Correction outside the competitors: inbound row sums below one, and growth at least $g/(1+\eta)$, or the channels removed | Selecting the selectors | Propositions 4.3, 4.4, 4.8 | $\chi=0$, or the product of conversion channels is below its damping (Remark 2.3) |
| **R4** Bounded positional stakes: no decisive position that can be held unilaterally before common custody exists; self-enforcement after | Decisiveness; compounding | Propositions 5.7, 5.10; Theorem 5.16 | Advantage saturates (Branch I′) |
| **R5** Tempo bound to correction | Tempo | Proposition 5.11; Theorem 5.12 and its converse | (X), (U) or (φ) fails |
| **R6** Observation independent of the observed; shared measurement and verifiable signals | Masking and attribution; language | Remark 3.7; Proposition 4.6; Remark 4.7; Proposition 5.14 | Channels that observe actions directly suffice |
| **R7** Standing for those affected; no inclusion that stops at a club | Accounting at the level of the system | Proposition 3.11; V3 | Never, for the normative part |
| **R8** Admission constraints common to all systems; no retention of decisions by contest performance | The training loop; delegation | Lemma 6.5; Proposition 6.7 | Systems are not retained by contest performance |

*R2, R3 and R4 cannot be met by any actor alone (Propositions 8.3, 4.3; Theorem 5.16), and competition among actors does not supply them.*

The requirements are necessary, not sufficient. The proposition mostly reorganizes results proved elsewhere as necessary conditions; what is new is the organization, R4, R7 and R8. Its consequence is the strongest form in which "the world must cooperate" can be defended. On branches where the mechanisms are active, a minimum of cooperation is necessary: binding commitments covering every capable actor, with a layer of correction that no competitor controls. This is a conditional theorem combined with evidence and a stated standard, not a proof from first principles.

**Lemma 7.11 (When an arrangement cannot do worse) [T].** *Compare an arrangement $A$ with continuation $C$ in survival with respect to O1 and O2. Assume:*
- *(F1) a safe fallback: if $A$ dissolves at any date $\tau$, the conditional hazard thereafter is pointwise no higher than $C$'s from the same date;*
- *(F2) bounded own risk: while $A$ lasts, its residual hazard together with its own hazards (capture, unjustified restriction, single points of failure, collusion among its AI systems) is pointwise no higher than $C$'s.*

*Then $S^A(H)\ge S^C(H)$ at every horizon $H$, for every distribution of dissolution dates.*

The proof couples the two paths period by period through the chain rule (Appendix A). If F1 and F2 hold at every point of the uncertainty range $\Theta$, the conclusion holds in the worst case.

With dissolution at 2% a period and a fallback that restarts continuation ten periods later [N]:
- the arrangement survives with probability $0.976$, $0.864$ and $0.211$ at horizons 10, 30 and 100;
- continuation survives with $0.875$, $0.575$ and $0.026$.

The lemma turns the comparison into two design conditions that can be checked.
- An agreement whose fallback is a short sprint from preserved relative standing violates F1.
- A cooperative design whose own concentration, capture or collusion can exceed continuation's hazards violates F2. That is crux C4.

Where F2 fails, there is no pointwise dominance, and standard D compares the worst cases of both. That is where regulated competition can win.

### 7.11 What an arrangement changes, and how the comparison is made

**Definition 7.12 (What an arrangement changes) [D].** An arrangement is described by a profile, not a label.
1. **Mechanisms.** For each mechanism of Section 2.3: does it neutralize it, weaken it, leave it, or strengthen it?
2. **Selection.** Does it change what the system selects for, or constrain conduct under an unchanged selection? Changing the selection means changing the map from conduct to share, resources and standing. Four marks of such a change:
   - consequences return;
   - success cannot buy control of its own assessment;
   - decisive stakes are bounded or held in common;
   - measurement and language are shared.
3. **Coverage**: unilateral, plurilateral, or every capable actor.
4. **Bindingness**: obligation, precision and delegation (Abbott, Keohane, Moravcsik, Slaughter and Snidal 2000).
5. **Decisiveness**: does its enforcement survive the arrival of a decisive position?
6. **Reversibility by success**: can its beneficiaries amend it, and are its correctors' row sums below one?
7. **Fallback and own risk** (Lemma 7.11).
8. **Durability**: D1–D8.

The *pivot* is the direction in which these profiles move. It changes what the system selects for, by arrangements that cover every capable actor, bind, survive decisiveness and cannot be undone by success. The name of the arrangement does not matter.

The definition carries a prediction, registered in advance [C]: **arrangements that constrain conduct while leaving the selection pressure intact erode as the stakes and the speed grow; arrangements that change what is selected persist.** The prediction is refuted if the former stay durable as power grows. The classification uses Proposition 4.9, Proposition 5.7 and the profile, never the outcome.

Five present policies are profiled in advance:

| Policy | Mechanisms acted on | Changes selection? | Coverage | Survives decisiveness? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Export controls on advanced chips to preserve a lead | Decisiveness, for others; strengthens the expectation that falling behind cannot be recovered | No: it constrains rivals, and its aim is a lead | Unilateral or bloc | It aims at decisiveness |
| Compute thresholds with reporting and evaluation | Tempo; observation | Only if evaluation is separated from the evaluated and the threshold binds all | Depends: the same tool can be unilateral or common | No, unless custody follows |
| A public AI safety institute | Selecting the selectors, locally | Partly, if independence is statutory and access mandated; otherwise no | One jurisdiction | No |
| Benefit sharing (services, access, dividends) | Compounding, through the divisible prize | Yes, if contemporaneous and binding; a promise to share after victory is not credible (Proposition 5.10) | Varies | Only if contemporaneous |
| A bilateral incident channel | Language and attribution | No change in payoffs; a seed of shared measurement | Bilateral | No |

The comparison at common horizons, policy by policy:

| Policy | What it changes | Main residual risk | Crux |
|---|---|---|---|
| Technical alignment and AI-assisted correction alone | Residual harm per unit; tempo; not selection | The game among principals; absorption of gains; common causes; the outer training loop | Can contestable coverage keep pace with hazardous variety? |
| Regulated competition within each jurisdiction | Selection in part, within one jurisdiction | Coverage across jurisdictions; the rule-makers' contest; weakening commitments | Will restraint hold when rivals advance (EV2)? |
| Leadership and margin | Tempo for a time | The margin decays and is judged inside | Do leads get spent on safety, and do rivals follow? |
| The benign winner | Nothing before victory | Others' O2 rests on unverifiable restraint | Can a winner's limits be made checkable before victory? |
| Finite sprint, then restraint | Tempo, after the sprint | Peak exposure and prevention during the sprint | How small is the hazardous ratio built (Proposition 5.13)? |
| Deterrence by sabotage | Nothing in selection; holds the contest at the brink | Misjudgment; militarization | Observability and credibility |
| Verified agreement over a remaining contest | Tempo and decisiveness, bounded | Fallback and handoff (D1, D3) | Does it lower the value of winning, or only bound defection? |
| Temporary training pause | Tempo, for a time | Resumption unchanged | Readiness dimensions Q1–Q7 (Definition 9.5) |
| A design meeting R1–R8 (Section 8) | Selection | Its own dissolution, capture and restriction | D1–D8 for the design; participation (Section 8.5) |

**An illustration of the two cruxes [N].** Let the per-period hazard on a path be $0.05$ times its uncovered share, plus a background $0.001$. On the cooperative path, add a hazard of dissolution times the loss its fallback carries. Survival at horizons 10, 30 and 100 is as follows.

| Path | $H=10$ | $H=30$ | $H=100$ |
|---|---|---|---|
| Continuation, uncovered share rising from $0.2$ by $0.01$ a period | 0.871 | 0.567 | 0.028 |
| Continuation, uncovered share kept summable | 0.966 | 0.941 | 0.873 |
| Cooperation, summable share, dissolution 2% a period with fallback loss 0.5 | 0.873 | 0.696 | 0.319 |
| Cooperation, summable share, dissolution 0.2% a period | 0.956 | 0.913 | 0.789 |

The numbers are illustrative, not estimates. They show the structure of the decision:
- If continuation keeps the uncovered hazardous share summable, it can beat a cooperative arrangement that is fragile.
- If the uncovered share rises on the continuation path, any cooperative arrangement with a modest dissolution hazard does better at every horizon past the near term.

So the comparison turns on two quantities: whether correction on the present path keeps pace with hazardous variety, and how durable a cooperative arrangement can be made. The first is crux C1 of Section 10. The second is the subject of Sections 8 and 9, which design for it and test it.

**Where the decision criteria disagree.**
- Under standard D, the worst case over the justified range includes a rising uncovered share on the continuation path, because no evidence today excludes it (Section 2.10, EV5–EV7). So continuation fails the tolerance at medium and long horizons unless its correction can be shown to keep pace.
- An expected-value criterion that puts low probability on the rising-gap case, and values the benefits of speed highly, can prefer continuation.
- A discounted criterion prefers racing whenever the hazard it adds is below the growth it adds (Section 7.9).

These disagreements are the normative cruxes, and Section 10 states them. In the illustration, a well-corrected continuation outperforms a fragile cooperative arrangement; the analysis does not establish that every cooperative arrangement outperforms regulated competition, and Section 10 does not claim it.

**Conditions under which continuation, or regulated competition, wins (registered in advance).** The recommendation of Section 10 changes toward continuation with strong correction if the following are measured:
- correction on the present path keeps the uncovered hazardous share summable (crux C1);
- advantage saturates, so that $p_d$ stays low (C5);
- detection of covert breakouts is faster than the time to decisive capability (C7);
- the winner's internal correctors keep row sums below one (D4 inside the winner);
- rules in every capable jurisdiction meet R1–R8.

In that case the text will say that regulated competition meets the requirements. It will not rename it.

### 7.12 The logic to oppose, in the vocabulary of international relations

The logic of Section 2.6 has two sources.
- Actors hold it, and selection on actions rewards its behaviour (Proposition 3.9(4)).
- Structure also produces it. Anarchy, decisive advantage and the absence of any commitment device produce outcomes of the same kind among actors who do not hold it (Proposition 7.2; Proposition 5.7).

An actor holding it does not stop compounding, because no level of standing makes more stop rewarding. On a continuation branch where the premises of Section 5.3 hold, Theorem 5.12 applies to its own capability as well as to everyone else's: the logic then endangers its holder along with its host.

If a winner's logic is of this kind, falling behind cannot be survived, and every actor has reason first to oppose the logic itself. To oppose it is to change the structure and the selection environment, not to oppose any actor. That means three things:
- credible, verifiable limits on what anyone would do with decisive advantage;
- no decisive advantage without a commitment device;
- making it common knowledge that the logic endangers its holder.

The first step is the shared assessment of Stage 1 (Section 9.4).

In the vocabulary of international relations, this is defensive realism with one more variable. When the shared costs of competition are high enough and the rival seeks security, cooperation is self-help (Jervis 1978; Glaser 1994/95; Glaser 2010). The variable added here is a shared hazard in development that endangers its holder. A hazard in development acts as a negative payoff to the offense, as a weapon that endangers its holder does. The Biological Weapons Convention is the closest precedent (Section 9.6).

The three outcomes of Proposition 5.7 also correspond to the three cultures of anarchy of constructivism:
- enmity, where only stage-game equilibria hold;
- rivalry, where cooperation is enforced;
- friendship, where it is built into objectives (Wendt 1999).

The present theory adds the selection that moves a system between them, and the decisiveness that collapses the middle one.

**Remark 7.13 (Why insuring against falling behind is not an exit).** One proposal for an actor that cannot obtain cooperation is to bound the penalty for falling behind, $L_b$.
