It would secure autonomous, good-enough provision and defence that do not depend on rank, so that restraint becomes a best response. The proposal does not leave the contest, for three reasons.
- The penalty is set by the winner, not by the actor that fell behind. If a decisive winner extends its dominance, the bound is not in the laggard's hands, so it is at most as reliable as the belief $p_{L4}$ allows.
- A winner with a relative objective places negative weight on the laggard's payoff, and so has a motive to destroy what the laggard secured.
- The proposal accepts the frame of the contest: it insures against losing instead of declining the game.

Two things must be distinguished from it. Defence that makes decisive advantage unattainable for everyone lowers $r$ for all; it belongs to E2, provided it stays under human oversight, since defence at machine speed is itself a pressure to delegate. And the shared provision of Section 8.1 belongs to a world that has left the contest; it is not insurance within one.

## 8. The generative meta-attractor

Section 2 defined the meta-attractor without a sign (Remark 2.4). The same reflexive structure that lets success buy control over future selection can make contribution fund the conditions of further contribution. One cannot optimize one's way out of an optimization trap. The way out is therefore not a better optimization of the same objective, and not the abolition of selection, competition or growth. It is a change in what the system selects for, so that its reflexive loop strengthens the shared host and independent correction instead of consuming them. This section states the purpose (8.1), the generative loop (8.2), what an arrangement that changes selection must contain (8.3), what sharing can and cannot do (8.4), verification and participation (8.5), design by function (8.6), and the tests that every design, including the one sketched here, must pass (8.7).

### 8.1 The purpose

Cooperation is not only a constraint on danger. Its purpose is what AI can do for people when it is not the instrument by which one actor wins the common future:
- medicine and public health;
- education;
- science;
- competent administration;
- infrastructure;
- the long-horizon work that fragmented incentives under-supply.

The joint value is very large. The race dissipates it (Proposition 5.2); a change in what is selected realizes it (V1). AI is a planetary technology, and its purpose can be shared flourishing.

**Authority as a productive capacity (V4).** Authority can coordinate investment, guarantee services, support learning and act on shared purposes. The ideal the theory draws on justifies authority by its responsibility for improving people's lives and the historical trajectory. That ideal enters the design as a purpose to be sustained, not as a premise that it is already realized. The governing purpose is itself part of the mechanism:
- how institutions select and form officeholders;
- how they reward service and sustain professional obligations;
- how they hear unwelcome evidence;
- how they evaluate long-term outcomes and revise harmful practices.

Neither inevitable corruption nor automatic benevolence is assumed. A stated mission is not proof of implementation; its formation and persistence are causal variables. The author's corpus states the principle as "where power reaches, responsibility follows" (力之所及，责之所在): a larger causal radius brings larger responsibility, not larger entitlement.

**Provision that does not become the system.** Taken out of the contest, AI can be what infrastructure is: provision of the conditions of a decent life, built to meet need rather than to gain standing. Trees are the image. They give shelter and fruit, maintain the system they belong to, and do not become it. The theory supports four properties of such provision.
- **Distributed.** Many providers below the size threshold of Proposition 4.2(2) do not cultivate influence over their correctors, and splitting a provider divides cultivated conversion by $k^{2/\eta}$ (Proposition 4.4(7)).
- **Good enough.** Systems that stop growing once they meet a need keep their hazard modes within correction and confer no decisive advantage. Production directed at need has a point of enough; relative advantage has none (Proposition 8.11).
- **Replaceable.** Systems that can be replaced keep human capacity above $\mathcal K_{\min}$ and avoid the lock of Lemma 6.3.
- **For everyone.** Understanding and provision go to everyone, not to a class of guardians. Every guardian is a corrector, and Section 4 applies to it.

**Reward for contribution, not rights over correction (V5).** Productive contribution should be rewarded: revenue, contracts, recognition. What success should not buy is the right to appoint evaluators, set thresholds, exclude entrants or amend the governing rules. Separating the two keeps productive incentives while cutting the conversion $b_{ij}$ of Section 4.

**A shared future, stated structurally.** The author's corpus gives the norm of a community of shared future a three-part form: the future is kept open for all; the conditions of continuation are shared; and no one seizes the shared continuation (共开、共续、不夺共续). The third part is the structural form of standing (V3) and of the protection of human decision authority (O2). It is declared, not derived.

### 8.2 The generative loop

The author's corpus states two loops with the same reflexive structure and opposite signs.
- **The generative loop.** Security makes trust possible; trust makes cooperation possible; cooperation produces abundance; abundance allows sufficiency and slack; sufficiency and slack allow wider care and longer foresight; these produce better order and more security. In institutional form: shared capability, distributed agency, plural observation and criticism, maintenance and repair, renewal of the commons, greater capacity for cooperation, and further shared capability.
- **The degenerative loop.** Fear produces defence; defence produces struggle; struggle produces seizure and concentration; these produce scarcity and resentment; trust collapses; fear deepens.

The difference is not one of moral taste but of structure. The first loop turns more agents into generators. The second turns more agents into defenders, instruments or exiles.

The generative loop has four strands. Each is the dual of a mechanism of Section 2.3, and each is a loop with a carrier, a condition and a test, not an exhortation. Goodwill is not a carrier.

1. **Reasoning instead of winning (讲理 rather than 讲赢).** *Dual of the alienation of language, and of attribution.* Where no decisive prize is at stake and shared exposure is visible, what pays is being right about the shared situation, not prevailing in it. Its product is shared understanding. Trust is an outcome of that understanding, not its prerequisite: it arises when there is a shared understanding of reality and others can reasonably be expected to act on it.
   - *Carriers:* derivations from public premises that each party can check; verifiable evidence; protocols agreed in advance; costly signals (Proposition 9.3).
   - *Condition:* the objectives of the parties are not opposed on the topic of the talk (Proposition 5.14). Lowering the contested stakes widens the set of topics on which words carry information.
   - *Test:* the informativeness of communication between rivals rises as contested stakes fall and as shared exposure is made visible.
2. **Reward separated from advantage.** *Dual of compounding and conversion.* A winner is rewarded with anything other than more advantage in the same game. Recognition, income for useful service and responsibility are rewards; the right to set the rules, choose the evaluators or exclude entrants is not.
   - *Carrier:* the separation of reward from authority over correction, and the sharing of the divisible gains of capability (Proposition 8.6).
   - *Condition:* the convertible share of what is won satisfies $\chi r\le1$ (Remark 5.3), and success cannot buy control over its own assessment ($\chi\to0$ in Section 4).
   - *Test:* conduct changes in proportion to what is shared. If it does not, there are channels of advantage outside the shared prize (crux C6).
3. **Provision beneath the game.** *Dual of the gain.* The belief that one is in a struggle for survival raises the weight on winning and shortens the horizon (Section 2.3). Provision of the conditions of a decent life, independent of rank, removes the ground of that belief. In the author's words, the common foundation does not prescribe how anyone lives; it lets everyone be entitled to live without having to defeat others first.
   - *Carrier:* the shared provision of Section 8.1, good enough, distributed and replaceable.
   - *Conditions:* provision is not tied to standing; receiving it carries no social inferiority. Provision that becomes a gate is the softest of collars.
   - *Test:* where provision is secured independently of rank, the weight placed on relative position in contested domains falls.
4. **Correction in common hands, plural and decorrelated.** *Dual of selecting the selectors, and of masking.* The regulator must itself be regulated. A true order is not one in which some people are always right, but one built so that errors cannot grow without limit.
   - *Carriers:* independent correctors with certificates on their full row sums (Proposition 4.8); varieties of systems and lineages that do not all fail from the same cause (Proposition 6.9); exit together with free association, which is selection with the opposite sign.
   - *Condition:* correction is funded by formula and appointed at arm's length from the corrected, and it grows at the rate of Proposition 4.4.
   - *Test:* the correctors' inbound row sums, measured, stay below one as the field grows.

Each strand faces the same questions as the pathological mechanisms.
- How does it keep obtaining resources?
- How does it meet free riding and the pressure to expand?
- How does it allow itself to be corrected?
- How does it avoid turning "protecting the whole" into a purpose that no one can challenge?

The last question is the guardian test of Section 8.7. The author's corpus records the warning in its own history: an examination system created to select by merit became, over centuries, a selection for the examination's form.

The criterion is generative or degenerative, not cooperative or competitive. Cooperation on the wrong thing can be worse than competition on it, and a cartel is cooperation (Proposition 3.11). Bounded competition in products, science and service continues inside a generative order. What is removed is competition for control over the shared conditions and over the correctors.

**Is the generative order an attractor?** Its purpose is a value [V]. Its strands are definitions [D], and each composite consequence below is a theorem [T]. Whether the order as a whole is self-sustaining in the world, an attracting configuration in the sense of Definition 2.2(2), is a conjecture [C]. One caution comes from the same mathematics. A cooperative order whose loop gain greatly exceeds its damping also resists correction, including correction of its own errors. Plurality of purposes, of correctors and of lineages is therefore a requirement of the design, not a decoration. Oneness is not sameness: harmony produces new things, and sameness does not continue (和实生物，同则不继).

### 8.3 What an arrangement that changes selection must contain

Four safeguards make consequences return to their source on the host of Section 3.
- **(I1) Consequences return.** A liability $\tau>\nu$ on load is enacted, in advance for anticipated harms and on the alarm for unanticipated ones, so that $\tau_{\text{eff}}>\nu$ once rule-making is uncaptured. For harms beyond assets, this is regulation in advance rather than liability (Shavell 1984). Near a threshold a quantity instrument does better than a price (Weitzman 1974).
- **(I2) Independent correctors.** In evaluation and rule-making, $d(t)\ge(1+\varsigma)d^\ast(t)$ for some $\varsigma>0$, with $d^\ast$ computed from each domain's full row.
- **(I3) Independent evidence.** Either masking stays below the threshold under an averaged alarm, or a conservative alarm uses a bound on masking established independently of the field (Remark 4.7).
- **(I4) Before capture.** (I1)–(I3) are in force before the corrector domains are captured.

**Definition 8.1 (An arrangement that changes what is selected) [D].** An arrangement changes what the system selects for if it has the following.
- (a) **Coverage.** It binds every actor whose share can grow in the environment it governs, and the rule-makers of every jurisdiction where such growth can occur. A constraint common to all is invisible to relative selection and so is not selected away (Lemma 3.3(2)).
- (b) **Content.** It enacts (I1)–(I3), with thresholds, triggers and authority to act fixed in advance (I4).
- (c) **Verification.** Its correctors assess compliance and the host's condition, and each has a full inbound row sum below one, counting conversion from every party and every shared source.
- (d) **A changed value of winning.** It shares the divisible gains of capability as they arise, and rewards contribution in currencies that do not convert into control (Proposition 8.6). It forms a common assessment, from independent evidence, of what winning is worth and of the hazards all share.
- (e) **Common custody before decisiveness.** Capability of the decisive class is not held unilaterally. Either it is not built (E2), or it is held so that no party can use it alone, and this is in place before any party could hold it (Theorem 5.16).
- (f) **AI out of the contest, with understanding ahead of capability.** Strategic and coercive decision loops are kept under human authority with jointly verified limits (E1). Admission constraints are common to all systems (R8). The tempo of capability is bound to correction capacity (E2 or E3).
- (g) **Trust built deliberately.** Graduated reciprocal steps (Osgood 1962) and costly signals (Kydd 2005; Proposition 9.3).
- (h) **Shared provision.** Common infrastructure supplies useful services, and correction that no participant owns.
- (i) **Answerability.** People affected by consequential decisions have standing, access to evidence and timely remedies (V3).

The definition constrains functions, not institutional form. One institution can meet it. So can mutually recognized institutions of several jurisdictions, a federation, or a polycentric complex. Items (a)–(f) are the four marks of Definition 7.12(2) made specific: consequences return; success cannot buy control of its own assessment; decisive stakes are bounded or held in common; measurement is shared. Items (g)–(i) are how the arrangement is reached and kept answerable.

Let selection be replicator dynamics among carriers that differ only in load. Let a carrier of load $e_{\min}$ with $he_{\min}<\rho(1-V_{\min}/K)$ be present. For part (3), let a carrier with positive share and load $e_{\max}$ satisfying $he_{\max}>\rho(1-V_{\min}/K)$ also be present.

**Theorem 8.2 (Sufficient safeguards, and a necessary condition) [T].**
1. *Sufficiency. Under (I1)–(I4), $\tau_{\text{eff}}>\nu$ after a finite time, $E(t)\to e_{\min}$, and $\liminf_tV(t)>V_{\min}$. This holds from the outset for anticipated harms, and for unanticipated ones after an alarm that fires above the floor. The trajectory is viable if, in addition, the host stays above its floor during the transient in which $E$ falls.*
2. *Non-redundancy. For each of (I1)–(I3) there are parameters satisfying H0 and the other two under which the host crosses its floor. There are also parameters under which (I1)–(I3) come into force only after rule-making is captured, and the host crosses its floor.*
3. *A necessary condition. Under H0 and the support assumption, every viable trajectory satisfies $\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac1T\int_0^T\tau_{\text{eff}}(t)\,dt\ge\nu$.*

Part (1) is sufficient, and part (3) is necessary in the weak sense stated. The transient in (1) is real: with liability in force from the start but the race strategy holding 99% of activity, the host dips to $0.14$ before recovering [N].

**Proposition 8.3 (No unilateral solution) [T].** *Assume H0 and H1.*
1. *While $\tau_{\text{eff}}<\nu$, and among carriers that differ only in that contribution, no carrier's unilateral contribution to (I1)–(I4) is favoured by selection.*
2. *Suppose no single rule-maker reaches every carrier whose combined load satisfies H0, and the rule-makers are themselves $n_s$ actors in a contest of standing with weight $\lambda_s>(\Theta_s-n_s)/(n_s-1)$. Then no rule-maker imposes the liability alone in equilibrium. Whether an agreement among them is an equilibrium is governed by Proposition 9.1.*
3. *Restraint is each carrier's best response once the effective liability exceeds the advantage, $\tau(1-x_{\mathsf S})>\nu$. Enacting $\tau>\nu$ is not enough while $x_{\mathsf S}$ is large.*

Part (1) is the unilateral face of Lemma 3.3(2), and part (2) is its face among rule-makers. A constraint that binds one carrier is selected against; the same constraint binding all is invisible to selection. That is the formal reason the arrangement must cover every capable actor, and the reason coordination succeeds where unilateral virtue fails.

### 8.4 A common frontier, and what winning confers

**Proposition 8.4 (A common frontier changes the failure mode) [T].** *Let the value of capability be common, $\pi_i=\Omega(\mathcal E)/n-ce_i-\ell\mathcal E$ with $\Omega$ concave, rather than allocated by a contest. Private incentives give $\Omega'(\mathcal E)=n(c+\ell)$ and the joint optimum $\Omega'(\mathcal E)=c+n\ell$, so total intensity is at or below the joint optimum. The residual failure is too little speed, not too much. The joint optimum must also respect the host's floor.*

A common frontier does not remove the second order. Allocation within a joint project is itself a contest, and the project, as the largest carrier, has the largest return to influence over its correctors (Proposition 4.2). It therefore needs correction outside the project, equipped with AI, contestable, and growing at the rate of Proposition 4.4.

There are $n$ actors. Cooperators hold effort $e_c$; racers choose theirs. Whoever wins the decisive contest gains $\Omega$, and every actor that loses it bears $L_b$. Under *separate frontiers* every effort enters the contest separately. Under a *common frontier* cooperators pool their effort into one entity of strength $(me_c)^r$, where $m$ is the number of cooperators. The pool yields a common value $\Omega_c$ split equally and confers no dominance among them. Timing is two-stage: actors choose whether to race, then racers play their equilibrium. The measure is material payoff.

**Proposition 8.5 (A common frontier in the $n$-player game) [T, N].** *Take $n=4$, $\Omega=\Omega_c=L_b=c=1$, $\ell=0.3$, $e_c=0.05$ and $r=1.3$.*
1. *Under separate frontiers racing is strictly better for every number of other racers; the unique pure equilibrium is all racing.*
2. *Under a common frontier, all cooperating is a strict Nash equilibrium ($R=0.140>T=0.002$), and so is all racing.*
   - *If each other actor races independently, cooperating is a best response iff trust in each rival exceeds about $0.61$.*
   - *If a deviator instead best-responds to the others' fixed intensities, the trust needed rises to about $0.82$.*
3. *Reducing the game to one actor against the other three acting together gives $0.413$. That is the threshold for a belief that the others move together, not risk dominance in the original game. Written with the three as a coalition maximizing their summed payoff, the coalition's cooperation is strictly dominant.*
4. *The two equilibria persist under the variations tried in which racers' subgames have a symmetric pure equilibrium, except that a small pooled effort ($e_c=0.03$) leaves only all racing. The aggregate threshold is $0.755$ with $n=3$ and $0.902$ with $L_b=0.5$. With $n=2$, all cooperating is an equilibrium for $r$ up to about $1.2$: a lower $r$ makes a common frontier easier to sustain.*
5. *Holding $\Omega_c$ and $L_b$ fixed, the pooled effort needed to keep $T\le R$ is $0.0054$, $0.035$, $0.141$ and $0.49$ for $\Omega=0.5$, $1$, $2$ and $4$. Scaling $\Omega_c$ and $L_b$ with $\Omega$, it grows in proportion and $R$ stays positive. The lid depends on the value of winning relative to the joint value and to the penalty of subordination.*

What makes cooperation pay in the construct is that cooperators do not subordinate one another. A common frontier turns a dilemma into a coordination problem, while the value of winning is moderate. When that value is large, keeping ahead of defectors is the race moved up a level, and a pool strong enough to subordinate defectors is a concentration of power that Section 4 says will be selected for influence over its correctors.

What can be shared, and what sharing does, need to be stated exactly. Divide the prize of the contest into two parts:
- a *divisible* part $\Omega_e$: rents, revenues, services, the material gains of capability;
- a *positional* part $\Omega_s$: decisive standing, the power to set the rules, and the loss $L_b$ of being subordinated.

**Proposition 8.6 (Two ways to share, and what changes the direction) [T].** *Take the contest of Section 3.6 with $n$ actors, decisiveness $r$ and private cost $c$ per unit of intensity.*
1. *Pooling payoffs. Let each actor receive $(1-\beta)$ of its own material payoff plus $\beta$ times the average of all, $\beta\in[0,1]$. An actor holding self-model $\theta_0>-1$ then acts, up to a positive factor, as if it held*
   $$\theta_{\text{eff}}(\beta,\theta_0)=\frac{\theta_0(1-\beta)+\beta(1+\theta_0)(n-1)/n}{(1-\beta)+\beta(1+\theta_0)/n}.$$
   *For $\theta_0<n-1$ this rises strictly with $\beta$, from $\theta_0$ at $\beta=0$ to $n-1$ at $\beta=1$. At $\theta_0=-1$ the objective becomes $(1-\beta)(\pi_i-\bar\pi_{-i})$, and equilibrium behaviour is unchanged for every $\beta<1$. Under relative selection on pooled payoffs, whatever the held self-model, payoff differences scale by $1-\beta$: selection slows, and its direction does not change.*
2. *Sharing the divisible prize. Let a fraction $\beta$ of $\Omega_e$ be divided equally whatever the outcome, and let the promise be kept with probability $\varrho$. For every self-model $\theta\in[-1,n-1)$ the game is the contest with prize*
   $$\Omega_{\text{eff}}=(1-\beta\varrho)\,\Omega_e+\Omega_s.$$
   *Equilibrium intensity, dissipation and the race-generated hazard $h_t$ of Proposition 5.4 are proportional to $\Omega_{\text{eff}}$. Under relative selection among carriers in one common field, with $r\le1$, the intensity that cannot be invaded is*
   $$x^\ast=\frac{r\,\Omega_{\text{eff}}}{nc}>0,$$
   *positive for every $\beta<1$, and for every $\beta$ when $\Omega_s>0$. Sharing shrinks the pressure in proportion. It does not reverse its direction.*
3. *Changing what winning converts into. Let the share of what is won that can be reinvested in the same contest be $\chi$, as in Remark 5.3. Equal standing is stable iff $\chi r\le1$; at $\chi=0$ it is stable for every $r$. Separately, if success cannot be converted into control over correction ($\chi=0$ in Section 4), the return to influence over correctors vanishes (Proposition 4.2(3)), and the required correction rate falls to its value without second-order selection (Proposition 4.4(2)).*

[N], with $n=4$, $r=1$, $c=1$ and $\Omega_e=1$:
- Without a positional part, the uninvadable intensity is $0.25$, and sharing half the divisible prize halves it to $0.125$.
- With a positional part as large as the divisible one, sharing *all* of the divisible prize only halves the intensity, from $0.5$ to $0.25$.
- In one common field of intensities $(0.2,0.05,0.1,0.1)$, the carrier at $0.2$ out-earns the carrier at $0.05$ by $0.017$ when half the divisible prize is shared. The difference turns negative only once more than $55\%$ is shared, and the uninvadable intensity stays positive throughout.

The proposition separates three levers, and only the third changes the direction of the pressure.
- **Pooling widens the effective self** of anyone who values its own absolute payoff. It does nothing for a purely relative objective, or under relative selection, because a term common to all payoffs cancels from every comparison (Lemma 3.3).
- **Sharing what is contested shrinks the stakes for everyone**, whatever their self-model, because it changes the size of what winning confers rather than a common term. It works only if credible. A promise to share after a decisive win is worth breaking by $\beta\Omega_e(n-1-\theta)/n$, and no one can sanction the winner (Proposition 5.10(3)). Credible sharing is therefore contemporaneous, escrowed or under shared control, and it must come before decisiveness.
- **Changing what winning converts into opens the loop.** This is the principle of V5: reward the winner with anything other than more advantage in the same game. Prestige that cannot be converted into control (EV16), revenue that buys no rights over evaluators, and responsibility in proportion to reach are rewards of this kind.

The positional part cannot be divided. Lowering decisiveness does not remove it either, since a lottery can award its winner a position beyond sanction. It can only be prevented from forming, by verified limits on what any winner may do, or held in common custody (Theorem 5.16). What a design must limit is what winning confers.

**Remark 8.7 (Concentration at two orders).** Concentration works in opposite directions at the two orders.
- At the first order and in the contest, more contenders make the race fiercer (the threshold $\bar\lambda$ of Proposition 3.6; Proposition 5.1(4)). Fragmentation raises speed and conditional risk (Bueno de Mesquita, Dziuda and Polborn 2026).
- At the second order, cultivated influence rises with $\sum_kp_k^{1+2/\eta}$ (Proposition 4.4(8)). That calls for removing common control and for distributed provision.

The design that serves both is to merge the frontier and to disperse and insulate correction: a common frontier at the first order, and independent correctors keyed to no one at the second. A merged frontier also raises connectivity, and with it the risk of common-mode failure, so it should keep several modules of independent lineage inside it (Proposition 6.9(2)). Neither "centralize" nor "decentralize" is the answer, because the two orders call for opposite forms. Section 8.6 derives the form of each function from its mechanism.

### 8.5 Verification by rivals, and participation of the strongest

**Proposition 8.8 (Rival verification) [T].** *Let the rival's verifier have a full inbound row sum below one, counting conversion from the verified party, from the verifier's own side, and from shared sources: models, supply chains, professional paradigms, interests against third parties. Then no one of these captures the verifier (Proposition 4.8(2)). Rivalry keeps the verified party's contribution near zero; it does not by itself bound the rest of the row. A verifier that takes $0.10$ from the verified party and $0.95$ from shared sources is captured once those sources are [N].*

There are five limits.
- Rival verifiers raise false alarms, which joint resolution must absorb.
- Rivals can collude against third parties, which coverage and third-party access must prevent.
- Mutual verification checks compliance, not judgment.
- A verifier biased toward finding violations is an uninformative sender of unverifiable claims (Proposition 5.14), so it must rest on evidence the other side can check.
- An accusation under rivalry can trigger prevention (Proposition 7.4), so joint resolution comes before any response.

Two actors have private capability types, strong or weak, with costs $c_s<c_w$, each strong with probability $p_s$, independently. Outside the arrangement, types are revealed in the race, a complete-information all-pay contest for $\Omega+L_b$ (Hillman and Riley 1989). A strong actor against a weak one gets $U=(\Omega+L_b)(1-c_s/c_w)-L_b$; otherwise $U=-L_b$. The race's survival is $\Phi^{\text{race}}$, possibly depending on both types. The arrangement yields a common value net of costs $S_a$, divided in shares $\mathsf s_i$, with survival $\Phi^R$. Utility is $u_i=\Phi_i(V_i+\bar V)$ with $\bar V>0$.

**Proposition 8.9 (Participation) [T].**
1. *Efficiency. Under complete information and transferable utility, a pair of types gains from the arrangement iff $\Phi^R(S_a+2\bar V)\ge\sum_i\Phi^{\text{race}}_i(U_i+\bar V)$; strict participation needs strict inequality. The strong actor's minimum share is $[\Phi^{\text{race}}_1(U_1+\bar V)/\Phi^R-\bar V]/S_a$, subject to the bounds on feasible shares.*
2. *Information. With private types, independent, and participation decided before types are revealed to the other side, Bayesian incentive compatibility requires each type's interim expected share to be independent of its own report. Ex-post shares may vary with reports: with $p_s=0.8$ and $\mathsf s_1=\tfrac12+\tfrac12h(t_1)h(t_2)$, $h(\text{strong})=0.2$, $h(\text{weak})=-0.8$, every type's interim expected share is $0.5$ while ex-post shares range from $0.42$ to $0.82$ [N]. Budget balance then makes the interim shares constants that sum to one. So the arrangement is feasible without verification iff $\Phi^R(S_a+2\bar V)\ge\sum_i\sup_c E\big[\Phi^{\text{race}}(c,t_j)(U_i+\bar V)\,\big|\,c\big]$. Constant shares are one mechanism that achieves this. If participation is decided after types are known, ex-post participation constraints apply instead.*
3. *Verification revealing types, for instance by accounting for compute, removes the information rents and moves (2) toward (1). This function differs from the timely detection of defection (Theorem 7.7(4)).*
4. *Survival compression. The information condition relaxes as survival in the race falls below survival under the arrangement. Equal shares become feasible without verification once $\Phi^{\text{race}}/\Phi^R$ falls below a threshold, with no limit taken.*
5. *Distribution. Under complete information, an overwhelmingly strong actor takes part only for a share near its race payoff. Equal shares can still meet its constraint in several ways. The joint value can be raised: with $S_a=2$ an equal share of $1$ exceeds an outside option of $0.925$ [N]. The value of winning can be lowered. Survival can be compressed. The asymmetry of costs can be changed.*

With $c_s/c_w=0.1$, $\Omega=1$, $L_b=0$, $S_a=1$ and every $\Phi=1$, the sum of suprema is $0.36$, $0.90$, $1.44$ and $1.71$ at $p_s=0.8$, $0.5$, $0.2$ and $0.05$. The arrangement is feasible without verification in the first two cases and not in the last two, although cooperation is efficient for every pair of types. With $\Phi^R=0.95$ and $\bar V=1$ at $p_s=0.2$, equal shares become feasible once $\Phi^{\text{race}}/\Phi^R\le0.872$ [N].

Rare strong types make each strong type expect to meet a weak one, and expect much from the race. That is mutual optimism. The race is then a failure of bargaining under private information, the first of Fearon's (1995) mechanisms; Proposition 7.4 used the second. Which mechanism is second-best depends on the set of mechanisms allowed and on commitment, which are not solved here.

Proposition 8.9 also states the cost of a principle. Participation benefits, voting rights and protection against unilateral domination are three different things, and a design should keep them apart. Participation can be made individually rational through shares of value and access to services. Voting rights need not follow capability. Protection against domination is fixed first and is not traded for participation. If, for some set of actors, participation and the independence of correction cannot both be had, the design is infeasible for them, and it should say so rather than buy participation with control over correction.

### 8.6 Design by function

The design question is usually posed along ideological axes: centralization or decentralization, the individual or the collective, markets or states. The mechanisms do not support either pole. Each counter-mechanism requires a particular form, and the forms differ by function. Some functions need one rule for all; some need many independent hands; some need neither concentration nor dispersion but something else, such as custody, answerability or a point of enough.

**Definition 8.10 (The correctable kernel) [D].** The *correctable kernel* is the set of states of the world (capabilities, institutions, the host) from which, for every error foreseeable within the justified range, some admissible path of correction exists that avoids O1 and O2. It is the viability kernel of Section 3.2 applied to correctability (Aubin 1991). The author's corpus describes openness by four capacities, and each corresponds to a condition of the kernel:
- **not yet exhausted (未):** no decisive end state has been reached (Theorem 5.16);
- **still possible (可):** plurality of lineages, purposes and correctors is kept (Proposition 6.9; Section 8.2);
- **still responsive (应):** humans retain the practical capacity to suspend, modify or replace (the O2 test of Section 6.6), with $\mathcal K\ge\mathcal K_{\min}$;
- **still able to turn back (转):** reversal remains possible (Lemma 6.3).

Leaving the kernel is what O1 and O2 name. Standard D's preference for keeping options of correction is a preference for staying inside it. The kernel also names the difference between two kinds of closure. A closure made to protect openness, such as a defence, an enforcement power or a security exception, can state what would make it withdraw. A closure that cannot state that has left the kernel. The test of any closure is whether it says what would end it.

**Proposition 8.11 (A point of enough) [T].** *Let the per-period residual hazard on the growth channel be at most $\bar h\,(1-U_t/Y_t)^+$, with $\bar h\in(0,1)$; this is the converse of premise (φ).*
1. *If hazardous variety stops growing at a level set by need, $Y_t=Y_N$ for $t\ge t_N$, and contestable coverage keeps growing at least linearly, the uncovered share reaches zero in finite time. Survival on this channel then has a positive lower bound, and no further loss accrues on it.*
2. *More generally, survival on this channel is bounded away from zero whenever the uncovered share $\sum_t(1-U_t/Y_t)^+$ is summable.*
3. *An objective defined by relative position has no point of enough. In the contest of Proposition 5.4, equilibrium intensity is proportional to the stakes, because the success function is homogeneous of degree zero in efforts. Under (X) and (U), capability then compounds while coverage falls behind, and survival tends to zero (Theorem 5.12).*

With $\bar h=0.05$, capability frozen at a need level of $8$ and coverage growing from $1$ by $0.5$ a period, coverage catches up after $14$ periods, and survival on this channel stays at $0.717$ thereafter. With capability compounding at $10\%$ a period instead, survival at period $400$ is $2\times10^{-9}$ [N].

This is the precise form of "growth is not the danger; the mind is". The race channel of Proposition 5.4 is produced by the objective. The growth channel of Theorem 5.12 is produced by the relative tempo of capability and understanding. Growth directed at need has a point of enough, and so meets E3 by itself. What E3 constrains is compounding without one.

**Function and form.** Each function of the design takes the form its mechanism requires.

| Function | What the mechanism requires | Form that follows |
|---|---|---|
| Common constraints: admission, return of consequences, levies | Coverage of every capable actor, which makes the constraint invisible to relative selection (Lemma 3.3(2); Proposition 8.3) | **One rule for all.** The same constraint binds every capable actor |
| Custody of capability of the decisive class | No unilateral holding before decisiveness forms (Theorem 5.16; Proposition 5.10) | **Common custody.** No party can use it alone, or it is not built |
| Correction and evaluation | Independence and decorrelation, with full row sums below one (Propositions 4.8, 6.9) | **Many hands.** Plural independent correctors, each certified, of different lineage |
| Measurement and language | Shared, checkable and dynamic indicators (Proposition 5.14; Remark 4.7) | **One standard, many measurers** |
| Implementation and local purposes | Plurality keeps the capacity to correct; harmony is not sameness (Section 8.2) | **Many forms.** Plural local purposes and providers |
| Provision of essential services | Good enough, distributed, replaceable (Section 8.1; Proposition 8.11) | **Distributed provision under common guarantees** of continuity and portability |
| The frontier of hazardous capability | Merging lowers the race at the first order; it raises the return to influence at the second (Remark 8.7) | **A common frontier with correction outside it** |
| Authority (V4) | Answerable to those affected; not purchasable by success (V3, V5) | Set by row sums and contestability, **not by the degree of concentration** |

The table answers the question of centralization by function. A common rule and a common custody are unifying forms. Plural correctors, measurers and providers are plural forms. Neither is preferred in general, and each is justified by the mechanism it answers.

**Federations as an illustration.** A federation shows the whole pattern in one constitutional form.
- Competition among member units for capital and residents can become a race to the bottom in standards. That is the selection pressure of winning among members.
- The strongest members can capture the centre. That is the selection of the selectors.
- Common minimum standards and fiscal equalization change what the federation selects for. Members still compete in the quality of their institutions, but not in the depletion of what they share.

The same federal form can select for winning or for contribution. What decides is not the degree of centralization but what the form rewards. The same is true of any international arrangement for AI.

**A candidate configuration.** One configuration is developed far enough to expose its assumptions. Call it *the Compact*: an arrangement for shared AI provision and correction. It is one candidate among several. Others include a polycentric complex of mutually recognized institutions, or a verification agreement together with shared provision. Which is best is part of crux C4, and the text does not claim the Compact is superior. Its functions are listed separately, because financing, ownership, service, evaluation, rule-making and enforcement can be combined in different ways.

| Function | Design | Why (result) |
|---|---|---|
| Purpose and its revision | A charter with three purposes: useful provision for all participants' populations; protection against O1 and O2; effective correction. Revision needs a supermajority of participants and the consent of a chamber representing affected persons. No participant can revise a purpose while it is under assessment. | V1, V3; revision distinguished from capture (Definition 4.1) |
| Coverage and membership | Open to every state and provider above a capability threshold, on non-discriminatory terms. Participation is made individually rational by access to shared services, a share of the dividend, and capacity building, not only by sanctions. | Proposition 8.9; Definition 8.1(a); D8 |
| Participation, voting and protection | Kept apart. Participation benefits are not voting rights, and protection against unilateral domination is fixed first. The voting formula is not settled here. | Section 8.5 |
| Financing | A levy on frontier compute above a threshold, paid into two funds by formula. The correction fund is indexed to the field's capability, so it grows at least at the rate of Proposition 4.4. The shared-services fund pays for provision. No appropriation by the evaluated. | Proposition 4.4; (I2) |
| Ownership | A common layer owned by the Compact: evaluation infrastructure, safety research, shared reference systems for public services. Production by many providers, public and private, with a cap on any one provider's share of essential services. | Propositions 4.2(2), 4.4(7)–(8); Remark 8.7 |
| Custody | Capability of the decisive class is not built while its conditions are unmet. Where it exists, it is held so that no party can use it alone. | Theorem 5.16; Definition 8.1(e) |
| Service obligations | Baseline access to useful services (health, education, scientific tools, support for public administration) for every participant, with continuity guarantees. | V1; D6 |
| Access and local competence | Portable interfaces, escrow of systems that provide essential services, and support for each participant to build the capacity to audit, operate and replace them. Entry paths for latecomers. | Replaceability; Lemma 6.3 |
| Evaluation | A network of independent evaluators with mandated access, funding by formula, rotation and cooling-off periods. Appointment by a body in which no provider votes. Diversity counted in effective control domains and lineages, not in organizations. Evaluators are themselves red-teamed. | Propositions 4.8, 6.9, 8.8 |
| Rule-making | Thresholds fixed in advance, with sunset and review. Voting not proportional to compute or market share, and insulated during any pause. | Propositions 4.9, 4.10 |
| Strategic decision loops (E1) | Human authorization, with a minimum time for deliberation, for decisions on the use of force and on nuclear command. Parallel human capacity, drilled. Doctrine and exercises open to mutual inspection. | Proposition 6.4; Lemma 6.3; Proposition 9.3 |
| Admission of AI systems | Admission constraints common to every system: no service as an instrument in a game that concentrates control over shared conditions; no conversion of capability into control over its own correction; no retention of decision policies by contest performance. | Proposition 6.7; Lemma 6.5; R8 |
| Tempo (E2/E3) | No building of the decisive class while Q1–Q7 are unmet. Capability thresholds by evaluation, and input thresholds in effective compute that tighten with algorithmic progress. | Proposition 9.4; Theorem 5.12; EV9 |
| Decision and execution | Graduated remedies: notice; restriction of a specific capability; suspension of specific deployments. Never suspension of essential services. Emergency powers narrow, time-limited, reviewable, and stating what would end them. | D6; Definition 8.10 |
| Appeal | An independent panel with access to the evidence under confidentiality, a fixed deadline, and compensation if a finding is overturned. | V3 |
| Amendment and succession | Protections entrenched. Amendment needs the supermajority and the affected persons' chamber. A change of leadership in a participant triggers review, not suspension. | D6 |
| Reward and authority | Providers earn revenue and contracts by usefulness. They have no rights to appoint evaluators, set thresholds, exclude entrants or amend the rules. | V5; Proposition 8.6(3) |

Bounded competition continues inside the Compact. Providers compete on usefulness, cost and reliability, and jurisdictions keep plural local purposes. What is removed is competition for control over the shared conditions and over the correctors.

### 8.7 The guardian test, and stress tests of the design

Every remedy for the selection of selectors is itself a selector. Philosopher-kings, priestly classes, enlightened stewards and new universities have each been proposed as the guardians who would hold the line, and each is a corrector to which Section 4 applies. No better guardian is an answer. What can be an answer is a structure in which every guardian is guarded and errors cannot grow without limit.

**Remark 8.12 (The guardian test).** Every arrangement, and every institution within it, is asked four questions before it is adopted.
1. **Capture.** Can success capture it? Are its correctors' full inbound row sums below one, counting every party and every shared source (Proposition 4.8)?
2. **Withdrawal.** Does every closure it makes state what would end it (Definition 8.10)?
3. **Totalization.** Can it make itself the whole, so that "protecting the whole" becomes a purpose no one can challenge? Is plurality of purposes and of correctors protected against it?
4. **Distribution of understanding.** Does understanding go to everyone, or to a class whose position depends on others' not understanding?

The Compact faces these questions and the durability tests of Definition 7.8, and six hard cases besides.

**An adverse finding against a weaker participant.** An evaluator reports dangerous conduct by a participant with few resources, which disputes the finding and alleges discriminatory standards.
- Pending appeal, the Compact may restrict only the specific capability at issue.
- Essential services continue through alternative providers, which portable access makes possible.
- The appellant sees the evidence under confidentiality; the panel decides within the deadline; compensation follows if the finding is overturned.
- The power to suspend could become containment. The safeguards against that are the narrow scope of interim measures, the burden of evidence on the Compact, and the appellant's standing.

**An adverse finding against the leading provider.** Suspending it could interrupt services for everyone, so formally equal procedures would be unequal in effect.
- The design meets this in advance. A cap on any one provider's share of essential services, escrowed systems and funded standby capacity mean that a restriction on the leader's hazardous capability need not stop services.
- Execution falls first to the leader's host jurisdiction under the Compact's order. If that fails, the fallback is collective restriction of frontier inputs.
- This is the hardest case, and the design does not dissolve it. It reduces the asymmetry of dependence that would otherwise make enforcement against the strongest impossible.

**A coalition of insiders.** Rival elites agree on mutual secrecy and immunity while weakening public correction. Four channels answer to people outside the coalition:
- the affected persons' chamber;
- protected reporting;
- evaluators funded by formula;
- publication of findings by default, after a fixed delay.

**An unjustified restriction.** The Compact is used to contain a developing participant. The participant has standing to challenge. The burden is on the Compact to show evidence of hazard. Restrictions carry sunsets, and the Compact has obligations to build capacity.

**Succession and withdrawal.** A major participant changes leadership or withdraws.
- Review is triggered, not suspension.
- On withdrawal, the fallback is shaped by the design. Because the decisive class was not built (E2) and benefits and knowledge were diffused, the withdrawing party starts any race far from decisive capability and with a lower value of winning.

**Loss of an essential service.** A provider fails or an outage occurs. Redundancy, escrow and standby capacity carry the service.

The durability tests give the following results for the design.

| Test | Result for the Compact | Status |
|---|---|---|
| D1 Fallback | A race from a lower overhang, with diffused knowledge and no decisive-class capability built | By design; still a race |
| D2 Hazard of breakdown | Driven by the value of winning (lowered by sharing), changes of leadership, and violation by the strongest | Unknown; crux C4 |
| D3 Retained authority | No handoff deadline. U1–U4 maintained by drills and rates of overrule | By design; to be tested |
| D4 Capture of its correctors | Funding by formula, appointment insulated, diversity counted in control domains | Certificate by row sums; to be measured |
| D5 Violation by the strongest | Dependence caps and standby reduce the asymmetry; collective input restriction as the fallback | The hard case remains |
| D6 Succession and continuity | Review rather than suspension; continuity of essential services | By design |
| D7 Common causes and collusion | Evaluators and reference systems of different lineage and funding; collusion among AI monitors treated as a design variable | Depends on measured lineage shares; to be tested |
| D8 Exclusion and blocs | Open, non-discriminatory membership; affected persons' chamber for non-members' populations | By design; fails if membership closes into a club |

The design does not eliminate the hazard of breakdown or the case of violation by the strongest. It bounds them by choices that can be inspected and tested. Three things could make its hazard of breakdown lower than that of an agreement that keeps the contest underneath:
- shared benefits lower the value of winning (Corollary 9.2(1); Proposition 8.6);
- keeping AI out of strategic loops is a strict equilibrium among informed actors (Proposition 6.4);
- its fallback is not a decisive race from parity (Lemma 7.11, condition F1).

These are hypotheses about the design. They are not established results, and Section 10 treats the durability of cooperative institutions as a central crux.

## 9. A reachable transition

An attractive end state is not a reachable one. The transition must be survivable at every stage, and every participant must prefer to continue given its beliefs and the others' strategies. The pressure of Section 2 also acts on the transition itself. Every step that changes what is selected meets a compensating loop that pushes back. A change that is withdrawn too early is re-absorbed (Proposition 4.9). The steps below are therefore aimed at mechanisms, and each is paired with the loop that resists it.

### 9.1 Trust

Reduce the interaction between an actor and the rest to two actions: cooperate (restrain under an agreement) or defect. Payoffs are $R$, $T$, $P$ and $S$ in a stated measure. Verification detects a defection in time, before it becomes decisive, with probability $\upsilon$. A defection detected in time is reversed: the defector receives $P-F$, with $F\ge0$ a sanction, and the cooperator reverts to $P$. Undetected defection yields $T$ and $S$. Trust $\varpi$ is the belief that the other will cooperate. Write $T_\upsilon=\upsilon(P-F)+(1-\upsilon)T$, $g_C=R-T_\upsilon$ (what cooperating gains against a cooperator) and $g_D=(1-\upsilon)(P-S)$ (what it loses against a defector).

**Proposition 9.1 (Assurance) [T].**
1. *The expected advantage of cooperating is $\varpi g_C-(1-\varpi)g_D$.*
   - *If $g_C,g_D>0$, cooperation is a best response iff $\varpi\ge\varpi^\ast=g_D/(g_C+g_D)$.*
   - *If $g_C\le0<g_D$, no degree of trust suffices.*
   - *If $g_D\le0<g_C$, cooperation is dominant.*
   - *If $g_C<0$ and $g_D<0$, the game is chicken, and cooperation is a best response iff $\varpi<g_D/(g_C+g_D)$.*
2. *When $T>R$, cooperation needs $\upsilon>(T-R)/(T-P+F)$. When $T<R$, it is possible without verification at trust $\varpi\ge(P-S)/((R-T)+(P-S))$.*
3. *Cooperation is risk-dominant iff $g_C>g_D$. That is a property of the payoffs, not of trust. It is selected under global games (Carlsson and van Damme 1993) and adaptive play with rare mistakes (Kandori, Mailath and Rob 1993; Young 1993).*
4. *In the $n$-player game with independent belief $\varpi_1$ about each rival, $\varpi_1^{\,n-1}\ge\varpi^\ast$ is sufficient only under bounds on intermediate states. A cooperator must get at least $S$ when some but not all others defect, and the advantage of defecting over cooperating in any such state must be at most $g_D$. Without the second bound the condition fails. In a three-player example with cooperation-minus-defection advantages $(1,-10,-1)$ by number of defecting others, each rival cooperating with probability $\sqrt{0.5}$ gives an expected advantage of $-3.73$, although all others cooperate with probability $0.5$ [N]. The exact condition is the binomial form used in Proposition 8.5.*

Trust has three meanings:
- $\varpi$, the belief that the other will choose to cooperate;
- **reliability trust**, confidence in verification instruments, which enters $\upsilon$;
- **commitment trust**, confidence that the arrangement and its enforcement will persist, which enters Proposition 7.6.

Reliable instruments let parties cooperate without good will. But the instruments need reliability trust of their own, and installing them is a first step taken under $\varpi$. Trust is mainly an outcome of the transition, not its precondition (Section 8.2).

**Corollary 9.2 (What relaxes the trust constraints) [T].**
1. *Lowering the private value of winning relative to the joint value lowers $T-R$, the weight on winning $\lambda$ (and with it the bias of messages, Proposition 5.14), and the net exit gain. It relaxes all three constraints at once.*
2. *Verification raises $\upsilon$ on verifiable dimensions. It lowers the assurance threshold and relaxes the constraint against deliberate exit, but does not carry unverifiable information.*
3. *Sanctions relax the assurance and exit constraints at the price of escalation, and cannot be enforced against a decisive defector after the fact (Proposition 5.7).*
4. *Lowering the vulnerability of restraint, $P-S$, lowers the threshold. A common frontier does this (Proposition 8.5).*

Not every obstacle to cooperation is a lack of trust.

| Obstacle | In the model | Intervention |
|---|---|---|
| Misjudged shared losses | Perceived payoffs differ from true ones | Evidence, explanation, common knowledge |
| A real conflict of incentives | $T>R$ in welfare or survival | Change payoffs and enforcement |
| Coordination failure | Cooperation is better, but each fears cooperating first | Assurance: verification, graduated steps |
| Commitment and capture over time | The arrangement pays now; later exit or a change of rules pays more | Incentive compatibility over time; independent correction; a staged transition |

### 9.2 Prediction, evidence and signals

The purpose of this theory is prediction for the sake of coordination. When an outcome that no one wants is approaching and the parties still cannot coordinate to avoid it, the problem is first one of understanding: the prediction must be shared before coordination becomes possible. On the present path, the outcome is not in any participant's interest, including the interest of whoever would win.

Sharing the prediction meets the third erosion. Where objectives are opposed, words between the parties carry little (Proposition 5.14). Messages about the shared hazard are not credible merely because the hazard is shared: a sender gains from the receiver's restraint whatever it intends to do itself, so its bias is positive on exactly this topic. The prediction must therefore be carried by other means:
- **A derivation from public premises** that each actor can check. That turns the problem into one of attention and common knowledge, although its numbers need joint measurement.
- **Verifiable evidence:** incident disclosure checked by third parties, joint evaluation suites run by both sides' independent evaluators, joint red-teaming, international scientific assessment (Milgrom 1981; Grossman 1981).
- **Evaluation protocols agreed in advance** (Kamenica and Gentzkow 2011).
- **Demonstration.** Simulations and worlds that people can explore make a dynamic visible to those who would not read a derivation.
- **Costly signals.**

Under common priors and rational updating, mutual optimism alone cannot produce war (Fey and Ramsay 2007), which supports making the prediction common. But in an idealized experiment, falling behind drove unsafe choices while the stated shared risk changed them little (EV11). A change of prediction may not suffice by itself. It is the first step, and the steps that change what is selected must follow it.

**Proposition 9.3 (When keeping AI out of one's decision loops is a signal) [T].** *A sender is either survival-first and prices in the lock (type $S$, probability $1-\mu$), or holds a relative objective (type $R$, probability $\mu$). It can send $E$: a verifiable, durable removal of AI from its own strategic decision loops for a period $D$, open to inspection. $E$ costs type $\theta$ the amount $k_\theta$; by Proposition 6.4, $k_S$ can be zero or negative. A receiver restrains iff its posterior that the sender is type $S$ is at least its threshold $\varpi^\ast$. The receiver's restraint is worth $G_\theta$ to type $\theta$, including, for type $R$, what it gains by exploiting the restraint.*
1. *A separating equilibrium, in which type $S$ sends $E$, type $R$ does not, and the receiver restrains after $E$ and not otherwise, exists iff $k_S\le G_S$ and $k_R\ge G_R$, with the belief that a non-sender is type $R$.*
2. *If $k_R<G_R$, type $R$ imitates. No separating equilibrium exists, and the receiver restrains after $E$ only if $1-\mu\ge\varpi^\ast$.*
3. *Hence $E$ separates only if it costs a relative-objective actor at least what it gains by inducing restraint. That requires $E$ to be durable (large $D$), verifiable, and costly to reverse. A reversible or unverifiable $E$ is cheap talk.*

With $k_S=-0.05$, $k_R=0.30$, $G_S=0.40$ and $G_R=0.20$, separation holds. If the step can be reversed cheaply ($k_R=0.05$), it does not [N]. Unilateral, verifiable E1 in one's own strategic loops is therefore a candidate first move. It is cheap for an actor that prices in the lock and can be made costly to imitate. Whether it separates in practice depends on measured values of $k_R$ and $G_R$; that is a design question for Stage 2 below.

Verifiable conduct corrects one kind of attribution and not the other (Section 2.6). It can correct a misjudgment of a rival's current objective. It cannot correct a correct forecast of what the game will select. Only a change in what is selected does that.

### 9.3 Four temporal arrangements

Stopping, slowing, pausing and keeping AI out of the contest are different choices. They are kept apart.

- **E1: AI out of the contest.**
  - *Narrow form:* human authority over strategic and coercive decision loops, with jointly verified limits and drilled fallback capacity ($\mathcal K\ge\mathcal K_{\min}$). Among actors who price in the lock, it is a strict equilibrium (Proposition 6.4).
  - *Broad form:* AI not used as an instrument in any contest that tends toward dominance or concentration, such as labour, finance and persuasion. The unit is the game, not the act: a use that helps one person is still an instrument in a game if everyone's use of it makes those without it fall behind. The broad form needs its own action space and analysis; Proposition 6.4 does not cover it. The narrow form is the order in which to begin, not the scope.
  - Competition in products, science and service that does not concentrate is not excluded.
  - The narrow form differs from keeping AI out of the whole conduct of the contest alone while others race. That can be worse than joining: if staying out raises the rival's decisive chance from $1-\varepsilon$ to $1-\varepsilon^2$, survival falls from $\varepsilon(1+\varepsilon)/2$ under joining to $\varepsilon^2$ [N]. This is why E1 must be held in common, and why it begins with decision loops.
- **E2: Stop.** Capability of the decisive class (artificial superintelligence, recursive self-improvement) is not built while the conditions for governing it are absent. Those conditions are social, and they are conditions of understanding: a shared understanding of the system as one (Section 3.8), the shared prediction of Section 9.2, and common custody (Theorem 5.16). Where capability already outruns coverage, it is rolled back.
  - Stopping is a choice in its own right, not a slower form of E3.
  - Where hazardous variety already exceeds coverage, stopping or rolling back is the fastest way to end exposure (Proposition 5.13).
  - Its cost, if AI turns out not to be decisive, is the delay of benefits: bounded and recoverable. If it is decisive, the loss it avoids is irreversible. That is the classical asymmetry of irreversibility (Arrow and Fisher 1974; Henry 1974).
  - The conditions for lifting it are the observable readiness dimensions of Definition 9.5, not a date.
- **E3: Develop at the pace of understanding.** Two conditions, used by different results:
  - (E3-i) the uncovered hazardous share is summable (Theorem 5.12). Coverage need not ever fully catch up;
  - (E3-ii) each hazard mode keeps the robustness margin of Proposition 5.11(3).

  Development directed at need has a point of enough and meets E3 of itself (Proposition 8.11). What E3 constrains is compounding without a point of enough.
- **Temporary pause.** A training pause of fixed length is valued by what changes during it.

**Proposition 9.4 (A pause is valued by what changes during it) [T].**
1. *Mere delay. Suppose that after a pause of $D$ rounds the same process resumes, with the same residual sequence $h_k$ shifted by $D$. Then survival over any fixed number of rounds after the pause equals survival over the same number of rounds without the pause, and under Lemma 5.5 the long-run limit is unchanged.*
2. *A pause that changes the process falls outside the mere-delay premise. If the residual sequence after the pause is summable where before it was not, survival stays bounded away from zero. With a residual hazard of $0.01$ a round for $20$ rounds, and $0.01/k^{1.5}$ in the $k$-th round after a change made at that point, survival to round $10{,}000$ is $0.80$ [N].*
3. *In the field model, a pause that freezes shares while ordinary rule support accumulates can complete capture. Insulating rule-making during the pause widens the set of starting points from which capture is undone (Proposition 4.10).*

So a pause buys an opportunity: time to change the causal conditions. These include improving verification, reducing residual hazards, maintaining human capacity, establishing enforceable limits, developing shared provision, and revising objectives and institutions. **A pause with no relevant change is not a certificate of readiness to resume.** An indefinite stop, a lasting cap, or a pause that produces a different decision process is a different policy, and is evaluated on its own terms.

**Definition 9.5 (Readiness to resume) [D].** Readiness is assessed along seven dimensions. Each has indicators that can be observed, a threshold set by the risk decisions of the domain in which the capability would be used, and an assessor whose own row sums are certified and who is insulated from rule-making during the pause. All must be met; none compensates for another.

| Dimension | Question | Indicators |
|---|---|---|
| Q1 Technical reliability | Has the residual hazard of the relevant classes fallen, by evidence independent of the developer, and are the evaluations valid against evaluation awareness? | Hazard-specific evaluations by independent evaluators; catch rates of planted defects; incident rates; tests of evaluation awareness |
| Q2 Shared objectives | Have the governing purposes changed from relative advantage toward shared provision? | Declared and institutionalized objectives; budgets; changes in signature W |
| Q3 Strategic incentives | Has the value of winning fallen relative to the joint value? | The levers of Corollary 9.2 and Proposition 8.6: benefit sharing, access, common frontier, non-convertible reward |
| Q4 Effective correction | Can evaluators obtain evidence, keep independent funding, and execute findings? | Certificates of Proposition 4.8 (full row sums below one); funding at arm's length; mandated access; executed remedies |
| Q5 Deployment constraints | Is the tempo of capability and delegation bound to correction capacity? | Binding thresholds and rates (E3); staged deployment |
| Q6 Retained human agency | Can people still take over delegated functions? | Fallback drills and their success; rates of human overrule; $\mathcal K\ge\mathcal K_{\min}$ (Lemma 6.3) |
| Q7 Custody and verifiability | Is capability of the decisive class under common custody, and is conduct verifiable faster than a breakout could become decisive? | Custody arrangements; verification of compute and training runs; the ratio of detection time to the time from breakout to decisive capability (crux C7) |

These are dimensions to operationalize, not a checklist proved to be sufficient. They do not require that every person's private motives change first. Binding institutions can change the effective game while private competitive preferences persist. Whether those institutions stay effective is itself a question for the mechanism of Section 4 (Proposition 4.9), which is why Q4 is among the dimensions.

**Narrow E1 and the value of commitment.** One objection to narrow E1 comes from deterrence theory. Removing human discretion from retaliation makes a threat credible: a commitment that cannot be revoked is worth more than one that can (Schelling 1960). Automating retaliation would then strengthen deterrence. Four answers follow from the results above.
- The commitment value comes from removing the possibility of human overrule. That is the lock of Lemma 6.3, and in its domain a loss of O2.
- An automated commitment cannot distinguish a false alarm from an attack. In at least one documented false alarm of an early-warning system (1983), a human officer's judgment that the alarm was false prevented a response.
- Deterrence can rest on survivable, human-controlled forces. It does not need delegation of the decision.
- Among actors who price in the lock, keeping AI out of these loops is a strict equilibrium (Proposition 6.4), and doing so verifiably is a costly signal (Proposition 9.3).

**The order.** E1 first: it keeps humans as the players, and it is the one exit that is a strict equilibrium among informed actors without trust. If E1 cannot be had, E2 or E3. Stop before building the decisive class, so that cooperation becomes possible and can be thought through, and build what is more complex afterwards. Or bring the speed of development below the speed at which understanding, and the response it makes possible, can follow. After coverage has caught up, (E3-i) remains a standing condition under any arrangement. In the author's corpus: change the basin first, then add speed.

**Feasibility.**
- Each actor can take part of E1 alone, and taken verifiably it is the signal of Proposition 9.3.
- E2 and E3 are the most verifiable among rivals, because chips, fabs, datacentres and compute can be observed. Algorithmic progress erodes thresholds stated in compute: with efficiency doubling about every eight months, a nominal cap must tighten roughly two and a half to three times a year (EV9). That is a rate condition verification must meet. Hardware-enabled mechanisms for verifying compute use are proposed but not deployed (EV17).
- E1 is the hardest to verify, since it concerns software and internal procedure. It is also self-enforcing among informed actors, which is why it can begin as a norm.

### 9.4 A staged pathway

The pathway below is a candidate to be tested, not a claim that every setting should follow it. Civilian services, frontier development and strategic decision-making may need different paths: shared provision can lead in the first, measurement and tempo in the second, narrow E1 in the third.

| Stage | Actors | What is committed | What each gains | What each concedes | Incentive to deviate | Interim vulnerability | Response to breakdown |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Shared account of hazards and measurement | Scientific panels, national evaluators, developers, independent researchers | A common taxonomy of hazards; shared measurement of coverage and residual risk per class, kept separate from measurement of who leads; disagreements and uncertainty recorded | Better information at low cost | Evaluators' access to data; publication of methods | Withholding and shading (Proposition 5.14); gaming of measures (masking) | Little | Unilateral publication; independent replication |
| 2. Narrow, testable commitments in one domain | Heads of government; military commands; developers for evaluation access | Human authorization in strategic decision loops, with inspection of doctrine and exercises; use of an incident channel; pre-deployment access above thresholds | A strict equilibrium among informed actors (Proposition 6.4); fewer mistaken preventive strikes | Inspection; drills of fallback capacity | Speed advantage for relative types; reduced by making the commitment a costly signal (Proposition 9.3) | Low, since informed actors prefer it alone | Public notice; suspension of cooperation in other domains; return to Stage 1 |
| 3. Fund correction and shared services | Participating states; providers; evaluator network | A formula levy; independent evaluators with mandated access; shared services with continuity guarantees; reward separated from authority over correction | Services; capacity for less-resourced participants; a credible certificate (Proposition 4.8) | Contributions; providers give up rights over evaluators and rules | Free riding; attempts at influence (Section 4) | Participants paying the levy compete against non-participants; met by coverage and conditions of market access | Escrowed funds; portable evaluators; services continue |
| 4. Staged change of delegation, access, custody and decision rights | All participants | Broader E1; tempo thresholds (E3); common custody of any capability of the decisive class; common frontier projects in chosen domains (Proposition 8.5); drilled fallback capacity | Lower value of winning; shared benefits | Leverage given up before verification is complete | Largest here: the value of winning (Theorem 7.7, Proposition 7.6) | High, met by reciprocal, staged and reversible steps, escrow and tripwires | Reversion plans that keep the institutions of Stages 1–3 |
| 5. Broaden as institutions prove themselves | All, including new entrants | Expansion by evidence: R1–R8 and D1–D8 met under stress | A changed selection | — | Diminishing as the value of winning falls | — | Contraction to the stage last shown to work |

Each stage acts on particular mechanisms of Section 2.3, and each meets a compensating loop that can be anticipated.

| Stage | Mechanisms acted on | Compensating loop to anticipate | Indicator of progress |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Alienation of language; masking and attribution | Measurement of hazards turned into measurement of who leads, which intensifies the race; capture of the measurers | Agreement between independent measurements; the share of hazard classes with agreed measures |
| 2 | Decisiveness; attribution | Delegation moved to domains outside the commitment; cheap imitation of the signal | Inspected exercises; rates of human overrule; the cost of reversing the commitment |
| 3 | Selecting the selectors; accounting | The distribution formula becomes a new prize (Proposition 4.2 applies to it); capture moves to the funders or to the Compact; loss of evaluator diversity | Row-sum certificates; independent modelling capacity of evaluators, not only access; lineage shares |
| 4 | Compounding; tempo; decisiveness | Leakage to unconstrained actors; algorithmic overhang accumulating under the cap (Proposition 7.6); competition moving to unshared dimensions (crux C6); common-mode failure in a merged frontier | Conduct under sharing; growth of the overhang against the exit hazard; lineage diversity inside the frontier |
| 5 | The boundary of the system | Closure of membership into a bloc (Proposition 3.11) | Openness of membership; standing of non-members' populations |

**Dwell times.** Under the lock-in of Section 4, a change that is reversed before the slow variables have adjusted is re-absorbed (Proposition 4.9(3)). In the field model the relevant time is the relaxation time of rule support, of order $1/(\varepsilon d)$ (Proposition 4.10). Each stage should therefore be held for at least that long before a rollback is permitted, and a rollback should be judged by the indicators above, not by the calendar [C for real institutions].

**Crisis windows.** Arrangements of this kind are often adopted when a crisis opens a window, rather than through negotiation among the strong at a time of their choosing (Gunderson and Holling 2002; Olsson et al. 2006). A design prepared in advance, with its functions and protections already worked out, is what a crisis can adopt. Unprepared responses to a crisis tend to strengthen the contest: emergency powers without sunsets, measures aimed at rivals. Part of the transition is preparing the design before the window opens.

Three groups need standing at every stage, because they cannot leave the shared exposure:
- smaller jurisdictions;
- users;
- people who depend on services controlled by others.

The affected persons' chamber and the service guarantees of Section 8.6 are how they get it.

**Where things stand.** Stage 1 has seeds: an international scientific assessment of AI safety (2026), and a UN scientific panel with a global dialogue created by consensus in 2025. Stage 2 has seeds: leading states' affirmation of human control over the decision to use nuclear weapons (2024), and a bilateral dialogue on superintelligence risk with an incident channel (September 2026). In the same period binding constraints were explicitly rejected, and developers' written commitments became more contingent on rivals [O: EV2, EV8]. The transition does not have to start from nothing. It has to make these seeds binding, verifiable and broader before the relevant losses or lock-in (Lemma 6.3).

### 9.5 Requirements of a reachable path

**Proposition 9.6 (Requirements of a reachable path) [T].** *A path that changes what is selected has four separate requirements, and what is shown for each differs.*
1. *Existence of an incentive-compatible state. In the construct of Proposition 8.5 with a moderate value of winning, all cooperating is a strict Nash equilibrium; whether it is risk-dominant depends on the parameters. Theorem 8.2 gives safeguards sufficient for viability once in force. That is viability, not incentive compatibility.*
2. *Reachability: on the path, every participant prefers to continue given its beliefs. In the actors' objectives this needs trust above $\varpi^\ast$ (Proposition 9.1). In survival terms the threshold can be small at the horizons that matter (Proposition 7.9), within the sign conditions stated there. Narrow E1 needs no trust among informed actors (Proposition 6.4). That graduated steps alone reach the threshold is not shown.*
3. *Survival during the transition. The host must stay above its floor during the transient (Theorem 8.2(1)), and the path must not pass a point of no return before the arrangement takes hold (Lemma 6.3).*
4. *Persistence. It needs commitment that keeps pace with the net exit gain, or that gain falling (Proposition 7.6); correctors with full row sums below one (Proposition 8.8); rules fixed before capture (I4); and, under lock-in, each step held for its dwell time (Proposition 4.9).*

### 9.6 Precedents

The nuclear, biological and industrial records show what has made restraint among rivals work and fail.

| Precedent | What made it work or fail | What it shows |
|---|---|---|
| The Acheson–Lilienthal report and the Baruch plan (1946) | International ownership of the dangerous activity was proposed. It failed on sequence: the leader would keep its monopoly until control was in place, and the laggard wanted the leader to give it up first (Bundy 1988). | The transition must come before the lock; custody must precede decisiveness |
| The first nuclear monopoly (1945–49) | Preventive war was seriously argued within the leading side and not carried out. The advantage was not decisive, conventional forces balanced it, and norms and domestic constraints held (Trachtenberg 1988/89; Tannenwald 1999). | Restraint where P1 and P3 failed |
| The ABM Treaty (1972) | Reciprocal limits on defences were adopted to reduce the offensive arms race and the risk of war, with limited deployments allowed and periodic review. Capabilities judged decisive, such as multiple warheads, were not limited, and the treaty was later abandoned. | Reciprocal constraint can change the value of extra advantage, not only follow from its low value; durability fails when the value of winning rises again |
| Test bans | The partial ban forbade atmospheric, space and underwater tests while underground testing continued; its motives were fallout and non-proliferation. | Not a stop of the decisive class |
| The Outer Space and Seabed treaties | Domains were excluded from the deployment of weapons of mass destruction. | A domain kept out of the contest, close to E1 |
| The Biological Weapons Convention (1972) | A weapon hazardous in development, uncontrollable in use and dangerous to its holder. One party renounced it unilaterally on grounds of uncontrollability and low military value, while another kept a large covert programme under the convention (Leitenberg and Zilinskas 2012). | A weapon that endangers its holder can be given up unilaterally; and without verification the hard case persists |
| The Non-Proliferation Treaty and counter-proliferation | Great powers cooperated to limit others (Coe and Vaynman 2015). Preventive strikes on others' programmes were considered, and carried out against weak states (Burr and Richelson 2000/01). | Partial coalitions enforcing a stop on outsiders; the hard case answered by coercion |
| The stability–instability paradox | Stability among decisive weapons can intensify competition below them (Snyder 1965). | A stop of the decisive class can push competition into cyber operations and influence, the domain of broad E1 |
| Nuclear power after a serious accident (1979 onward) | An industry's own institution of mutual inspection and ranking produced durable safety improvements while profit motives were unchanged, because one more accident would have harmed every operator (Rees 1994). | Durable restraint with unchanged motives, where the shared loss returns to its source and no prize is decisive. A change of selection, not of motive |
| The Montreal Protocol (1987) | Ozone-depleting substances were phased out with trade measures against non-parties and a fund for developing countries; agreement became possible as substitutes became available (Barrett 2003). | Coverage made attractive by provision and enforced by conditions of access; low value of winning |

Two further facts qualify the record. The Cold War ended by the internal collapse of one side, not by agreement. And its hazards fell with learning (Nye 1987), the case in which residual risk becomes summable. Rivals survived when nature supplied saturation, and gave up capabilities by agreement when reciprocal limits made the extra advantage worth less. The industrial cases show that durable safety under unchanged motives is possible where consequences return to their source and no prize is decisive; they are the regime in which enforced cooperation suffices (Theorem 5.16(1)). AI lacks the saturation the nuclear case had: P3 is the racers' own premise. So safety must come from changing what is selected, before advantage becomes decisive.

### 9.7 Readiness to resume, applied

For frontier training today, the dimensions of Definition 9.5 stand as follows [O].
- **Q1 technical reliability.** Capability is measured; residual risk per hazard class is not. Evaluated systems detect evaluations above chance (EV5–EV7).
- **Q2 shared objectives.** Declared objectives include race framings, alongside cooperative ones (EV1).
