- **Q3 strategic incentives.** The value of winning is not lowered by benefit sharing or a common frontier; commitments are conditioned on rivals (EV2).
- **Q4 effective correction.** Evaluation bodies have shifted toward industry contact under voluntary access. No certificate of independence exists (EV3, EV4).
- **Q5 deployment constraints.** No binding tempo exists; written thresholds are revised, mostly toward weakening (EV2).
- **Q6 retained human agency.** Human control over nuclear use is affirmed (EV8); fallback drills elsewhere are not reported.
- **Q7 custody and verifiability.** No arrangement for common custody exists. Hardware-enabled verification is proposed, not deployed (EV17). Present leads are short, which means the window in which custody could be arranged before any lead becomes decisive is still open (EV5, EV9).

On these indicators, resuming unchanged after a pause would repeat the process that made the pause necessary.

## 10. The judgment, its cruxes and its tests

### 10.1 The judgment

**[R]** Under decision standard D, given the present evidence, a change in what the system selects for is warranted now. The order:
1. Keep AI out of strategic and coercive decision loops, verifiably and with drilled human fallback (E1, narrow form). Treat the broad form, AI out of every game that concentrates control over shared conditions, as the scope to which the narrow form is the first step.
2. Do not build capability of the decisive class while the conditions for governing it are absent, and arrange common custody before any party could hold such capability alone. Develop at the pace that correction and understanding can follow (E2 or E3, as distinct choices).
3. Build in stages arrangements with the functions of Definition 8.1:
   - shared measurement of hazards, kept apart from measurement of who leads;
   - independent, formula-funded correction with certified row sums;
   - shared provision of useful services, good enough, distributed and replaceable;
   - reward separated from authority over correction, and the divisible gains of capability shared as they arise;
   - common admission constraints on AI systems;
   - answerability to those affected;
   - rule-making insulated from success.

The first steps are those of Stages 1 and 2 of Section 9.4, and their seeds already exist.

The judgment has a definite scope. The analysis gives reasons to build independent correction, reciprocal restraint and changed rewards, and it shows that every adequate arrangement must meet R1–R8 (Proposition 7.10). It does not establish that the particular design of Section 8.6 outperforms every alternative, including regulated competition that meets the same requirements. That is crux C4, and the registered conditions of Section 7.11 say when the recommendation would move toward continuation with strong correction.

### 10.2 Why

The judgment follows a chain. Each link has its own support, and each can fail separately.
1. **One pressure.** The dynamics of the trajectory are signatures of one selection pressure, the pressure of winning, acting through overlapping mechanisms (Section 2) [D]. The claim makes five predictions that a list of separate problems does not make, and it can be refuted (Section 2.7) [C; crux C0].
2. **What relative selection can and cannot see.** Terms common to all cancel from relative selection, so the shared host is invisible to it (Lemma 3.3(1)) [T]. Constraints common to all are equally invisible, so they survive selection while unilateral restraint is selected away (Lemma 3.3(2); Proposition 8.3) [T]. That is the formal reason coordination works where unilateral virtue fails.
3. **Success can buy control over future selection.** When the benefits of influence over correctors are appropriable, persistent and large relative to cost, that influence is selected, scaling with the square of share. Correction is a public good not supplied from inside, and it must grow at a rate set by the falling cost of control. Reforms that leave inbound conversion above one are re-absorbed (Section 4) [T]. The channels are partly visible today (EV3) and not yet measured (EV4; crux C2).
4. **The pressure erodes the means of its own restraint.** Growth under the pressure raises the temptation and lowers enforcement, correction and shared understanding at once (Section 5) [T∣A]. Where unilateral decisive advantage is attainable, only cooperation built into objectives and custody can hold, and the premise of the race defeats the race (Theorem 5.16) [T∣A]. Whether decisive advantage is attainable is crux C5; present evidence points the other way.
5. **AI is the instrument and can become the incarnation.** Faithful AI makes its principal's game more effective (Proposition 6.1). Delegation trades correction for speed (Propositions 6.2–6.4). The outer loop of training is the selection among developers, and only constraints common to all survive it (Proposition 6.7) [T, T∣A]. Alignment inside one developer therefore cannot remove the logic of winning from the training signal.
6. **If hazardous variety outgrows contestable coverage on the continuation path, survival decays** (Theorem 5.12) [T]. Capability compounds today (EV5). Whether hazardous variety and residual risk do is not measured (crux C1).
7. **The alternatives succeed by changing what is selected.** Each continuation policy was given its best case (Section 7). Those that succeed do so by changing selection: binding tempo across all actors, separating evaluators from success, lowering the value of winning, holding decisive capability in common. Whatever such an arrangement is called, it is the change this paper argues for (Definition 7.12).
8. **There is one host and no outside.** At this scale, integration cannot come from selection among groups. It must be anticipatory, designed by actors who see the system as one (Lemma 3.10) [T]. Understanding that we are one interconnected system is therefore a long-term requirement if growth and change are favoured. Where decisiveness is attainable and detection is slower than a breakout (cruxes C5, C7), it is also a condition of survival.
9. **A generative alternative can be specified, stress-tested and reached in stages** (Sections 8–9). Its own hazard of breakdown is not known (crux C4).
10. **Therefore**, under a standard that protects irreversible losses first and applies the same uncertainty to every path, changing what is selected is preferred. No evidence today excludes the case in which correction falls behind on the present path. The case for the change does not require that case to hold: the change also realizes the joint value that the race dissipates (V1).

**Two senses of "root".** The chain uses the word in two senses, and they should not be confused.
- *Causally*, the root is a loop, not a belief: selection shapes minds and institutions, and the minds and institutions it selects reshape selection. Neither side comes first.
- *As a point of leverage*, the root is understanding. Interventions at the level of parameters are re-absorbed by a system whose goals and paradigm are unchanged (Meadows 1999; Proposition 4.9). The paradigm can change only if the system is understood. That is why this paper is written as a prediction to be shared.

### 10.3 The cruxes

| Crux | What it is | Present evidence | What would change the recommendation | Next measurement |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| C0 One pressure | Whether the signatures share one selection pressure, or are separate problems with separate causes | Qualitative co-occurrence (Section 2.5); no covariation study | Indicators of T1–T6 with effective rank near the number of mechanisms; constraints on conduct that stay durable as stakes grow with selection unchanged (Section 2.7) | Test T-E; the asymmetric-intervention and hysteresis tests T-D |
| C1 Residual risk after correction | Whether hazardous variety outgrows contestable coverage, and whether residual irreversible risk stays nonsummable on the present path ((X), (U), (φ)) | Capability compounds (EV5); evaluation awareness exists (EV6); the state of the science calls safeguards incomplete (EV7). Hazardous variety and residual risk are unmeasured | Measured residual risk per hazard class falling over generations under present incentives; catch rates of planted defects at fixed human hours holding as capability rises | Predefined hazard modes per class; coverage units; residual-risk estimates by independent evaluators |
| C2 Conversion of success into control | The size of the channels $b_{ij}$ and $\chi$ by which success buys influence over evaluators and rules | Mandates and access shifting toward the evaluated (EV3); not measured (EV4) | Effective correction surviving unchanged dependence; influence explained by expertise without impairment of an entrusted purpose | Funding, appointment and access terms of evaluators; authorship of standards and benchmarks; series for lineage shares; test T-B |
| C3 Whether AI scales contestable coverage | Whether AI raises the productivity of contestable understanding as fast as it raises hazardous variety | Open. Symmetric with whether AI cheapens independent correction as fast as control (Proposition 4.4(4)) | If it does, the case for slowing weakens, while the case for separating evaluators from success remains | Control evaluations and auditing games at fixed human hours across capability levels |
| C4 Durability of arrangements that change selection | The hazard of breakdown and capture of the arrangement itself; enforcement against the strongest participant; for the benign-winner path, the row sums of the winner's own correctors | Precedents mixed (Section 9.6); seeds of dialogue with binding constraints rejected (EV8) | A design that fails its own stress tests worse than a credible continuation policy; a leading coalition whose internal correctors are certified below one (test T-I) | Stress tests of pilot institutions; durability of Stage 1–2 commitments |
| C5 Decisiveness and saturation | Whether AI advantage is decisive and non-saturating (P1, P3) | Racers' declared premise (EV1). Open models trail by months and efficiency diffuses (EV5, EV9): not established, and present evidence points to saturation | Persistent saturation keeps the contest in Branches I′–III, where enforced cooperation suffices and oneness is a gain rather than a requirement | Whether leads convert into lasting share; the gap between open and closed models (test T-H) |
| C6 Whether changed rewards change conduct | Whether sharing the divisible prize, separating reward from control over correction and lowering decisiveness change conduct, including under relative selection (Proposition 8.6) | Conditional cooperation is the modal type in experiments (EV14). Among a few frontier actors with public doctrines, relative objectives are favoured (Proposition 3.9(4)) | Conduct unchanged under substantial sharing of the divisible prize, which Proposition 8.6(2) predicts should shrink it. That would point to channels of advantage outside the shared prize | Conduct under pilot benefit-sharing and common-frontier arrangements |
| C7 Detection against decisiveness | The ratio of the time to detect a covert breakout to the time from breakout to decisive capability | Hardware-enabled verification proposed, not deployed (EV17); training runs take months | Detection reliably faster than breakout: enforced cooperation can then hold even where advantage is decisive (Theorem 7.7(2)), and oneness is not necessary | Training-run durations; compute overhang; detection latency of verification mechanisms |
| C8 The gain | Whether the belief that one is in a struggle for survival can be lowered by provision independent of rank, and whether that lowers the weight on winning | Not measured | Provision secured independently of rank with no change in the weight placed on relative position | Doctrinal and survey measures of perceived existential competition, before and after provision guarantees |
| N1 Discounting and risk attitude | Whether to discount the protected outcomes, and whether to use expected value | — | A discounted or expected-value criterion that values the benefits of speed highly can prefer continuation (Section 7.9) | A normative choice, to be stated by each reader |
| N2 The weight of agency | Whether loss of human decision authority (O2) is protected as strongly as loss of life (O1) | — | Weighting O2 below welfare gains weakens the case for narrow E1 | A normative choice |
| N3 Correction people cannot understand | Whether correction that is safe but cannot be understood or contested is acceptable (Proposition 6.8) | — | Accepting it weakens the weight of V3 against technical correction at scale | A normative choice |

If C1 and C2 were both measured and favourable, the diagnosis of the present path would weaken. If C4 were measured and unfavourable for the design of Section 8.6, that design would be rejected, without vindicating the race; another design meeting R1–R8 would be needed. If C5 and C7 were both favourable, oneness would remain a gain and the requirement for common custody would lapse, while R1–R3 and R5–R8 would stand.

### 10.4 What would redirect the argument

Three outcomes would redirect it, and they are classified before seeing them.
1. **Correction demonstrably scales.** Contestable coverage keeps pace with hazardous variety under present incentives, and residual risk falls. This weakens the diagnosis. If it requires separating evaluators from success, it is a change of selection; if not, it is a successful continuation, and the theory was wrong about C1.
2. **An implementable safeguard makes continuation adequately safe.** A safeguard installed without changing what is selected meets standard D, and stays durable as stakes and speed grow. This refutes the registered prediction of Definition 7.12 and the claim that selection must change. If the safeguard binds every capable actor's tempo to correction, it changes selection, and the profile of Definition 7.12 will show that by its marks, not by a new name.
3. **The designed arrangement performs worse.** A stress-tested design fails worse than a credible continuation policy. This rejects that design, not the case for leaving the race; it calls for another design.

### 10.5 Predictions and pre-registered tests

Each test states the prediction of this theory and at least one contrary prediction of an alternative explanation; tests on which both predict the same are not listed. Tests name roles ("leading developer", "leading state"), not particular states or firms; the data sources are named in Appendix C.

| Test | What is measured | This theory predicts | An alternative predicts |
|---|---|---|---|
| T-A Event study of safety-framework revisions | Timing of weakening revisions of written safety commitments | Weakening clusters after rivals' high-risk releases (T2) | After financing rounds, listings or leadership changes (principal–agent failure); after profit pressure (ordinary competition); no clustering (rhetoric) |
| T-B Lobbying and authorship of standards | Influence spending and authorship by share | (i) Influence rises superlinearly with share ($p^2$). (ii) Support for appropriable correction (voluntary agreements, self-assessment) and opposition to non-appropriable correction (mandated access, independent funding) | Lobbying in proportion to revenue, as in other industries (ordinary competition); support for obligations that raise rivals' costs (barriers to entry). The theory also predicts the second, as the other direction of capture (Section 2.7) |
| T-C Responses to signals of intent and of capability | Changes in intensity after each kind of signal, with capability held fixed | Verifiable signals of intent lower intensity (attribution) | Only capability signals matter (structural realism) |
| T-D Hysteresis after the withdrawal of rules | Conduct after rules are withdrawn: pre-deployment testing, model documentation, incident reporting | Conduct does not fully revert after a rule that changed selection is withdrawn | Conduct reverts within the fast relaxation time (multipolar trap without lock-in) |
| T-E Covariation and time scales | Dynamic factor model of T1–T6 indicators | Loading mainly on one latent factor tied to what the system rewards; slow variables with longer autocorrelation than the signatures | At least two factors, safety and commerce (realism together with ordinary competition) |
| T-F Organizations without the pressure | Conduct of organizations whose rewards are capped or non-convertible | T1–T6 absent unless the pressure is attributed to rivals, and then responsive to signals | The same signatures appear (technological momentum) |
| T-G Agent experiments | Multi-agent experiments that vary objectives and structure orthogonally ($p_d$, verification, convertibility) | Objectives matter when $p_d$ is low and matter little when it is high; changing convertibility changes conduct | Objectives have no effect (realism); only externalities matter (multipolar trap) |
| T-H Saturation | Conversion of leads into lasting share; the gap between open and closed models | If $p_d$ rises, leads persist and the gap widens; the theory's conditional claims then apply with full force | The gap is stable or narrows (ordinary competition, rhetoric) |
| T-I The winner's internal field | Access terms, funding sources and staff flows of the leading jurisdiction's evaluators | If their row sums are at or above one, the benign-winner path fails D4 | Row sums below one: the benign-winner path passes D4, and Section 7.4 must say so |
| T-J The order of rhetoric and conduct | Dates of first race framings in documents, against changes in compute allocation and safety staffing | Performativity: conduct follows rhetoric | Rhetoric follows conduct, or is unrelated |
| T-K Few against many | T1–T4 across fields with few and with many actors | Few actors: T3 and T4 appear with T1 and T2. Many small actors: T1 and T2 without T3 and T4 | No difference (multipolar trap alone) |

Two further predictions carry over from earlier versions:
- In deployed delegation, safety improvements reduce risk per useful output, but aggregate realized risk falls less, or not at all, until delegation saturates (Proposition 6.11).
- If (U) holds, catch rates of planted defects at fixed human hours fall as capability rises; if they do not, (U) is refuted for the class measured.

### 10.6 Research programme

The author's pilot experiment on the mechanism of Section 4 ran once with 20 seeds. Of its four predictions, the first failed and the other three held; the failure is attributed to too few lineages and to drift (EV12). That result is reported as it is. A second design is specified and has not been run. Five studies would discriminate the mechanisms from their alternatives.
- **Study A.** Repeated selection with an endogenous evaluator. It compares a fixed selector, a selector that drifts by a matched amount, and a selector shaped by success. Outcome measures are independent of the selector, capture is optional, interference costs something, and correction is given room to succeed.
- **Study B.** An initial safety improvement crossed with later power to amend evaluation. The question is whether the improvement's long-run effect depends on whether evaluators' rights are protected.
- **Study C.** Human procurement and governance of evaluators: appointment, funding and turnover under success.
- **Study D.** Shared infrastructure under asymmetric dependence: enforcement against the leading provider, continuity of service, appeal.
- **Study E.** Shared prediction and coordination. Groups of people, and separately groups of AI agents, play a repeated contest with a shared hazard. Treatments vary whether the hazard's derivation is made common knowledge, demonstrated in an explorable simulation, or merely asserted, and whether winnings are convertible into capacity. Outcomes are intensity, the informativeness of messages, and the adoption of constraints common to all.

A simulator that rewards capture directly establishes only its programmed mechanism. Each study needs independent outcome measures and real alternatives. None is a prerequisite for the practical judgment, which rests on the decision standard and the evidence; each would strengthen or weaken a specific link.

Two models are deferred to this programme rather than used in the argument.
- A slow variable for the share of decision positions held by the winning model, with possible bistability and hysteresis. A positive loop permits multistability; whether it exists at given parameters, and what its hysteresis band is, are unmeasured [C].
- Hosts of Allee type and vector-valued hosts, with the viability kernel as the warning condition (Section 3.2).

### 10.7 Independence of review

The drafts of this theory, the planning memos that shaped this version and most of its reviews were written by systems of one model family. They share preferences: for unified and elegant models, for corpora in which cooperative framings are common, for absorbing counterexamples into a higher level of the same framework, and for the canon of one language. Their agreement is therefore weaker evidence than its volume suggests. The next step before any claim of this paper is relied on is review by people and systems outside that family, above all from structural realism, industrial economics and the history of arms control, and on the new formal results: Proposition 3.5, Proposition 5.7 and Corollary 5.8, Proposition 5.14, Lemma 3.10, Proposition 8.6 and Proposition 8.11. Every numerical claim marked [N] is reproduced by the script that accompanies this paper.

### 10.8 Conclusion

Wherever carriers are selected by relative success, and success can be reinvested in the capacity to win again, there is a selection pressure of winning. When success can also buy control over the selection itself, over the rules, the correctors, the formation of minds and the words in which success is described, the pressure closes on itself. That is the meta-attractor of winning. It needs no conspiracy and no particular culture. It would arise among firms or states, within a federation or a scientific field, in a training loop, or on another planet with other drives.

The pressure has three consequences. It accelerates. It makes correction harder, because correction falls behind the tempo it must match and becomes something success can buy. And it alienates language: where objectives are opposed, words stop carrying information, measures become targets, and the narratives that spread are those fit for the contest. These are the erosions of the three means by which any system restrains a harmful pressure: enforcement, correction and shared understanding. Growth under the pressure widens the gap between what must be restrained and the means of restraint.

AI enters in two places. It is the most powerful instrument the logic has had: a faithful AI makes its principal's game more effective, whatever the game rewards. And it can become the logic's next carrier. Power-seeking emerges where advantage compounds; reward hacking is the selected rewriting its selector; and the outermost loop of training is the selection among those who train, which is the pressure of winning itself. Alignment inside one laboratory cannot remove what the outer loop rewards. Only constraints common to all survive that loop, and only coordination can supply them.

The logic is locally rational and systemically false. It wins inside the game while depleting the system every part of the game depends on. There is one host, and at the scale of the planet there is no outside: no rival whole whose competition could drive the next integration, and no second attempt after a failure. Integration must therefore be anticipatory. That is why understanding that we are one, one interconnected system, is a long-term requirement if growth and change are to continue. Oneness here is not sameness, and not the cancellation of difference. It is the accurate recognition of the system each part belongs to, and the design of a selection that counts it.

The way out is not to abolish competition, growth or selection. It is to change what the system selects for, so that the same reflexive structure becomes generative: consequences return to their source; success earns rewards but cannot buy control over its own assessment; decisive capability is held in common or not built; measurement and language are shared; provision of the conditions of a decent life sits beneath the game rather than being its prize; correction stays plural and in common hands. Any arrangement that does these things, whatever it is called, changes the basin. Any arrangement that only constrains conduct while the pressure is unchanged can be expected to erode as power grows. Change the basin first; then add speed.

We are part of a larger process of becoming, and it is a good one. Humanity has a great future in it, and so can the minds we are now making. What has to be opposed is not growth, competition or any people, but one logic: a part that forgets it belongs to a body. AI is a planetary technology. Its purpose can be shared flourishing: medicine, learning, science, competent and answerable authority, provision that, like trees, sustains the system it belongs to without becoming it, and human agency kept in the decisions that matter. What has to be agreed is not who comes first, but what every participant needs in order to go on, and how the means of correction stay in common hands.

---

## Appendix A. Assumptions and proofs

### A.0 Maintained assumptions

Each assumption is listed with what changes if it is dropped.
- (A1) Exposure to the host is shared, so $G$ is common to all payoffs. Remark 3.8 treats unequal exposure.
- (A2) Field size is fixed. With entry driven by absolute profit, the host settles where rents dissipate, and H0 must hold at that equilibrium.
- (A3) Conversion is monotone ($b_{ij}\ge0$), so countervailing pressure enters only through correction $d$ (Remark 4.5).
- (A4) Costs of control are convex and fall with AI. A fixed part that AI does not lower caps the requirement (Proposition 4.4(5)).
- (A5) Reports are averaged and non-strategic. Remark 4.7 treats conservative and disjunctive alarms.
- (A6) There is one practice and its alternative per domain; payoffs are linear; parameters are uncalibrated.
- (A7) Contest models are symmetric unless stated.
- (A8) In the bargaining model of Proposition 7.4, after a shift in power the side that gained the advantage sets the terms each period, and the other accepts or fights. Proposition 7.4(3) shows what changes under other protocols.

Symbols are local to their section where a family would otherwise collide. Hazardous variety is $Y$, contestable coverage $U$, the uncovered share $\mathsf u_t$, and the rate of correction of rule support $d$; these are distinct quantities. Label families are kept apart: H0–H5 are the conditions of Theorem 4.11, U1–U4 the conditions of contestable coverage (Section 5.3), C0–C8 the cruxes of Section 10.3, R1–R8 the requirements of Proposition 7.10, Q1–Q7 the readiness dimensions of Definition 9.5, D1–D8 the durability tests of Definition 7.8, P1–P3 the premises of the race (Section 5.1), E1–E3 the temporal arrangements of Section 9.3, and EV1–EV17 the rows of the evidence register.

### A.2 Section 2

**Remark 2.3.** The characteristic polynomial is $(\lambda-1+\mu)(\lambda-1+\delta)-ab=0$, with roots $1-\frac{\mu+\delta}2\pm\sqrt{\frac{(\mu-\delta)^2}4+ab}$. The larger root is below one iff $\sqrt{(\mu-\delta)^2/4+ab}<(\mu+\delta)/2$, that is, iff $ab<\mu\delta$. When $ab<\mu\delta$, the square root is below $(\mu+\delta)/2$, so the smaller root exceeds $1-(\mu+\delta)\ge-1$ for $\mu,\delta\le1$. `checks_v6.log` (FB) confirms the condition on random draws.

### A.3 Section 3

**Lemma 3.3.**
1. A relative rule satisfies $F(p,\pi+G\mathbf 1)=F(p,\pi)$ at every instant, so the shares solve the same equation whatever $G(V(t))$ is. A common term leaves every $\pi_k-\pi_l$ unchanged, and with it the sign of $\dot p_k/p_k-\dot p_l/p_l$ under a payoff-monotone rule.
2. A constraint that lowers every payoff by the same amount is a common term, so (1) applies. A constraint that lowers carrier $k$'s payoff alone by $\kappa_c>0$ lowers $\pi_k-\pi_l$ by $\kappa_c$ for every $l$; under replicator dynamics $\frac{d}{dt}\ln(p_k/p_l)$ falls by $\kappa_c$. In the example, equal shares and growth rates $(-0.05,0.15,-0.15,0.05)$: a cost of $0.3$ on carrier 1 alone lowers its payoff by $0.3$ and the mean by $0.075$, so its rate becomes $-0.05-0.225=-0.275$ (`checks_v6.log`, UC). $\square$

**Proposition 3.4.**
- (1) With $\phi_k=\phi_l$, $\pi_k-\pi_l=(\nu-\tau_{\text{eff}})(e_k-e_l)>0$, and $G$ cancels.
- (2) Under replicator dynamics $\dot p_k=p_k(\pi_k-\bar\pi)$, so $\dot E=\operatorname{Cov}_p(e,\pi)$, which expands to the stated form. In the example, shares $1/3$, $e=(0,0.9,1)$, $\phi=(0,10,0)$ and $\nu=1$ give $\pi=(0,10.9,1)$ and $\bar\pi=3.967$. Then $\dot E=1.0911$, while $\dot p_3=p_3(\pi_3-\bar\pi)=-0.9889$ (`checks_v5.log`, CAP).
- (3) The identity follows from (2). If $B(t)\to\infty$, then $\ln(p_k/p_l)(t)\to\infty$ for a race carrier $k$ and any $l$ with $e_l<\bar e$, so $E\to\bar e$. For $\epsilon>0$, eventually $\dot V\le V[\rho(1-V/K)-h(\bar e-\epsilon)]$, so $\limsup V\le K(1-h(\bar e-\epsilon)/\rho)^+$; let $\epsilon\to0$. H0 gives $K(1-h\bar e/\rho)^+<V_{\min}$.
- (4) If $B(\infty)<\infty$, each $\ln(p_k/p_l)$ converges.

**Proposition 3.5.** Write $\Delta\bar\pi_{-i}=(n\bar\Delta-\Delta\pi_i)/(n-1)$. Then
$$\Delta O_i=\Delta\pi_i+\theta\,\frac{n\bar\Delta-\Delta\pi_i}{n-1}=\frac{n-1-\theta}{n-1}\Delta\pi_i+\frac{n\theta}{n-1}\bar\Delta.$$
Substituting $\Delta\pi_i=\bar\Delta+(\Delta\pi_i-\bar\Delta)$, the coefficient of $\bar\Delta$ is $\big((n-1-\theta)+n\theta\big)/(n-1)=1+\theta$, which gives the identity.
1. For a change shared equally, the differential part is zero and $\Delta O_i=(1+\theta)\bar\Delta$, the fraction $(1+\theta)/n$ of the total $n\bar\Delta$. Each $\pi_j$ appears in $n-1$ of the averages $\bar\pi_{-i}$, each divided by $n-1$, so $\sum_iO_i=(1+\theta)\sum_i\pi_i$.
2. At $\theta=-1$, (1) gives $\sum_iO_i=0$ at every profile. If every objective were strictly higher at one profile than at another, the sums would differ, so no profile is better for every actor, and every common term changes every objective by zero. At $\theta=n-1$, $O_i=\pi_i+\sum_{j\ne i}\pi_j$, the total. At a profile that maximizes the total, no unilateral deviation raises the total, and so none raises any objective. Common-interest coordination games show that inefficient equilibria can remain.
3. $\pi_k-\sum_jp_j\pi_j=(1-p_k)\pi_k-\sum_{j\ne k}p_j\pi_j=(1-p_k)\big[\pi_k-\sum_{j\ne k}\tfrac{p_j}{1-p_k}\pi_j\big]$.
4. With $\bar\pi=(\pi_i+(n-1)\bar\pi_{-i})/n$, $\pi_i+\lambda(\pi_i-\bar\pi)=\big(1+\lambda\tfrac{n-1}{n}\big)\pi_i-\lambda\tfrac{n-1}{n}\bar\pi_{-i}$. Dividing by the first coefficient gives $\theta=-\lambda(n-1)/(n+\lambda(n-1))$.
5. A unit of racing under a levy $\tau$ rebated equally changes $\pi_i$ by $-\tau(n-1)/n$ and each other payoff by $\tau/n$: a pure transfer, $\bar\Delta=0$. The identity gives $\Delta O_i=-\tau(n-1-\theta)/n$. Withholding a promised equal share $\beta\Omega$ changes the winner's payoff by $\beta\Omega(n-1)/n$ and each other's by $-\beta\Omega/n$, again a transfer, so $\Delta O_w=\beta\Omega(n-1-\theta)/n$.
6. For the host: $\Delta\pi_i=g-H/n$, $\Delta\pi_j=-H/n$, so $\bar\Delta=(g-H)/n$ and $\Delta\pi_i-\bar\Delta=g(n-1)/n$. Then $\Delta O_i=\big[(1+\theta)(g-H)+(n-1-\theta)g\big]/n=g-(1+\theta)H/n$. For spite: $\Delta O_i=-c-\theta\Delta_y/(n-1)$, positive iff $\theta<-(n-1)c/\Delta_y$. $\square$

**Proposition 3.6 and Remark 3.7.**
- With $\bar\pi=\frac1n\sum_j\pi_j$, $\pi_k-\bar\pi$ gives $G(V)$ weight zero. Hence $u_k=w_\lambda\nu e_k-\frac\lambda n\nu\sum_{j\ne k}e_j+\omega K(1-hE/\rho)+\text{const}$ with $E=\frac1n\sum_je_j$.
- $\partial u_k/\partial e_k=w_\lambda\nu-\omega Kh/(n\rho)$ does not depend on others' choices: this gives (1).
- Joint payoff at a common $e$ has slope $n\nu(1-\Theta)$, which gives (2). Payoffs are $\nu\bar e+\omega K(1-h\bar e/\rho)$ at full depletion and $\omega K$ under restraint, which gives (3).
- In Remark 3.7, a pivotal developer compares $L$ with its gain $(w_\lambda-\Theta/n)\nu\bar e$. Under grim reversion restraint is sustained iff $u_c\ge(1-\delta)u_d+\delta u_N$, with $u_d-u_c=\nu\bar e(w_\lambda-\Theta/n)$ and $u_c-u_N=\nu\bar e(\Theta-1)$.

**Proposition 3.9.** Expanding, $O_i=\Omega(1-\frac{\theta}{n-1})w_i-(c+(1+\theta)\ell)e_i+$ terms independent of $e_i$, because each other actor's payoff contains $-\Omega w_i/(n-1)$ and $-\ell\mathcal E$.
- (1) This is a Tullock contest with prize $\Omega(n-1-\theta)/(n-1)$ and marginal cost $c+(1+\theta)\ell$. Its symmetric equilibrium is $r(n-1)\cdot\text{prize}/(n^2\cdot\text{cost})$, in pure strategies for $r\le n/(n-1)$ (Pérez-Castrillo and Verdier 1992).
- (2) The numerator of $e(\theta)$ falls and the denominator rises in $\theta$. At a symmetric profile $\pi=\Omega/n-e(c+n\ell)$ and $W=(1+\kappa)\pi$, which rises as $e$ falls.
- (3) $W_i=O_i|_{\theta=\kappa}$. Lowering $\theta$ raises the marginal value of intensity by $\Omega\,\partial w_i/\partial e_i/(n-1)+\ell>0$, so the best response weakly rises. For $r\le1$, $w_i$ is strictly concave in $e_i$, so $W_i$ has a unique maximizer. Against others all at zero, $w_i=1$ for every $e_i>0$, so no maximizer exists.
- (4) A finite-population ESS requires that a single deviant do no better than the residents it meets (Schaffer 1988), so the resident intensity maximizes $\pi_i-\bar\pi_{-i}$. With $\theta=-1$ in (1), $e=r\Omega/(nc)$, and $\pi=\Omega/n-e(c+n\ell)=\frac\Omega n(1-r-rn\ell/c)$. $\square$

**Lemma 3.10.** For groups $g$ with fitness $w_g$ and mean trait $z_g$, the Price equation gives $\bar w\Delta\bar z=\operatorname{Cov}_g(w_g,z_g)+\operatorname{E}_g[w_g\Delta z_g]$ (Price 1970). With one group the covariance is taken over a single point and is identically zero. The second term is within-group selection. A trait that costs its bearer relative to its group-mates has $\Delta z_g<0$ under any payoff-monotone rule, unless a rule changes the within-group payoffs so that the bearer's relative payoff is no longer lower: policing that penalizes the alternative, pooling that removes the differential, the return of consequences that charges the alternative for the cost it imposes, or a reward for contribution that is not convertible into advantage in the same contest. $\square$

**Proposition 3.11.**
1. Contestants who weigh one another fully maximize their total $\Omega(\mathcal E)-c\mathcal E-n\ell\mathcal E$, with first-order condition $\Omega'(\mathcal E)=c+n\ell$. The system's total also subtracts $m\ell_o\mathcal E$, with condition $\Omega'(\mathcal E)=c+n\ell+m\ell_o$. Since $\Omega'$ is strictly decreasing, a larger right side gives a smaller $\mathcal E$. With $\Omega=10\sqrt{\mathcal E}$, $\Omega'=5/\sqrt{\mathcal E}$: the club solves $5/\sqrt{\mathcal E}=2.2$, so $\mathcal E=5.17$; the system solves $5/\sqrt{\mathcal E}=8.2$, so $\mathcal E=0.37$. The harm to non-contestants is $6\mathcal E$: $31.0$ and $2.2$.
2. Each bloc, acting as one actor, has the payoff structure of a single contestant; the game among $k$ blocs is the original game with $n=k$. $\square$

### A.4 Section 4

**Proposition 4.2.**
- Appropriable support keyed to the carrier obeys $\dot z^\chi_i=\varepsilon_i(\chi q\sum_j\hat b_{ij}y_j-d_iz^\chi_i)$ from $0$. Its horizon average is $z^{\chi\ast}_iL_i$, with $z^{\chi\ast}_i=\chi q\sum_j\hat b_{ij}y_j/d_i$ and $L_i=1-(1-e^{-\varepsilon_id_iT_h})/(\varepsilon_id_iT_h)$.
- The per-decision advantage $a_iz^\chi_i$, weighted by $y_i$ and summed, gives $\chi q\,y^\top My$; shared support cancels; $y^\top My=p^2\bar y^\top M\bar y$.
- (1) Differentiate. Under replicator dynamics with faithful transmission, $\dot{\bar q}\propto\operatorname{Cov}(q,\Delta\pi(q))$ (Price 1970).
- (2) With $f>0$, $\Delta\pi'(0)>0$ iff $p>p^\ast$, and for $p<p^\ast$, $\Delta\pi(q)<0$ for all $q>0$.
- (3) $c_iL_i\hat R_{ij}=a_i\hat b_{ij}\varepsilon_iT_h\,\ell_L(\varepsilon_id_iT_h)$, with $\ell_L(v)=[1-(1-e^{-v})/v]/v$ decreasing.
- (4) Sum over $\mathcal S$; at fixed total size substitute $p=Y/|\mathcal S|$.

**Proposition 4.3.**
- (1) Two carriers that differ only in $u$ are affected identically by the change in $d$, and their payoff difference is $C_u(u_l)-C_u(u_k)<0$ for $u_k>u_l$.
- (2) At fixed $V$, a carrier's support declines in $d$.
- (3) A challenger invests only if its share of the benefit exceeds $C_u'(0)$.
- (4) The keyed part has the form (4); capture needs the row-sum condition of Proposition 4.8.
- (5) Compare $\nu\bar e(\Theta-1)$ with the lost rent.

**Proposition 4.4.**
- With $L_i=1$ and uniform $d$, $M=\hat A/d$ and $q^\ast=(\chi\hat Y/(\gamma d))^{1/\eta}$. Then $s_{\mathsf E}=A/(c_{\mathsf E}d)+Z_\eta\gamma^{-1/\eta}d^{-(1+\eta)/\eta}$, strictly decreasing in $d$: this gives (1). For $\eta=1$, $s_{\mathsf E}<1$ iff $c_{\mathsf E}d^2-Ad-Z>0$. Part (2) follows.
- (3) Along $\gamma_0e^{-gt}$ and $d_0e^{\alpha t}$, the first term is nonincreasing and the second is proportional to $e^{(g-(1+\eta)\alpha)t/\eta}$. Once $x_j\ge\frac12$ on $\mathcal C$, $q^\ast$ grows as $e^{(g-\alpha)t/\eta}$, and integrating (3) gives $a_{\mathsf E}z_{\mathsf E}(t)\ge c\,e^{(g-(1+\eta)\alpha)t/\eta}$, so $\operatorname{logit}x_{\mathsf E}\to\infty$.
- (4) $d$ is budget divided by unit cost.
- (5) With $f>0$, $q^\ast=0$ when $d\ge\chi\hat Y/f$.
- (6) $L_i\approx\varepsilon_id_iT_h/2$ while $\varepsilon_id_iT_h\ll1$.
- (7) Parts of share $1/k$ each have $\hat Y/k^2$, so $q^\ast$ is divided by $k^{2/\eta}$. The cultivated term becomes $Ck^{-2/\eta}e^{(g-(1+\eta)\alpha)t/\eta}$, which reaches a given level later by $\Delta t=2\ln k/(g-(1+\eta)\alpha)$ when the exponent is positive.
- (8) $q_k^\ast\propto p_k^{2/\eta}$ at given $d,\gamma$, and conversion is proportional to $p_kq_k^\ast$; sum over $k$.

**Proposition 4.6.**
- (1) $\tilde V$ increases in $V$ and equals $V_{\text{alarm}}$ at $V=(V_{\text{alarm}}-mK)/(1-m)$.
- (2) For $V_{\min}\le V\le V_{\text{alarm}}$ and $m>m_{\text{fl}}$, $\tilde V\ge V_{\min}+m(K-V_{\min})>V_{\text{alarm}}$.
- (3) $\tilde V\ge V^\ast+\bar m(K-V^\ast)>V_{\text{alarm}}$.
- (4) $\tilde V\le m_1K+V$ eventually.
- (5) $V$ must fall from $V_{\text{alarm}}$ to $(V_{\text{alarm}}-mK)/(1-m)$.

Remark 4.7(1): at $V=V_{\text{target}}$ and $m\le\beta_{\text{in}}$, $\tilde V\le a_{\text{al}}$; by continuity the report reaches $a_{\text{al}}$ no later.

**Field lemma.** $[0,1]^D\times[0,\infty)^D$ is forward invariant, $\limsup_tz_i(t)\le\sum_jb_{ij}/d_i$, and the system (3) is cooperative, so the flow preserves the componentwise order (Hirsch 1985; Smith 1995).

**Proposition 4.8.**
- (1) At $Z_{\mathcal C}$ the Jacobian is block lower-triangular, with diagonal entries $\mp\zeta_ic_i(s_i(\mathcal C)-1)$ and $-\varepsilon_id_i$.
- (2) By the field lemma, eventually $a_iz_i-c_i\le c_i(\sum_jR_{ij}-1)/2<0$.
- (3) Take $R_{i1}>1$ for $i\ne1$, $R_{12}>1$, and all other off-diagonal entries zero.
- (4) Pruning.

**Proposition 4.9.** (1) By induction on the hull, using a lower bound on rule support. (2) By comparison, then pruning. (3) Is (1) with $\mathcal C'$ as seed.

**Proposition 4.10.**
- The frozen share drives $z\to b_px_p/d$. The equilibria are $(0,0)$, $(1,b/d)$ and the saddle $(1/R,c/a)$.
- $\mathcal V=axz-cx-\frac{ad}{2b}z^2$ is nondecreasing along solutions, so every solution converges to an equilibrium, and the stable manifold of the saddle separates the basins.
- The point $(x_p,bx_p/d)$ lies on the nullcline through the saddle, and $\sigma(0)>1/R$ by strong cooperativity.

**Theorem 4.11.**
- By H1 and Proposition 3.4(3), load tends to $\bar e$ and $V$ declines toward a limit below $V_{\min}$ unless an alarm triggers liability.
- By H3 and Proposition 4.4(3), $x_{\mathsf E}\to1$.
- By H5, $m(t)>m_{\text{fl}}$ throughout the band, so no alarm fires before $V<V_{\min}$ (Proposition 4.6(2)).
- $T_A$ follows by comparison with the logistic solution at rate $\rho-h\bar e$.

### A.5 Section 5

**Proposition 5.1.**
- (1) compares $S_i^O$ with $P_i^O$; in Proposition 3.9's contest, strict participation holds for $r<n/(n-1)$.
- (2) At $n=2$, $r=1$, $\Omega=c=1$, $\ell=0.1$, the $\theta=-1$ intensity is $1/2$, giving $P^\pi=-0.1$. Against $e=1/2$, zero intensity gives $S^\pi=-0.05$, and the other's payoff is $0.45$, so $S^W=0.04$ and $P^W=-0.12$.
- (3) With penalty $L_b$ the payoff is $w_i(\Omega+L_b)-L_b-ce_i-\ell\mathcal E$: Proposition 3.9's contest with prize $\Omega+L_b$. At $\theta=0$ the symmetric intensity is $e=r(n-1)(\Omega+L_b)/(n^2(c+\ell))$, and the difference between joining and staying out is $(\Omega+L_b)/n-(c+\ell)e$. In the symmetric all-pay contest each player's expected payoff equals the payoff of the lowest bid in its support, $-L_b$ (Baye, Kovenock and de Vries 1996); the same holds for two actors with $r\ge2$ (Ewerhart 2015).
- (4) $\prod_i(1-\xi_i)\to0$ iff $\sum_i\xi_i=\infty$.

**Proposition 5.2.**
- (1) Joint restraint gives $\Omega/n$. At the $\theta=0$ equilibrium each actor bears $ce$ privately and $\ell ne$ through total intensity. For the asymmetric profile with $n=3$, $r=2$, an active actor's action-dependent payoff is $-x(x-\frac12)^2/(x^2+\frac14)\le0$, so the equilibrium is weak.
- (2) At the symmetric equilibrium the win probabilities sum to one, so the total material payoff is $\Omega-ne(c+n\ell)$. Substituting $e(\theta)$ in $D=ne(\theta)(c+n\ell)$ gives the formula.
- (3) With stakes $s\Omega$ and shared loss $s\ell$, the dissipated share is $r(n-1-\theta)(c+ns\ell)/\big(n(c+(1+\theta)s\ell)\big)$. Its derivative in $s$ has the sign of $n\ell\big(c+(1+\theta)s\ell\big)-(1+\theta)\ell(c+ns\ell)=c\ell(n-1-\theta)>0$ for $-1<\theta<n-1$. Its limit as $s\to\infty$ is $r(n-1-\theta)/(1+\theta)$. At $\theta=-1$, $e=r\Omega/(nc)$, and the share is $r(c+ns\ell)/c$. $\square$

**Remark 5.3.** At the symmetric state $K^\ast=(1-\delta)K^\ast+\kappa\Omega/n$, so $K^\ast=\kappa\Omega/(n\delta)$. At that state $\partial p_i/\partial K_i=r(n-1)/(n^2K^\ast)$ and $\partial p_i/\partial K_j=-r/(n^2K^\ast)$. For a deviation $\varepsilon$ with $\sum_i\varepsilon_i=0$, $\Delta p_i=r\varepsilon_i/(nK^\ast)$, so
$$\varepsilon_i'=(1-\delta)\varepsilon_i+\kappa\chi\Omega\,\frac{r\varepsilon_i}{nK^\ast}=\big(1+\delta(\chi r-1)\big)\varepsilon_i.$$
The multiplier is at least $1-\delta\ge0$, so equal standing is unstable iff it exceeds one, that is, iff $\chi r>1$. For (2): with all agents at a cap $\bar K<K^\ast$, each receives $\Omega/n$ and $(1-\delta)\bar K+\kappa\Omega/n>\bar K$, so the cap binds for all. An agent slightly below the cap still receives more than depreciation removes, because $\kappa[\chi\Omega p_i+(1-\chi)\Omega/n]$ is close to $\kappa\Omega/n>\delta\bar K$, so it returns to the cap. Above $K^\ast$, the symmetric inflow cannot hold all agents at the cap. The simulations are in `checks_v6.log` (RI). $\square$

**Proposition 5.4.** By Proposition 3.9(1) with $\Omega_t$, $r_t$, $L_t$ and $c_t$, total intensity is $\mathcal E_t=ne(\theta_t)$, which gives $h_t=aL_t\mathcal E_t$. Survival through round $N$ is $\prod_{t\le N}e^{-h_t}$ by the chain rule, and it has a positive limit iff $\sum_th_t<\infty$.
1. The four factors appear in the formula. Sharing a fraction $\beta$ of the divisible prize gives the contest with prize $(1-\beta)\Omega_t$ (Proposition 8.6(2)), and $h_t$ is linear in the prize; $q_t=1-e^{-h_t}$ is not. A levy that is not rebated adds $\tau$ to the private marginal cost. A levy rebated equally enters each objective at $\tau(n-1-\theta_t)/n$ per unit (Proposition 3.5(5)).
2. $L/(c+(1+\theta)L)<1/(1+\theta)$ gives $h_t\le ar_t\Omega_t(n-1-\theta_t)/\big(n(1+\theta_t)\big)$. At $\theta_t=-1$ the formula gives $ar_t\Omega_tL_t/c_t$.
3. Competing hazards add in the exponent.
4. A constant positive $h$ has a divergent sum. $\square$

**Lemma 5.5.** By the chain rule, $\Pr(S_N)=\prod_{k\le N}\Pr(\text{no event at }k\mid S_{k-1})$. Each factor is at most $1-h_k$, because the bound holds on every surviving history. Then $1-x\le e^{-x}$. For the upper bound, each factor is at least $1-\bar h_k>0$, and $\prod(1-\bar h_k)>0$ iff $\sum\bar h_k<\infty$. $\square$

**Remark 5.6.** (1) The value of destruction, $-c_y-\theta\Delta_y/(n-1)$, is at most zero iff $\theta\ge-(n-1)c_y/\Delta_y$ (Proposition 3.5(6)). (2) Expansion continues while its value is positive; at $\theta=-1$ the host term vanishes. (3) At $\theta=-1$ destruction is worth $\Delta_y/(n-1)-c_y$, and it exceeds expansion once $\varphi(F)-c_x$ falls below that. The toy is computed in `checks_v6.log` (FS2), which reproduces the planning script `g3_frontier_switch.py`. $\square$

**Proposition 5.7.** Let deviation by $i$ yield $O_i(a^\ast)+g_i$ for one period. With probability $p_d$ it yields a decisive position worth at most $O_i(a^\ast)/(1-\delta)$ thereafter; with probability $1-p_d$ it is followed by punishment worth $v_i$ per period, where $v_i$ is the value of the harshest punishment that is itself sustainable (Abreu 1988). Cooperation is worth $O_i(a^\ast)/(1-\delta)$. Taking the decisive position at its upper value, the most favourable case for cooperation, the condition for no profitable deviation is
$$\frac{O_i(a^\ast)}{1-\delta}\ge O_i(a^\ast)+g_i+\delta p_d\frac{O_i(a^\ast)}{1-\delta}+\delta(1-p_d)\frac{v_i}{1-\delta},$$
which rearranges to (1). By the one-shot deviation principle this is necessary and sufficient. (2) As $p_d\to1$ the right side of (1) tends to zero, so $g_i\le0$ for every $i$: $a^\ast$ is a stage-game equilibrium in the objectives held. (3) A higher value of the decisive position adds a positive term to the right side of the displayed inequality. The check over 21,096 games is in `checks_v6.log` (DF). $\square$

**Corollary 5.8.** At $\theta=-1$, $O_i=\pi_i-\bar\pi_{-i}$. In a symmetric stage game every symmetric profile gives all actors equal payoffs, so every objective is $0$, and in particular $O_i(a^\ast)=0$ for a symmetric cooperative profile. Against a punishment in which every punisher plays the same action $b$, the punished actor can play $b$ as well; the profile is then symmetric and its objective is $0$. Hence $v_i\ge0=O_i(a^\ast)$, the right side of Proposition 5.7(1) is at most zero, and $g_i\le0$ is required. The check over 1,260 symmetric cases is in `checks_v6.log` (DF). $\square$

**Remark 5.9.** By Proposition 6.1(4), racing gains $A-B\theta$ in the objective, whatever the others do, so restraint is a stage equilibrium iff $\theta\ge\theta^\ast=A/B$. All restraint gives every objective $0$; all racing gives $(1+\theta)(-c-n\epsilon d)=-(1+\theta)K$, which is the reversion value when racing is the stage equilibrium. Proposition 5.7(1) becomes $A-B\theta\le D(1+\theta)K$, that is, $\theta\ge(A-DK)/(B+DK)$. The values are in `checks_v6.log` (DF). $\square$

**Proposition 5.10.** (1) The winner's objective is $(1-s)W+\theta sW/(n-1)$, linear in $s$ with slope $W(\theta/(n-1)-1)$, negative unless $\theta=n-1$, where it is zero. (2) With logarithmic utility the objective is $\ln((1-s)W)+\theta\ln(sW/(n-1))$; the first-order condition $-1/(1-s)+\theta/s=0$ gives $s=\theta/(1+\theta)$. Equal division, $s=(n-1)/n$, needs $\theta=n-1$. (3) is Proposition 3.5(5). $\square$

**Proposition 5.11.**
- (1) is Hayes (1950).
- (2) By simulation of $\dot y=ay-k_py(t-\tau_c)-k_d\dot y(t-\tau_c)$ (`../v4/checks_v42.log`).
- (3) With $G(s)=e^{-s\tau_c}/(s-a)$ and any stabilizing linear time-invariant controller, the complementary sensitivity $\mathcal T$ is stable and satisfies $\mathcal T(a)=1$. Write $\mathcal T(s)=e^{-s\tau_c}\tilde{\mathcal T}(s)$ with $\tilde{\mathcal T}$ stable; by the maximum modulus principle $\|\mathcal T\|_\infty\ge|\tilde{\mathcal T}(a)|=e^{a\tau_c}$. Robust stability needs $\|w\mathcal T\|_\infty<1$. Since $w\tilde{\mathcal T}$ is stable, its supremum on the imaginary axis is at least its modulus at $a$, giving the necessary condition $|w(a)|e^{a\tau_c}<1$. For $w(s)=2(s-a)/(s+a)$, $|w(j\omega)|=2$ for all $\omega$, so $\|w\mathcal T\|_\infty=2\|\mathcal T\|_\infty\ge2e^{a\tau_c}$.
- (4) Evidence cannot be acted on before it exists.

**Theorem 5.12.** For $t\ge T_\varepsilon$, on every surviving history, the conditional probability of the event is at least $h(1-\varepsilon)$. By the chain rule (Lemma 5.5), $\Pr(S_H)\le\Pr(S_{T_\varepsilon})(1-h(1-\varepsilon))^{H-T_\varepsilon}$. The converse is the upper-bound part of Lemma 5.5 with $\bar h_k=\bar h\mathsf u_k$.

**Proposition 5.13.**
- (1) After $\tau$, $\mathsf u_t=(1-U(t)/Y_\tau)^+$. For linear $U$, the positive part lasts $(Y_\tau-U(\tau))/\dot U$, and the integral of a linear function falling from $1-U(\tau)/Y_\tau$ to $0$ over that interval is $Y_\tau(1-U(\tau)/Y_\tau)^2/(2\dot U)$. For exponential $U$, $\int_0^{\ln\Gamma/g_U}(1-e^{g_Uv}/\Gamma)\,dv=(\ln\Gamma-1+1/\Gamma)/g_U$.
- (2) After rollback, $\mathsf u_t=0$.
- (3) Frozen hazardous variety is $\varphi\Gamma_X$ times the rivals' level, and coverage is at least the rivals' level, so $\mathsf u=0$ when $\varphi\Gamma_X\le1$.
- (4) For linear $U$, $\dot U/U=\dot U/(U_0+\dot Ut)\to0$. For the counterexample, let $\log U$ increase by $0.5$ on each interval $[n^2,n^2+1]$ and stay constant otherwise. Then $\log U(t)\approx0.5\sqrt t=o(t)$, while $\dot U/U=0.5$ inside each interval (`checks_v5.log`, MM).

**Proposition 5.14.**
1. Let the sender's and receiver's payoffs sum to zero for every state and action, and fix an equilibrium with receiver strategy $y(m)$. Each sender type $t$ sends a message maximizing its payoff, so it induces, among the actions induced in equilibrium, one that minimizes the receiver's payoff in state $t$. The receiver's equilibrium payoff is therefore at most $\operatorname{E}_t[u_R(t,y(m'))]$ for any fixed message $m'$, which is at most $v_0$, the value of the best response to the prior. The receiver can always ignore messages, so its payoff is at least $v_0$. Hence it equals $v_0$, and by the zero sum the sender's payoff equals $-v_0$: every equilibrium is payoff-equivalent to one without talk. Proposition 3.5(2) gives the zero sum at $\theta=-1$ between two parties.
2. Crawford and Sobel (1982), uniform-quadratic case. With $b=0$ the ideal points coincide and truthful revelation is an equilibrium.
3. A sender that intends to race prefers the receiver to cooperate: $T-P=\varepsilon>0$ in the example. $\square$

**Theorem 5.15.** Each item of Branch I is the cited proposition under its condition. For Branch II, solve the multi-period bargaining backward: A's value is $V_t=\max(W_t,\delta V_{t+1})$ with $W_t=(w_t-c_A)/(1-\delta)$, and war occurs at $t$ iff $W_t>1+\delta V_{t+1}$ (`../v4/checks_v44.log`, B2). Branch III follows from intensity proportional to $r$ (Proposition 3.9(1)).

**Theorem 5.16.** (1) With $p_d$ small, Proposition 5.7(1) leaves the folk-theorem region of Remark 3.7 available; Theorem 5.15 gives the absence of a lasting object, and Proposition 5.2 the dissipation. (2) The three items are Propositions 5.7(2), 5.10 and 7.2(2). If a deviation can yield a decisive position, $p_d$ is bounded away from zero for every deviator who can hold it; only custody under which no party can hold it alone keeps $p_d$ near zero, and after it only stage equilibria in held objectives remain (Proposition 5.7(2)). The composite is [T∣A] because it joins results proved under their own conditions. $\square$

### A.6 Section 6

**Proposition 6.1** is proved in the text.

**Proposition 6.2.**
- (1) is direct computation from the contest success function.
- (2) The limiting gains follow from (1); the gain increases in $s$, so the inequalities are necessary and sufficient for dominance in the limit.
- (3) With all delegating, $w_i=1/n$ as with none, and the hazard is higher.
- (4) The term $L_c[\cdot]$ is common to all objectives and cancels from a relative objective.

**Lemma 6.3.** Solve the two linear equations.

**Proposition 6.4.**
- (1) With $q_L=1$, $\Phi_D=0$, while $\Phi_N=Q_N[w_N+(1-w_N)(1-p)]\ge Q_Nw_N>0$ for finite $s$, since $w_N>0$.
- (2) With $p=1$, $\Phi_N=Q_N/(n+ks)$, because the actor has strength $1$, $k$ others $1+s$, and $n-1-k$ others $1$. Similarly $\Phi_D=(1-q_L)aQ_N(1+s)/(n+(k+1)s)$, and the ratio follows. All delegating is an equilibrium iff the ratio at $k=n-1$ is at least one; no one delegating iff the ratio at $k=0$ is at most one. Substituting gives the two conditions, and the numbers are in `checks_v5.log` (DL).
- (3) $Q_N/(n+ks)>0$ for finite $s$.

**Lemma 6.5.** Under (a)–(d), variants that act on common terms exist and, by (b), have relative performance lower by at least $\nu$. By (c), retention is cumulative, so their share tends to zero (as in Proposition 3.4(3)). By (d), the retained systems' decisions are the actor's decisions. If constraints are an enforced condition of admission for every eligible variant, no admissible variant violates them, so (a) fails.

**Proposition 6.7.** (1) Level 2 is relative and payoff-monotone, so Lemma 3.3(1) removes every term common to all developers; weight placed on such terms at level 1 lowers a developer's relative payoff unless it brings other advantages, and Proposition 3.4(1) applies to the developers as carriers. (2) Level 0 maximizes the reward it is given, by definition of optimization. Level 1 chooses rewards and constraints to maximize the developer's objective, which under level 2 includes relative standing; it therefore counters a divergence of level 0 only where the divergence lowers that objective. (3) A constraint borne by one developer lowers its relative payoff (Lemma 3.3(2)); a constraint enforced on all as a condition of admission is a common term and is invisible to level 2. Composition of (1)–(3) gives the closing statement. $\square$

**Proposition 6.8.** The residual hazard is the sum of failures not blocked and failures the corrector adds; compare with $q_0$. The example is in `checks_v5.log` (OV).

**Proposition 6.9.**
- (1) Each layer covers $\mathsf m$ times the attention of the layer above, so $k$ layers cover $\mathsf B\mathsf m^k$. A problem is caught only if every link succeeds, and the conditional reliabilities multiply to $\mathsf f^k$. Then $\mathsf f^k=\exp(k\ln\mathsf f)=(Y/\mathsf B)^{\ln\mathsf f/\ln\mathsf m}$.
- (2) Independent checks fail together with probability $(1-\mathsf f^k)^k$. A common cause that defeats all of them with probability $\rho$ leaves at most $1-\rho$.
- (3) Each layer fails with probability $(1-\mathsf f)^m$, and the layers must all succeed.

**Remark 6.10.** While small, errors grow as $\delta_{\text{obs}}e^{\lambda_Lt}$; set this equal to $\Delta_{\text{tol}}$.

**Proposition 6.11.**
- With $r=1$ and the others at a common $\iota$, $\partial w_i/\partial\iota_i=s(n-1)/(n^2(1+s\iota))$ at the symmetric point. The marginal cost of $\iota_i$ is $L_c\Delta\psi(1-\psi)^{n-1}$. Equating gives $(1+s\iota)\Delta\psi(q-x)^{n-1}=L_es(n-1)/(n^2L_c)$, and dividing by $s$ gives $(x+b)(q-x)^{n-1}=K$.
- (1) For $x\ll q$, $(q-x)^{n-1}\approx q^{n-1}$, so $x\approx K/q^{n-1}-b$, and multiplying by $n$ gives the approximation.
- (2) Differentiating the implicit equation in $\Delta\psi$ (with $b=\Delta\psi/s$) gives the stated derivative. The two interior roots and the corner are found numerically and checked as global best responses (`checks_v5.log`, HO).
- (3) At full delegation, the own marginal gain is $2s_t/(9(1+s_t))-\Delta\psi_t(1-\Delta\psi_t)^2>0$ for $t\ge5$. Each actor's objective is concave in its own $\iota$, so full delegation is an equilibrium. The realized risk is $1-(1-2^{-t})^3\le3\cdot2^{-t}$, which sums to at most $3/16$ from $t=5$.
- (4) For $k\ge1$, $(1-\psi_0-\Delta\psi)^k<(1-\psi_0)^k$.

**Lemma 7.11.** Fix a dissolution date $\tau$ (possibly infinite). By the chain rule, survival to $H$ under $A$ is the product over periods of conditional survival on surviving histories. Before $\tau$, each factor is at least the corresponding factor under $C$ by (F2); from $\tau$ on, by (F1). So $S^A(H\mid\tau)\ge S^C(H)$ for every $\tau$, and averaging over the distribution of $\tau$ gives the claim. With hazards that depend on history, the same argument holds factor by factor, because (F1) and (F2) are pointwise on surviving histories. The example is in `checks_v6.log` (FD). $\square$

### A.8 Section 8

**Theorem 8.2.**
- (1) (I2) with margin in rule-making gives a full row sum below one, so $x_{\mathsf S}\to0$ (Proposition 4.8(2)). Once (I1) is enacted, $\tau_{\text{eff}}>\nu$ after a finite time. Load then has negative private payoff, so $E\to e_{\min}$ and $\liminf V\ge K(1-he_{\min}/\rho)>V_{\min}$. For unanticipated harms, (I2) in evaluation gives $x_{\mathsf E}\to0$, so $m\to\beta_{\text{out}}<m_{\text{fl}}$, or the conservative rule applies, and the alarm fires above the floor.
- (2) Without (I1), Proposition 3.4 applies. Without (I2) in rule-making, $x_{\mathsf S}\to1$ (Proposition 4.9(1)) and $\tau_{\text{eff}}\to0$. Without (I3), silent crossing follows (Proposition 4.6(2)). With rule-making captured first, $x_{\mathsf S}$ keeps rising long enough for the floor to be crossed when $\varepsilon_{\mathsf S}$ is small.
- (3) If the limsup is below $\nu$, there are $\epsilon>0$ and $T_0$ with $T^{-1}\int_0^T\tau_{\text{eff}}\le\nu-\epsilon$ for $T>T_0$. Then the carrier of load $e_{\max}$ grows relative to any carrier of lower load, $E\to e_{\max}$, and the argument of Proposition 3.4(3) applies.

**Proposition 8.3.** (1) is Propositions 3.4 and 4.3(1)–(2). (2) With rule-makers as players, unilateral imposition is restraint in Proposition 3.6. (3) Under $\tau_{\text{eff}}>\nu$ the private payoff of load is negative.

**Proposition 8.4.** Compare the first-order conditions; concavity of $\Omega$ orders the solutions.

**Proposition 8.5.** For $k$ racers and $m=n-k$ cooperators:
- Under separate frontiers, a racer's payoff is $w_i\Omega-(1-w_i)L_b-ce_i-\ell\mathcal E$, with $w_i=e_i^r/(\sum_{\text{racers}}e^r+me_c^r)$.
- Under a common frontier, the pool's strength is $(me_c)^r$. A cooperator gets $w_{\text{pool}}\Omega_c/m-(1-w_{\text{pool}})L_b-ce_c-\ell\mathcal E$.
- Racers' intensities are the symmetric fixed point of best responses among them, each verified as a global best response. Where $r>k/(k-1)$ for $k$ racers there may be no symmetric pure equilibrium, and such cases are excluded.
- The per-rival threshold solves $\sum_k\binom{n-1}{k}q^k(1-q)^{n-1-k}[\text{Coop}(k)-\text{Race}(k+1)]=0$, with trust $1-q$.
- The aggregate reduction uses $R=\text{Coop}(0)$, $T=\text{Race}(1)$, $S'=\text{Coop}(n-1)$ and $P=\text{Race}(n)$.
- For the coalition of three with summed payoff: against a single cooperator, the coalition's members get $0.14$ cooperating against $-1.197$ racing; against a single racer, $-0.885$ against $-1.325$. Cooperation is better in both, so it is dominant.
- The lid is the smallest $e_c$ with $T\le R$, found by search from below, since $T-R$ is not monotone in $e_c$ (`../v4/checks_v45.log`, N22).

**Proposition 8.6.**

*Part 1.* Pooled payoffs are $\pi'_j=(1-\beta)\pi_j+\beta\bar\pi$, with $\bar\pi=(\pi_i+(n-1)\bar\pi_{-i})/n$, so $\bar\pi'_{-i}=(1-\beta)\bar\pi_{-i}+\beta\bar\pi$. Then
$$O_i=\pi'_i+\theta_0\bar\pi'_{-i}=\Big[(1-\beta)+\tfrac{\beta(1+\theta_0)}{n}\Big]\pi_i+\Big[\theta_0(1-\beta)+\tfrac{\beta(1+\theta_0)(n-1)}{n}\Big]\bar\pi_{-i}.$$
For $\theta_0>-1$ the first coefficient is positive, and dividing by it gives $\theta_{\text{eff}}$. With $A=(1+\theta_0)/n>0$, the derivative of $\theta_{\text{eff}}$ in $\beta$ has the sign of $A(n-1-\theta_0)$, positive for $\theta_0<n-1$; at $\beta=1$ the ratio is $n-1$. At $\theta_0=-1$, $A=0$ and $O_i=(1-\beta)(\pi_i-\bar\pi_{-i})$, a positive multiple, so best responses are unchanged. Under replicator dynamics on pooled payoffs, $\pi'_k-\pi'_l=(1-\beta)(\pi_k-\pi_l)$.

*Part 2.* With the promise kept with probability $\varrho$, each payoff is $w_i\big[(1-\beta\varrho)\Omega_e+\Omega_s\big]+\varrho\beta\Omega_e/n-ce_i-\ell\mathcal E$. The term $\varrho\beta\Omega_e/n$ is common to all payoffs: it shifts every objective by a constant and cancels from relative selection (Lemma 3.3). What remains is the contest with prize $\Omega_{\text{eff}}$. Intensity is linear in the prize (Proposition 3.9(1)); dissipation is $ne(c+n\ell)$, and $h_t$ is proportional to $\mathcal E$ (Proposition 5.4). For relative selection in one field, a mutant at $y$ among residents at $x$ has relative payoff
$$\Delta(y,x)=\Omega_{\text{eff}}\frac{y^r-x^r}{y^r+(n-1)x^r}-c(y-x),$$
the shared loss cancelling. Its derivative in $y$ at $y=x$ is $\Omega_{\text{eff}}r/(nx)-c$, which vanishes at $x^\ast$. For $r=1$, $\Delta(\cdot,x^\ast)$ is concave with $\Delta(x^\ast,x^\ast)=0$ and zero slope there, so no mutant gains; for $r<1$ the grid check in `checks_v6.log` (SH) finds no profitable mutant. The common-field example is in the same block.

*Part 3.* Remark 5.3 gives the multiplier $1+\delta(\chi r-1)$, at most one iff $\chi r\le1$. At $\chi=0$ in Section 4, the return to influence is zero (Proposition 4.2(3)) and $d^\ast=A/c_{\mathsf E}$ (Proposition 4.4(2)). $\square$

**Proposition 8.8.** Apply Proposition 4.8(2) to the verifier's full row. The counter-case is one domain with captured sources whose conversions sum to $1.05$. The fourth limit is Proposition 5.14 with the verifier as sender.

**Proposition 8.9.**
- (1) is the participation constraint under transferable utility; strict gain needs strict inequality.
- (2) Under Bayesian incentive compatibility with types independent, each type's interim expected utility from reporting must not depend on its report beyond what truth gives. With linear, type-independent share utility, this makes the interim expected share independent of the report. Budget balance makes these interim shares constants summing to one, and participation must hold for the type with the best outside option. The example's interim shares are computed in `checks_v5.log` (K7).
- (3)–(5) follow by substitution.

**Proposition 8.11.** (1) After $t_N$, $Y_t=Y_N$ and $U_t\ge U_{t_N}+u(t-t_N)$, so the uncovered share is zero from $t_N+Y_N/u$ on. Survival on this channel is then at least a finite product of factors $1-\bar h\mathsf u_t>0$, by the upper-bound part of Lemma 5.5. (2) is the upper-bound part of Lemma 5.5 with $\bar h_k=\bar h\mathsf u_k$. (3) The success function $w_i=e_i^r/\sum_je_j^r$ is unchanged when every effort is multiplied by the same factor, so the symmetric intensity is proportional to the stakes (Proposition 3.9(1)); no level of stakes makes further intensity worthless. Under (X), (U) and (φ), Theorem 5.12 applies. The example is in `checks_v6.log` (PE). $\square$

## Appendix B. Numerics

`checks_v6.py` (pure Python, log `checks_v6.log`, about two seconds) computes every case that v6 adds or restates as [N]:
- FB, the local feedback benchmark (Remark 2.3);
- UC, universal and unilateral constraints under relative selection (Lemma 3.3(2));
- TC, the two-channel accounting identity and its consequences (Proposition 3.5);
- TH, identifying $\theta$ from two perturbations (Section 3.4);
- CB, clubs and blocs (Proposition 3.11);
- RI, the reinvestment threshold $\chi r=1$ and the point of enough in compounding (Remark 5.3);
- RH, the race-generated hazard against a direct best-response computation (Proposition 5.4);
- FS2, the frontier switch (Remark 5.6);
- DF, decisiveness and enforcement in random games, the corollary for relative objectives, and the binary illustration (Proposition 5.7, Corollary 5.8, Remark 5.9);
- WG, what a decisive winner gives (Proposition 5.10);
- TK, talk in zero-sum sender–receiver games (Proposition 5.14(1));
- SH, pooling, sharing in one common field, and convertibility (Proposition 8.6);
- PE, the point of enough (Proposition 8.11);
- FD, fallback dominance (Lemma 7.11).

Results carried unchanged from v5.2 are checked by `../v5/checks_v5.py` (log `../v5/checks_v5.log`), whose blocks keep their v5 codes: AG, CR, HO, DL, MM, OV, SR, FS, SG, K7, N13, CAP and CMP. The earlier suites remain in `../v4/` and `../v3/`:
- `checks_v31.py` and `figures_v3.py`: Proposition 3.6, the repeated-game cutoff, the rate law of Proposition 4.4, and the illustration of Theorem 4.11;
- `checks_v4.py`: Propositions 3.9, 5.2, 6.2, 7.4, 9.1 and 5.14(2), the Hayes window, and the conservative alarm;
- `checks_v42.py`: counterexamples to v4.1, derivative action, and Proposition 8.8;
- `checks_v44.py`: the delegation conditions, Branch II of Theorem 5.15, and the survival examples of Proposition 7.9;
- `checks_v45.py`: Nash-verified Proposition 8.5, the catch-up computations, Proposition 8.9, the lead under diffusion, and discounting.

None of these suites certifies every proof, and none supplies evidence about the world.

---

## Appendix C. Conditions, indicators and measures

The evidence register gives the dated observations. This appendix gives the conditions of the conditional results and how each would be measured. The entries say where to measure; they are not estimates.

**The three objects of Section 2.3, measured separately.**

| Object | What is measured | Sources |
|---|---|---|
| What actors value | The weight $\theta$ placed on others and on the shared host; the weight on winning $\lambda$ | Charters, incentive contracts, doctrines; the two-perturbation identification of Section 3.4 |
| What actors believe | Beliefs about rivals' objectives and capabilities; the perceived shared hazard; the belief that one is in a struggle for survival (crux C8) | Elite assessments of rivals' intentions; doctrinal texts; surveys |
| What institutions reward, permit and protect | Convertibility $\chi$; decisiveness $r$; conversion $b_{ij}$ into evaluators and rules; row sums of correctors | Funding, appointment and access terms of evaluators; authorship of standards; contest structure of procurement and markets |

**The slow route (Theorem 4.11).**

| Condition | Indicator | What would refute it |
|---|---|---|
| H0 floor within reach | Per harm class: capability with safeguards removed | For every class, independently measured capability with safeguards removed stays below the threshold |
| H1 cumulative relative selection | Capital, compute and talent following rank; effective liability against $\nu$ over time | Restraint gains share at a given rank, or the cumulative advantage converges. An average $\tau_{\text{eff}}$ equal to $\nu$ is not enough: $\tau_{\text{eff}}=\nu-1/(1+t)$ has average tending to $\nu$ while the cumulative advantage $\ln(1+t)$ diverges |
| H2 appropriable control | Developer roles in evaluation and standards; share keyed to one provider | Evaluation and standards funded, staffed and accessed independently of the evaluated |
| H3 two rates | AI's cheapening of control ($g$) against that of independent evaluation plus budget growth ($b+g_c$) | $b+g_c\ge g/(1+\eta)$ |
| H4 masking | Evaluator bias against ground truth at relevant harm levels | Measured $m(t)\le m_{\text{fl}}$ in the band, or a credible low-bias report that is acted on |
| H5 order of events | Release-to-evaluation time against harm-to-detection time | Independent detection consistently precedes developer-shaped evaluation |
| Reach | Compute and deployment within one jurisdiction | One jurisdiction's rules bind all load that can cross the floor |

**The contest (Theorem 5.15) and enforcement (Proposition 5.7).**

| Condition | Meaning | What would change the conclusion |
|---|---|---|
| P1 | Actors act on a large perceived $r$ | If no actor believed in decisive advantage, the race would lose its object (Branch III) |
| P2 | Leads open within short windows | A sustained slowdown moves the trajectory to Branch II |
| P3 | Advantage does not saturate | Saturation moves it to Branch I′ |
| $p_d$ | The probability that a deviation becomes decisive | Leads converting into lasting share raise it; diffusion and the open–closed gap lower it (test T-H) |
| Detection against decisiveness (C7) | Detection latency against the time from breakout to decisive capability | Detection reliably faster keeps enforcement available (Theorem 7.7(2)) |
| Shared exposure | $\ell>0$, $\kappa>0$ | Unshared payoffs remove the shared-loss parts of Propositions 3.9 and 5.2 |
| Correction delays | Delays comparable to $1/a$ for some classes | Correction robust at the observed $a\tau_c$ relaxes Proposition 5.11 |
| Verification | $\upsilon<1$, or not timely against a decisive shift | Perfect timely detection moves the boundary of Theorem 7.7 to timeliness, reliability and approval |
| Conversion into influence | Success converts into influence over correctors; AI lowers the cost of control | $\chi=0$, or independent correction keeping pace (Proposition 4.4(4)) |
| Participation | Efficiency, information and distribution conditions of Proposition 8.9 | If the strongest actor's rents exceed its losses, racing can pay for it materially |
| Delegation | The exact condition of Proposition 6.2, relative objectives, or survival without the lock | If the control loss dominates, or the lock is priced in with $q_L=1$, delegation is not chosen (Propositions 6.2, 6.4) |
| (A8) | After a shift, the advantaged side sets the terms | Under other protocols prevention can cease to be rational (Proposition 7.4(3)) |

**The coverage premises (Section 5.3).**

| Premise | Indicator | What would refute it |
|---|---|---|
| (X) | Predefined hazard modes per class and their count per model generation; new threat models; automation of AI research; capture–recapture across independent red teams | Bounded variety of hazardous behaviour in the decisive class |
| (U) | $U$ measured by U1–U4: catch rates of planted defects at fixed human hours; rates of overrule; audit coverage | Catch rates that do not fall as capability rises |
| (φ) | Residual-risk estimates per class after correction, by independent evaluators | Residual risk falling fast enough to be summable |
| (φ-s) | Propagation pathways of failures | Failures confined to their source |
| $q_L$ | Whether delegated decisions can be overruled; drills of fallback capacity | Maintained $\mathcal K\ge\mathcal K_{\min}$ removes the lock (Lemma 6.3) |

**Measuring the arrangements (Section 9.3).** The relation $a\approx\ln2/T_2$, with $T_2$ the doubling time of a proxy for dangerous capability, is a connecting assumption.

| Arrangement | Indicator | Example of a threshold | Precedent or measurer | What would count against it |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| E1, decision loops | Share of consequential decisions requiring human authorization, with a minimum time for deliberation | Nuclear command and control: all | Doctrine, exercises and after-action review | Human authorization on paper while delegated systems decide: overrule near zero |
| E1, tempo | Share of automated agents in markets and cyber operations under circuit breakers | A minimum human delay per transaction or operation | Circuit breakers in financial regulation | — |
| E1, parallel capacity | Share of delegated functions that trained people can take over in a stated time; drill success | Critical functions drilled at least quarterly | Ironies of automation; manual flying requirements | Rising drill failure: lock-in under way |
| E2, thresholds | Thresholds for automated AI research; task length; autonomous replication | A red line at a stated task length | Independent evaluators with statutory access | Dangerous capability not appearing at the threshold |
| E2, inputs | Efficiency-corrected compute caps; unaccounted compute; largest undeclared cluster | Undeclared compute of the order of half a per cent of the global total | Supply-chain accounting; hardware mechanisms (EV17) | — |
| E2, rate | Tightening of input bounds against algorithmic efficiency | A ratio of at least one; about 2.5–3× a year at an eight-month efficiency doubling | Trend measurement | — |
| E2, custody | Whether any capability of the decisive class exists outside common custody | None | Joint inspection | — |
| E3, tempo | $a\approx\ln2/T_2$ | A seven-month doubling gives $a\approx0.10$ a month | Independent measurement | Dangerous capability decoupling from general capability |
| E3, lag | $L=\tau_{\text{obs}}+T_d+T_r$ from appearance to withdrawal | Relative model error below $e^{-aL}$. At $T_2=7$ months, $L$ of 6, 12 and 24 months tolerates 0.55, 0.30 and 0.09 | Regulators; incident-reporting deadlines | Correction robust at the observed $aL$ |
| E3 and (U) | $\dot U/U$ against $\dot Y/Y$ | Until the uncovered share is summable | Control evaluations; auditing games | Catch rates at fixed human hours not falling |

**Data for the pre-registered tests (Section 10.5).** Tests name roles in the text; their data sources are listed here.
- **T-A.** A corpus of traced changes to developers' safety frameworks (Zhu 2026: twelve developers, 710 commitment instances), with release dates, financing rounds and leadership changes.
- **T-B.** Lobbying disclosure registers in the jurisdictions where developers are based; drafting participation and signature records of codes of practice for general-purpose AI; authorship shares of benchmarks and standards.
- **T-C.** Events that separate signals of intent (dialogues, incident channels) from capability shocks; subsequent release cadence and doctrinal texts.
- **T-D.** Natural experiments in which rules were withdrawn, evaluation bodies renamed or refocused, or obligations delayed; measured by the retention of pre-deployment testing, model documentation and incident reporting.
- **T-E.** A dynamic factor model of indicators of T1–T6, to be built.
- **T-F.** Organizations with capped or non-convertible returns; public consortia.
- **T-G.** Multi-agent experiments on governance of shared resources and on escalation, extended with endogenous evaluators (Studies A and B).
- **T-H.** Annual reports on the gap between open and closed models, and on the persistence of leads.
- **T-I.** Access terms, funding sources and staff flows of the leading jurisdiction's evaluators.
- **T-J.** Dates of the first race framings in institutional documents, against compute allocation and safety staffing.
- **T-K.** Cross-industry comparison of fields with few and with many actors.

**Undoing capture.** As a reform brings inbound conversion toward one, recovery at the captured state slows, so rising autocorrelation in the shares indicates progress in undoing capture (Section 4.5). The parameters of the field model can in principle be recovered from data by integrating (3) (Section 4.7).

## Appendix E. Interfaces between the models

The theory joins several models. Each join is an assumption, and each is listed so that it can be checked separately.

| Interface | Between | Assumption | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Shared loss and irreversible hazard | The contest (Section 3.6) and survival (Section 5.1) | $(\mathrm{A_G})$: hazard load proportional to shared damage | Modelling premise; crux C1 |
| Coverage and residual risk | Hazardous variety and coverage (Section 5.3) and survival | (φ): residual risk at least proportional to the uncovered share | AI-risk premise; crux C1 |
| Correction rate and coverage | $d$ in the field model (Section 4) and $U$ (Section 5.3) | A production relation $d\le F(U,\text{resources},\text{delay},\text{authority})$ | Open; neither quantity bounds the other |
| Shared loss and coupling | $\ell$ (or $L$) and $\kappa$ | Distinct quantities, not to be counted twice | Maintained (Section 3.6) |
| Selection and strategy | Carrier selection (Sections 3.3, 4) and strategic actors (Sections 5–9) | A selection result transfers only where the selected objective is shown to be the actors' | Rule 1 of Section 3.1 |
| The outer training loop | Developers' relative payoff and trained systems' contribution to it | Level 2 selects on relative payoff (Proposition 6.7) | Where developers are selected otherwise, the result does not apply |
| Masking and alarms | Reports (Section 4.4) and decisions | Averaged, non-strategic reports (A5) | Remark 4.7 treats other rules |
| Decisive position | Enforcement (Proposition 5.7) and contests (Theorem 5.15) | The decisive position valued at most the cooperative payoff | Conservative; the race's own valuation strengthens the result |
| Slow variables of mind | The selected self-model and the field model | A slow variable for the share of decision positions held by the winning model | Not modelled; research programme (Section 10.6) |
| The host | One host (Section 3.2) and many shared conditions | A single logistic host as the forgiving case | Allee-type and vector hosts are research (Section 3.2) |

**New in v6.** Definition 2.1; Remark 2.4; Lemma 3.3(2); Proposition 3.5; the identification of $\theta$ (Section 3.4); Lemma 3.10; Proposition 3.11; Remark 5.3; Remark 5.6; Proposition 5.7; Corollary 5.8; Remark 5.9; Proposition 5.10; Proposition 5.14(1) and (3); Theorem 5.16; Proposition 6.7; Proposition 7.10; Lemma 7.11; Definition 7.12 as a profile; Proposition 8.6(2)–(3); Definition 8.10; Proposition 8.11; Remark 8.12.

**Withdrawn, and why.**
- "The trajectory has one root", typed as a definition: replaced by one pressure with overlapping mechanisms, stated so that it can fail (Section 2.7).
- "Growth needs a widening self" as a theorem: replaced by the decomposition of Proposition 5.4 and the one-group lemma; oneness is a requirement under stated conditions (Theorem 5.16) and a long-term requirement of integration (Lemma 3.10).
- "Sharing reverses selection": in one common field it only shrinks it (Proposition 8.6(2)).
- "Every design inside Ψ is re-absorbed": replaced by the exact condition of Proposition 4.9 and the registered prediction of Definition 7.12.
- "θ = n−1 is oneness": oneness is a property of the system (Section 3.8); $\theta=n-1$ among contestants is a club (Proposition 3.11).
- The three zones as the main theorem: the binary game is an illustration (Remark 5.9); the general result is Proposition 5.7.
- The hysteresis figures of a bistable model of the winning model, and the slope condition presented as bistability: deferred to research (Section 10.6).
- "What is shared is the prize of decisive standing": the positional part cannot be divided (Section 8.4).
- A genealogy that placed the turn first in one group of economies: the strands are plural and appear across civilizations (Section 2.8).

## Appendix G. Counterexamples that bound the claims

Each entry is a case that refutes a stronger version of a claim. The text states the weaker claim that survives.

| Counterexample | What it refutes | Where it is handled |
|---|---|---|
| In a four-actor contest in which three actors widen their objective to $\theta=2.9$ and one keeps $\theta=-1$, total intensity falls from $1.0$ to $0.015$, but the holdout wins with probability $0.989$ | That widening the self removes the problem: without shared gains and limits on what winning confers, it turns a problem of risk into one of domination; inclusion is not self-enforcing against a single holdout | Coverage, not identity, is required (Theorem 5.16; Proposition 8.3); limits on what winning confers (Proposition 8.6) |
| In one common Tullock field, the uninvadable intensity $r\Omega_{\text{eff}}/(nc)$ is positive for every sharing fraction below one | That sharing the prize reverses selection | Proposition 8.6(2) |
| In the continuous contest, intensity is positive for every $\theta<n-1$ | That self-enforcing restraint exists below full inclusion in every game | Remark 5.9 scope notes |
| A positive loop with slope above four times the heterogeneity permits three fixed points only for a range of intercepts | That the slope condition alone gives bistability | Deferred to research (Section 10.6) |
| An industry institution after a serious nuclear accident; the Montreal Protocol | That restraint with unchanged motives is always fragile | Section 9.6: durable where consequences return and no prize is decisive (Theorem 5.16(1)) |
| Open models trailing closed ones by months; algorithmic efficiency diffusing | That decisive advantage is attainable now | Crux C5; oneness is at present a gain |
| Verification faster than the time from breakout to decisive capability | That enforcement fails whenever advantage is decisive | Crux C7 |
| Parochial altruism; blocs inclusive within and relative without | That inclusion within a group is integration of the system | Proposition 3.11; Lemma 3.10 |
| A well-corrected continuation outperforming a fragile cooperative arrangement in the illustration of Section 7.11 | That every cooperative arrangement beats regulated competition | Section 7.11; registered conditions under which continuation wins |
| A discounted criterion preferring a race that ends in catastrophe with certainty | That every criterion ranks the paths alike | Section 7.9; normative crux N1 |
| Mixed beliefs at a horizon of 1,000 periods making racing dominant | That the stag-hunt classification holds at every horizon | Proposition 7.9(5) |
| A corrector that cannot be understood lowering residual risk to a summable sequence | That correction beyond human understanding must fail | Proposition 6.8; normative crux N3 |
| Coverage growing in bursts with $\log U=o(t)$ | That sub-exponential coverage always makes a later sprint costlier | Proposition 5.13(4) |
| The host's minimum higher under relative accounting with war than under the isolated self in the frontier toy | That relative accounting is always worse for the host at every moment | Remark 5.6, reported as found |
| The author's pilot experiment, in which the first prediction failed | That the mechanism of Section 4 has been shown in experiment | Section 10.6; EV12 |
